3.23 Demo: Securely Storing Secrets with AWS Secrets Manager

  An inside Look at AWS Secrets Manager vs AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store
    https://acloudguru.com/blog/engineering/an-inside-look-at-aws-secrets-manager-vs-parameter-store

   Secrets Manager Introduction
     - Protect and store secrets for AWS Services, IT Resources, and Applications
     Centrally Manage
       - secrets used to access resources inside and outside AWS
     Rotate
       - secrets without code deployment
     Secure
       - Secrets with control of fine-grain permissions and encryption with AWS KMS

   AWS Secrets Manager
     Secret Types:
       RDS Database, Redshift Cluster, DocumentDB Database, Other Databases, API Keys

   Storing Database Secrets
       RDS Database secrets for MySQL, PostgreSQL, Oracle, MariaDB, SQL Server
         Information stored in Secrets
           - username and password
           - server address
           - database name and port

   Securing Secrets with Secrets Manager
     Secrets are Encrypted using and AWS KMS Key
       - customer Master Key (CMK)
         - logical representation of a master key, which holds key material to encrypt data
         - CMKs can only encrypt up to 4KB of data
     Configure Automatic Rotation of Secrets
       - every 30, 60, 90, or custom # of days (maximum of 365 days)
       - Create new or use existing Lambda for secret rotation

  Demo: Creating a Secret

    1. Create an RDS PostgreSQL Database

      - using AWS console
      -> console -> RDS -> Create Database ->  "Easy create", Engine Options: PostSQL,
           templates: Free Tier;
         Settings: master username: postgre, Master Password: rdsDemoPw;
         -> Create Database

    2. Create a Secret - store the database password
      -> console -> Secrets Manager -> Store a New Secret ->
         Secret Type: Credential for Amazon RDS Database;
         Credentials: User Name: postgres, Password: rdsDemoPw;
         Database: database-1; -> Next ->
         Configure Secret: Secret Name: RDS_Demo_PostgresSQL;
         Replicate: AWS Region: us-east-2; -> Next ->
         Configure Rotatation: Enable Rotation, Rotation Schedule: "Schedule expression builder"
           30 days; Rotation Function: "create a rotation funtion", SecretsManager: postgressql-rotation-function,
           Use Seperate credentials to rotate this secret: No; -> Next ->
           Review -> Store


       Notes:
          - by default, it encrypts using KMS key managed by AWS. You can specify your own key
          - can use "Resource Permissions" to "Add or edit a resource policy to access secrets
            across AWS accounts"
              - can  use to give access to a specific lambda function, ec2 instance, etc
             - use "Replicate Secret"  to "Create read-only replicas of your secret in other Regions"

          - in Review, under "Sample Code", it provides template lambda function if Java, JavaScipt, C#,
            Python3, Ruby, and Go languages

    3. View Secret Information in the console

           -> View Details


   Secrets Manager vs Parameter Store
      Secrets Manager
        - great for storing Database Credentials and API Keys
        - includes rotation of keys option
        - generate random secrets (e.g. passwords)
        - share secrets across accounts
      Systems Manager Parameter Store
        - Wider set of use Cases
        - Configuration Variables and License Keys
          - Because Systems Manager is used to perform system updates on your EC2 instances, and other
            AWS resources, Parameter Store is designed to store "configuration variables" and "license keys"


   Exam Tips

     Secrets Manager
       - Protect and store secrets for AWS Services, IT Resources, and Applications
       Centrally Manage
         - secrets used to access resources inside and outside AWS
       Rotate
         - secrets without code deployment
       Secure
         - Secrets with control of fine-grain permissions and encryption with AWS KMS

     AWS Secrets Manager
       Secret Types:
         RDS Database, Redshift Cluster, DocumentDB Database, Other Databases, API Keys

     Storing Database Secrets
         RDS Database secrets for MySQL, PostgreSQL, Oracle, MariaDB, SQL Server
           Information stored in Secrets
             - username and password
             - server address
             - database name and port

     Secrets Manager vs Parameter Store
        Secrets Manager
          - great for storing Database Credentials and API Keys
          - includes rotation of keys option
          - generate random secrets (e.g. passwords)
          - share secrets across accounts
        Systems Manager Parameter Store
          - Wider set of use Cases
          - Configuration Variables and License Keys
            - Because Systems Manager is used to perform system updates on your EC2 instances, and other
              AWS resources, Parameter Store is designed to store "configuration variables" and "license keys"

3.24 Discovering EC2 Image Builder

  EC2 Image Builder
    1. Create EC2 Images
      - Virtual Machine Images (AMIs) and container images
    2. Simple to use with Graphical Interface
    3. Validate Your Images
      - test and validate the images, e.g., for security compliance and functionality,
        using AWS provided tests or your own custom tests

  Why is EC2 Image Builder so cool?
    - automates the process of creating and maintaining your images
    - when software updates are available, Image build can automatically create a new image,
      run validation tests on the new image, and make it available to the AWS regions
      of your choice
    - Allows you to share your AMIs with other AWS accounts that you own

  EC2 Image Builder steps:
     1. Base OS
       - provide a base OS Image, e.g., Amazon Linux 2 AMI
     2. Define software to install
       - define software to install, e.g., .NET, Node.js, Python, latest security updates,
         latest kernel, security settings
     3. Test new image
       - run tests on the new image
       - for example, does it boot correctly?
       - it will spin up a new ec2 instance, and run tests on it
     4. Distribute AMI
       - distribute the image to the regions of your choice (by default, the region you are
         operating in).

  EC2 Image Builder Terminology
    Image Pipeline
      - defines the configuration and end-to-end process of building images include the image
        recipe, distribution, and test settings
    Image Recipe
      - creates a recipe for each image, which can be shared, version controlled, and re-used
      - includes:
         Source image - e.g., Amazon Linux 2 AMI
         Build Components - e.g., Apache Tomcat
    Build Components
       - the software to include in the image

   Note: Apache Tomcat: open source web server and Servlet container for Java code

  Exam Tips

    EC2 Image Builder
     1. Automated
       - automates the process of creating and maintaining AMI and Container images
     2. 4-Step Process
        - select a base image
        - customize by adding software (e.g., Apache Tomcat)
        - test
        - distribute to your chosen regions
     3. Terminology
        Image Pipeline:   settings and process
        Image Recipe:     Source image and build components
        Build components: The software to include

---------------------------------------------------
3.25 Demo: Creating and AMI Using EC2 Image Builder

   Lesson Objectives
     1. Create an IAM Role
        - add permissions for EC2 Image Builder

       console -> IAM -> Roles -> Create Role -> Trusted Entity Type: AWS Service, Use Case: EC2
          -> Next -> Permission Policies: EC2InstanceProfileForImageBuilder, AmazonSSMManagedInstanceCore
          -> Next -> Role Name: MyImageBuilderRole,
           Tag: team: developers -> Create role

       Note: AmazonSSMManagedInstanceCore policy is needed because Image Builder uses System Manager (SSM) Agent

     2. Create an Image Pipeline
       - this is where we will define our configuration settings

       console -> EC2 Image Builder -> Create Image Pipeline ->
          Pipeline name: "My Image Pipeline", unselect "Enable enhanced metadata collection"
          Build Schedule: Manual; -> Next ->
          Choose Recipe: Recipe: "create new recipe"; Image Type: Output Type: AMI,
          General: Name: "My Recipe", version: 1.0.0;
          Base Image: Select Image: "Selected managed images", Image OS: Amazon Linux,
            Image Origin: Quick Start (Amazon-managed) ,  Image Name: "Amazon Linux 2 x86"
            Instance Configuration: select "Remove SSM agent after pipeline execution"
            Components:
              Step1 Choose Build Components ....
                 Build Components: "update-linux"
              Step2 Optional select tests to verify the output AMI
                 Test Components: simple-boot-test-linux
               -> Next ->
               Define infrastucture configuration
                  Infrastucturce configuration: "Create a new infrastucture configuration"
                  Name: My Config, IAM Role: MyImageBuilderRole
                  AWS Infrastucture: Instance Type: t3.micro, SNS Topic: <skip>
               Define distribution settings - optional
                 Configuration options: "Create distribution settings using service defaults"
                  (note: create ami in current regions)
                  -> Next -> Review -> Create Pipeline

       Note:
          - "Enable enhanced metadata collection" option uses Systems Manager inventory to collect
             additional information about the image but requires some additional Systems Manager
             configuration.
          - Image builder can be used to create AMIs and Docker images

     3. Execute the Pipeline
       - Notice that Image builder launches temporary build and test EC2 instances

       -> console -> EC2 Image Builder -> select "My Image Pipeline" -> Action -> Run
         -> click on "View Details"
          -> could take 20 minutes
       -> console -> EC2 -> should see build instance, followed by test instance


     4. View the Image
       - we can view the image in the image builder and from the EC2 console under AMIs


   Note: Failed to get details for image:
   Error message: CredentialsError: Missing credentials in config, if using AWS_CONFIG_FILE, set AWS_SDK_LOAD_CONFIG=1

   Exam Tips

     EC2 Image Builder steps:
       1. Base OS
         - provide a base OS Image, e.g., Amazon Linux 2 AMI
       2. Define software to install
         - define software to install, e.g., .NET, Node.js, Python, latest security updates,
           latest kernel, security settings
       3. Test new image
         - run tests on the new image
         - for example, does it boot correctly?
         - it will spin up a new ec2 instance, and run tests on it
       4. Distribute AMI
         - distribute the image to the regions of your choice (by default, the region you are
           operating in).

3.26 Using AMIs in a Different Region

  AMIs are Regional
    - an AMI only exist in 1 region and can only be used in the region it belongs to
    - to use an AMI in a different region, you must first copy it to that region

  Copying an AMI
    - When copying an AMI, you can select the destination region
    - if AMI is unecrypted, it will have an option to "Encrypt EBS snapshot of the AMI copy"

  AMI Encryption and Copying
      Scenario                               Supported
      Unencrypted AMI to unencrypted AMI       Yes
      Encrypted   AMI to encrypted   AMI       Yes
      Unencrypted AMI to encrypted   AMI       Yes
      Encrypted   AMI to unencrypted AMI       No

    - By default, if you do not specify encryption parameters, the AMI is copied with its original
      encryption status
    - if you want to encrypt an existing AMI, create a copy and specify encryption to happen during
      the copying process, then delete the original unecrypted AMI

  Exam Tips

    Using AMI in a Different Region
      - to use an AMI in a different region, create a copy specifying the destination region
    Encryption status
      - you can apply encryption during the copying process
      - you can not remove encryption during the copying process


---------------------------------------------------
3.27 EC2 Summary - Part 1

  EC2 Overview
    - EC2 is like a VM, hosted in AWS instead of your own data center
    - select the capacity your need right now
    - grow and shrink when you need
    - pay for what you use
    - to provision, wait for minutes, not months to access


  EC2 pricing options
    on-demand instances
      - pay by the hour or second depending on the type of instance you run
      - great for flexiblity
    reserved instances
      - reserve capacity for 1 to 3 years
      - up to 72% discount on the hourly charge
      - the long the contract and the more you pay up front, the greater the discount
      - great if you have known, fixed requirements
      Regional Reserved Instances
        - When you purchase a Reserved Instance for a Region, it's referred to as a regional Reserved Instance.
        - A regional Reserved Instance does not reserve capacity.
      Zonal Reserved Instances
        - A zonal Reserved Instance reserves capacity in the specified Availability Zone.
        - When you purchase a Reserved Instance for a specific Availability Zone, it's referred to as a zonal Reserved Instance
    spot instances
      - purchase unused capacity at a discount of up to 90%
      - prices fluctuate with supply and demand
      - great for applications with flexible start and end times
      - If EC2 interrupts your Spot Instance in the first hour, before a full hour of running time,
        you're not charged for the partial hour used.
      - when price exceeds your max price to pay for the instance, then the instance will be
        terminated or hypernated depending on the options you selected
    dedicated hosts
      - a physical EC2 server dedicated for your use
      - the most expensive option
      - great if you have server-bound licenses to reuse or compliance requirements (e.g.
        can not use multi-tenent hardware)


  Instance Type
    - determines the hardware of the host computer
    - each instance type offers different compute, memory, and storage capabilities
    - grouped in Instance families
    - select an instance type based on the requirements of your application

  EBS volume type differences
    https://aws.amazon.com/premiumsupport/knowledge-center/ebs-volume-type-differences/

  EBS (Elastic Block Store) SSD Volume types
    - highly available and scalable storage volumes you can attach to an EC2 instance
    Note: if you want more than 16K IOPS, then use io1/io2
    General Purpose SSD (gp2)
      - suitable for boot disks and general applications
      - 16K IOPS per volume
      - up to 99.9 durability
      - Good for boot volumes or development and test applications that are not latency sensitive
    General Purpose SSD (gp3)
      - suitable for high perfomance applications
      - Predicatable 3K IOPS baseline perfomrance and 125 Mb/s regardless of volume size
      - up to 99.9 durability
      - 20% cheaper than gp2
    Provisioned IOPS SSD (io1)
      - suitable for OLTP (On-Line Transaction Processing) and latency-sensitive applications
      - 4K MB/s throughput per volume, up to 64K IOPS per volume
      - 50 IOPS per GiB
      - use if you need more than 16K IOPS
      - up to 99.9 durability
      - used for High perfomance and most expensive
    Provisioned IOPS SSD (io2)
      - suitable for OLTP (On-Line Transaction Processing) and latency-sensitive applications
      - 500 IOPS per GiB, up to 64K IOPS
      - 99.999 durability instead of up to 99.9
      - same [base?] price for io2 as io1
    Provisioned IOPS SSD (io2 block express)
      - for the largest, most critical, high-performance applications like SAP HANA, Oracle,
        Microsoft SQL Server, and IBM DB2
      - highest performance, sub-millsecond latency
      - up to 64 TB, 256K IOPS per volume
      - 99.999 durability
      - SAN (Storage Area Network) in the cloud
      - latest generation - available only with some instance types and in some regions
      - same durability as io2 and more IOPS than io2
      - io2 Block Express is the same price as io2

  EBS HDD Volume types
    Throughput Optimized HDD (st1)
      - use cases: big data, data warehousing, ETL (Extract/Tranform/Load), and log processing
      - max throughput of 500 Mb/s per volume
      - cannot be a boot volume
    Cold HDD (sc1)
      - max throughput of 250 Mb/s per volume
      - used for less frequently accessed data
      - low cost options
      - cannot be a boot volume
      - up to 99.9 durability

  EBS Volume comparison table:
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/ebs-volume-types.html

  EBS Snapshots
    - a point-in-time copy of the EBS volume.
    - Great for backing up EBS volumes
    - you can usea snapshot to create a new EBS volume
    Encrypted Snapshots
      - If you create an EBS volume from an encrypted snapshot, then you will get an encrypted volume
    Unencrypted Snapshots
      - If you create an EBS volume from an unencrypted snapshot, then encryption is only optional if
        default encryption has not been set at account level by your account admin

  EBS Encryption
    - EBS works with AWS KMS to encrypt and decrypt your EBS volume.
    - You can encrypt both the boot and data volumes of an EC2 instance.
    - When you create an encrypted EBS volume and attach it to a supported instance type, the following types
      of data are encrypted:
        - Data at rest inside the volume
        - All data moving between the volume and the instance
        - All snapshots created from the volume
        - All volumes created from those snapshots

  Elastic Load Balancer (ELB)
    4 Types of Load Balancers
       Application Load Balancer
         - intelligent load balancing for HTTP/HTTPS
         - route requests to a specific web server based on request type
         - best suited for load balancing of HTTP and HTTPS traffic
         - operates at Layer 7 and are application aware
       Network Load Balancer
         - high performance load balancer for TCP traffic
         - best suited for load balancing of Transmission Control Protocol (TCP), User Datagram
           Protocol (UDP) and Transport Layer Security (TLS) traffic where extreme performance is required
         - operates at the transport layer (layer 4)
         - are capable of handling millions of requests per sec, while maintaining ultra-low latenceis
         - can be assigned a static IP address (note: ALB cannot be assigned a static IP address)
         - low latency and the most expensive
         - Incoming connections remain unmodified, so application software need not support X-Forwarded-For
       Classic Load Balancer
         - legacy load balancers that support both HTTP/HTTP and TCP
         - can load HTTP/HTTPS applications and use layer-7 specific features, such a X-Forwarded and
           stickly seessions
         - Classic/Test/Dev Load Balancer
       Gateway Load Balancer
         - allows you to load balance workloads for 3rd party virtual appliances running in AWS, such as:
            - Virtual appliances purchased using AWS Marketplace
            - Virtual firewalls from companies like Fortinet, Palo Alto, Juniper, Cisco
            - IDS/IPS systems from companies like CheckPoint, Trend Micro, etc

    X-Forwarded-For
      - if you need the IPv4 address of your end user, look for the X-Forwarded header
      - applicable to Application Load Balancer and Classic Load Balancer

  Application Load Balancers (ALBs)
     Listeners:
       - a listener checks for connection requests from clients, using the protocol and port your configure
       - ports 80 or 443
     Rules
      - determine how the load balancer routes requests to its registered targets
      - each rule consists of a priority, one or actions, and one or more conditions

    Limitations of Application Load Balancers
      - only support HTTP and HTTPS

    ALB Target Groups
      - each target group routes requests to one or more registered targets, such as EC2s, Lambda, etc, using
        protocol and port number you specify
      - You can register a target with multiple target groups
      Target Types:
        instance: The targets are specified by instance ID.
        ip:       The targets are private IP addresses.
                    - You can't specify publicly routable IP addresses.
        lambda:   The target is a Lambda function.

     HTTPS Load balancing
       - to use HTTPS listener, you must deploy at least one SSL/TLS server certificate on you LB
       - LB uses server certificate to terminate the frontend connection and then decrypt requests
       - You can create an HTTPS listener, which uses encrypted connections (also known as SSL offload).
         This feature enables traffic encryption between your load balancer and the clients that initiate
         SSL or TLS sessions
       - As the EC2 instances are under heavy CPU load, the load balancer will use the server certificate to
         terminate the front-end connection and then decrypt requests from clients before sending them to the EC2 instances


     Error 504
       - Gateway Timeout
       - The target/application failed to respond within the timeout period
       - this could be either at the web server layer or database layer

---------------------------------------------------
3.27 EC2 Summary - Part 2

  Route 53 Exam Tips

    Route 53
    - Amazon's DNS service
    - allows you to map a domain name that you own to:
       - EC2 instances
       - load balancers
       - S3 buckets
    Mapping Route 53 to Application Load Balancer
      - map a friendly domain name to our Application Load Balancer so we can access our website using
        our domain name (e.g. ilovecloud.com) instead of the App LB DNS name
        - Used Route 53 create record to created an A-record, which is also and Alias, to map out Domain
          name to the Load Balancer's DNS address
    Route 53 Terminology
      Hosted Zone
        - a container for DNS records for your domain
      Alias
        - allows you to route traffic addressed to the zone apex, or top of the DNS namespace,
          e.g., ilovecloud.com, and send it to a resources with AWS, e.g. an Elastic Load Balancer
      A Record
        - allows you to route traffic to a resource, such as a web server, using an IPv4 address


   CLI Exam tips

     Least Privileges
       - Always give your users the minimum amount of access required to do their job
     Use Groups
       - create IAM groups an assign your users to groups
       - group permissions are assigned using IAM policy documents

     AWS CLI
        1. secret access key
          - you will only see it once. If you lose it, you can delete the access key ID and secret
            access key and regenerate them
          - You will need to run "aws configure" again
        2. Don't share key pairs
          - each developer should have their own access key ID and secret access key.
        2. AWS CLI supports Linux, Windows, & MacOS
          - you can install the CLI on your local Mac, Linux, or Windows PC
          - you can also use it on EC2 instances (preinstalled on Amazon Linux 2)

     AWS CLI Pagination
       - if you do see errors like "timed out" or errors related to too many results being returned:
           - adjust the pagination for the CLI results to avoid errors generated by too many results
            aws s3api list-objects --bucket <myBucket> --page-size 100
       - the CLI still retrieves the full list, but it performs a larger number of API calls in
         the background and retrieves a smaller number of items with each call

     AWS CLI options:

  AWS Roles Exam Tips
    --page-size     - specify that the AWS CLI requests a smaller number of items from each call to the AWS service.
                      The CLI still retrieves the full list but performs a larger number of service API calls in the
                      background and retrieves a smaller number of items with each call.
                    - By default, the AWS CLI uses a page size of 1000 and retrieves all available items
    --max-items     - prints out only the number of items at a time that you specify
                    - if the number of items output (--max-items) is fewer than the total number of items returned
                      by the underlying API calls, the output includes a NextToken that you can pass to a subsequent
                      command to retrieve the next set of item
    --starting-token  - use with <NextToken> value to retrieve items starting with <NextToken>

    Using Roles to access AWS Resources
    - Roles can be used to give EC2s instances access to AWS resources like S3
      Flow to give EC2 access to S3
        1. Create an IAM Role with S3 Access
        2. Create an EC2 instance and attach the role we just created
        3. Access S3 from our EC2 instance

    Using Roles
      1. The Preferred Option
        - Roles are the preferred from a security perspective
      2. Avoid Hard Coding your Credentials
        - roles allow you to provide access without the use of access key ID and secret access keys
      3. Policies
        - policies control a role's permissions
      4. Policy Updates
         - you can update a policy attached to a role, and it will take immediate effect
      5. Attaching and Detaching Roles
        - you can attach and detach roles to running EC2 instances without having to stop or
          terminate these instances. It will take immediate effect.

  RDS Exam Tips
    Note: RDBMS : Relational Database Management System

    RDS Database Types:
       Microsoft SQL Server, Oracle, MySQL, PostgreSQL, MariaDB, & Amazon Aurora

    RDS is for OLTP (OnLine Transaction Processing) workloads
      - great for processing lots of small transactions like customers orders, backing transactions,
        payments, and booking systems
    RDS is NOT suitable for OLAP
      - not suitable for OLAP (OnLine Analytical Processing) workloads
      - use a data warehouse like Redshift, which is optimized for OLAP

    RDS Backups vs Snapshots
      RDS Automated Backup
         - Automated, enabled by default, you define the backup window
         - Point-in-Time recovery using daily backup snapshot plus transaction logs
         - retention period of 1 to 35 days
         - Can be used to recover your database to any point-in-time within your retention period
         - Automated backups are limited to a single AWS Region
      RDS DB [Manual] Snapshot
        - User-initiated, ad-hoc
        - point-in-time snapshot only
        - no retention period; store indefinitely (till manually deleted)
        - Used to backup your DB instance to a known state and restore to that specific state at
          any time, e.g., before making a change to the database

     RDS Encryption
       1. Enable at Creation
          - includes all underlying storage, automated backups, snapshots, logs, and read replicas
          - can only enable encryption when you create the RDS DB
       2. KMS Integration
        - uses the AWS Key Management Service (KMS) service. AES-256 encryption
          Note: AES: Advanced Encryption Standard
       3. Encrypting Existing unencrypted RDS Instances
          - Take a RDS DB snapshot, create an encrypt snapshot, then create a new RDS instance
            using the encrypted snapshot

    RDS Multi-AZ
      - an exact copy of your production database in another AZ
      - used for disaster recovery (DR)
      - in the event of a failure, RDS will automatically fail over to the standby instance

    RDS read-replicas
      - a read-only copy of your primary database in the same AZ, cross-AZ, or cross-region
      - used to increase or scale read performance
      - great for read-heavy workloads and takes the load off you primary database
        for read-only workloads (e.g. Business intelligence reporting jobs)

    RDS Storage Scaling
      - With storage auto scaling enabled, when RDS detects that you are running out of free database space
        it automatically scales up your storage
      - RDS storage only scales up, it won't scale back down
      - Aurora starts with 10GB, scales storage automatically in 10GB increments to 128TB (storage auto scaling)

    ElastiCache
      - an in-memory cached designed to improve read performance/latency for read-heavy databases
        or compute-intensive workloads (such as a recommendation engine) by allowing you to store the
        objects that are often read in the cache.
      - it can front just about any database, but really excels being placed in
        front of RDS

    ElastiCache Memcached vs Redis
      Memcached
        - in-memory, key-value data store
        - one to use if object caching is your primary goal
        - a good choice if you just want basic caching and you want your caching model to be
          simple as possible
        - scales horizontally, but there is a no persistence, Multi-AZ or failover
        - if you don't need to support advanced data types or sorting
      Redis
        - in-memory, key-value data store
        - you are performing data sorting and ranking, such as gaming leaderboards
        - if you have advanced data types, such as lists and hashes
        - you need data persistence
        - if you need Multi-AZ

   Store Session data with ElastiCache
    - ElastiCache is the best option for storing session state as it is scalable, highly available,
      and can be accessed by multiple web servers.
    - DynamoDB is not optimal, but could be used for storing session state.

   ElastiCache [MemCached] Caching Stategies:
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonElastiCache/latest/mem-ug/Strategies.html
     Lazy Loading
       - lazy loading is a caching strategy that loads data into the cache only when necessary
       - Only requested data is cached
       get_customer(customer_id)
       Lazy Loading Example Psuedo Code:
         // Attempts to retrieve the record from the cache. If it is retrieved, the record is returned to the application.
         // If the record is not retrieved from the cache, it is retrieved from the database,  added to the cache, and
         // returned to the application

            customer_record = cache.get(customer_id)
            if (customer_record == null)

                customer_record = db.query("SELECT * FROM Customers WHERE id = {0}", customer_id)
                cache.set(customer_id, customer_record)

            return customer_record

     Write-through
       - The write-through strategy adds data or updates data in the cache whenever data is written to the database.
       Write-through pseudocode example
         // function that saves a customer's record.
         save_customer(customer_id, values)         // the application code that gets the data

             customer_record = db.query("UPDATE Customers WHERE id = {0}", customer_id, values)
             cache.set(customer_id, customer_record)
             return success

     Adding TTL
       - Lazy loading allows for stale data but doesn't fail with empty nodes.
       - Write-through ensures that data is always fresh, but can fail with empty nodes and can populate the cache
         with superfluous data.
       - By adding a time to live (TTL) value to each write, you can have the advantages of each strategy. At the same
         time, you can and largely avoid cluttering up the cache with extra data.
       - Time to live (TTL) is an integer value that specifies the number of seconds until the key expires. Memcached specifies this value in seconds.
     TTL pseudocode examples
       - The following is a pseudocode example of write-through logic with TTL.

         // function that saves a customer's record. The TTL value of 300 means that the record expires
         //    300 seconds (5 minutes) after the set command and future reads will have to query the database.
         save_customer(customer_id, values)

            customer_record = db.query("UPDATE Customers WHERE id = {0}", customer_id, values)
            cache.set(customer_id, customer_record, 300)

            return success

       - The following is a pseudocode example of lazy loading logic with TTL.

         // function that returns a customer's record.
         // Attempts to retrieve the record from the cache. If it is retrieved, the record is returned to the application.
         // If the record is not retrieved from the cache, it is  retrieved from the database,  added to the cache, and
         //    returned to the application.
         // The TTL value of 300 means that the record expires 300 seconds (5 minutes) after the set command
         //    and subsequent reads will have to query the database.
         get_customer(customer_id)

             customer_record = cache.get(customer_id)

             if (customer_record != null)
                 if (customer_record.TTL < 300)
                     return customer_record        // return the record and exit function

             // do this only if the record did not exist in the cache OR
             //    the TTL was >= 300, i.e., the record in the cache had expired.
             customer_record = db.query("SELECT * FROM Customers WHERE id = {0}", customer_id)
             cache.set(customer_id, customer_record, 300)  // update the cache
             return customer_record                // return the newly retrieved


    RDS Proxy:
      - pools and share database connections to assist with applicaiton scalability and database
        efficiency
      - fits between your client application and your RDS Database
      - performs connection pooling so that maintains a pool of active connections to the backend database
      - it helps with application scalability and DB efficiency

    RDS Proxy Connection:

            Client   <------------->   Proxy   <------------------------>  MySQL
        Client Application            RDS Proxy                            RDS DB
      Your application is pointed   RDS Proxy pools and shares        RDS DB receives information
      toward an RDS Proxy           DB connections to assist with     from the application through
                                    application scalability and       RDS Proxy
                                    DB efficiency


    RDS Proxy Increases Application Availability
      Serverless and scales automatically
        - to your workload through pooling and sharing database connections
      Preserves application connections
        - during failover
      Detects failover and routes requests
        - to standby quickly
      Deployable over Multi-AZ for protection
        - from infracture failure
      Up to 66% faster failover times


      App ------->  |------|
                    | RDS  |  ----> RDS primary DB
      App ------->  | Proxy|
                    |      |  ----> RDS standby DB
      App ------->  |      |
                    |------|

  MemoryDB for Redis
    1. In-memory database
      - Ultra-Fast Performance
      - supports > 160 million requests per second
      - microsecond read and single-digit millisecond write latency
      - massively scalable > 100 TB
    2. Use Cases
      - high-performance, large-scale microservices applications
      - online game with millions of users sharing digital assets
    3.  MemoryDB vs ElastiCache for Redis
      - memoryDB can store your whole dataset in memory - no database required
      - ElastiCache is an in-memory cache for databases (e.g. RDS)

---------------------------------------------------
3.27 EC2 Summary - Part 3

  Parameter Store
     Store confidential information
       - you can store passwords, database connection strings, license code, etc.
     Plain Text or Encrypted
       - you can  store values as plain text or encrypt them
     Reference
       - you can reference your parameter using the parameter name
         (e.g. in a bootstrap script)
     Integrated with AWS Services
       - you can use Parameter Store with EC2, CloudFormation, Lambda, CodeBuild,
         CodePipeline, and CodeDeploy

   Secrets Manager
     - Protect and store secrets for AWS Services, IT Resources, and Applications
     Centrally Manage
       - secrets used to access resources inside and outside AWS
     Rotate
       - secrets without code deployment
     Secure
       - Secrets with control of fine-grain permissions and encryption with AWS KMS

   AWS Secrets Manager
     Secret Types:
       RDS Database, Redshift Cluster, DocumentDB Database, Other Databases, API Keys

   Storing Database Secrets
       RDS Database secrets for MySQL, PostgreSQL, Oracle, MariaDB, SQL Server
         Information stored in Secrets
           - username and password
           - server address
           - database name and port

   Secrets Manager vs Parameter Store
      Secrets Manager
        - great for storing Database Credentials and API Keys
        - includes rotation of keys option
        - generate random secrets (e.g. passwords)
        - share secrets across accounts
        - you can use the resource-based policy to the secret including to allow access from other accounts
      Systems Manager Parameter Store
        - Wider set of use Cases
        - Configuration Variables and License Keys
          - Because Systems Manager is used to perform system updates on your EC2 instances, and other
            AWS resources, Parameter Store is designed to store "configuration variables" and "license keys"
        - if you used to store database credentials, beaware that it does not support
          password rotation (crediential rotation)
        - You can use parameter policies, but you cannot use a resource-based policy with a parameter in the Parameter Store

    EC2 Image Builder
      Automated
       - automates the process of creating and maintaining AMI and Container images
      4-Step Process
        1 select a base image
          - e.g,, Amazon Linux 2 AMI
        2 customize by adding software
          - e.g., Apache Tomcat, .NET, Node.js, Python, latest security updates latest kernel patches,
            security settings
        3 run tests on new image
          e.g., does it boot correctly?
        4 distribute to your chosen regions
           - by default, it will be distributed to the region you created it in
      Terminology
        Image Pipeline:   settings and process
        Image Recipe:     Source image and build components
        Build components: The software to include

  Using AMIs in a different Region
    - AMIs are regional
    Using AMI in a Different Region
      - to use an AMI in a different region, create a copy specifying the destination region
    Encryption status
      - you can apply encryption during the copying process
      - you can not remove encryption during the copying process

4.1 S3 101

  S3 FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/s3/faqs/
  Amazon S3 Performance Tip & Tricks
    https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/amazon-s3-performance-tips-tricks-seattle-hiring-event/
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/optimizing-performance.html
  Sharing an object with a presigned URL (query string parameters / query parameters)
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/ShareObjectPreSignedURL.html


   What is S3 (Simple Storage Service)
    Object Store
      - secure, durable, highly scalable object storage
      - manages data as objects rathern than in file systems or data blocks
      - upload any file type to S3, e.g.: photos, videos, code, documents, text files
      - cannot be used for an Operating System or database
    Simple
        - easy to use, with a simple web service interface
    Scalable
      - store and retreive any amount of data from anywhere on the web at a very low cost

    S3 Basics
      Unlimited Storage
      - the total volume of data and the number of objects you can store is unlimited
      Objects up to 5 TB in size
      - S3 objects can range in size from 0 bytes to 5 TB
      S3 Buckets
      - files are stored in buckets (similar to folders)

    Working with S3 Buckets
      Universal Namespace
       - s3 is a universal namespace - bucket names must be unique globally
      Example S3 URLs
       - bucket name
           https://<bucketname.s3.amazonaws.com/key-name  (N. Virginia)
           or
           https://<bucketname>.<awsRegion>.amazonaws.com/key-name  (other Regions)
      Uploading Files
        - HTTP 200 code if upload was successful (when using API or CLI)

   S3 Objects:
     - key (name of object)
     - value (object data)
     - version ID
     - metadata
     - subresources
        - access control lists
        - torrent

   S3 is a safe place to store your data
     - data is stored across multiple devices at multiple facilities
     Highly Available and Highly Durable
        Availability:
          - Built for 99.95 and 99.99 service availability depending on S3 tier
        Durability:
          - designed for 99.999999999% (11 9's) durability for data stored in S3
        S3 guarantees
          - AWS guarantees 99.9% availability
          - AWS guarantees 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s)

   S3 data consistency
      - read after write consistency for PUTS of new objects
      - eventual consistency of overwrite PUTS and DELETES (can take time to propagate)

   S3 features
     - tiered storaage
     - lifecycle management (change tier after time periods)
     - versioning
     - encryption at reset
     - MFA deletes (when versioning is enabled)
     - Secure your data using Access Control Lists and Bucket Policies

   Securing your S3 data
     Server-Side Encryption
       - you can set default encryption on a bucket to encrypt all new objects when they
         are stored in the bucket
     Access Control Lists (ACLs)
       - define which AWS accounts or groups are granted access and the type of access
       - you can attach ACLs to individual objects within the bucket
     Bucket Policies
       - S3 bucket policies specify what actions are allowed or denied (e.g., allow user Alice
         to PUT but not DELETE objects in the bucket)


  Exam Tips:

    S3 Basics
     Object-Based
      - S3 is object based
     Files up tp 5 TB
      - files can be from 0 bytes to 5 TB can be uploaded
     Unlimited storage
       - the total volume of data and the number of objects you can store is unlimited
     Not for OS or DB storage
       - not suitable to install an operating system or run a database on
     S3 Buckets
      - files are stored in buckets (similar to folders)
     Universal Namespace
       - s3 is a universal namespace - bucket names must be unique globally
       Example S3 URLs
         - bucket name
            https://<bucketname>.s3.amazonaws.com/key-name  (N. Virginia)
            or
            https://<bucketname>.<awsRegion>.amazonaws.com/key-name  (other Regions)
     Uploading Files
       - HTTP 200 code if upload was successful (when using API or CLI)

   S3 Objects:
     - key (name of object)
     - value (object data)
     - version ID
     - metadata
     - subresources
        - access control lists
        - torrent

4.2 Reviewing S3 Storage Classes

   S3 Storage Classes:
     https://aws.amazon.com/s3/storage-classes/
   S3 Glacier Storage Classes:
     https://aws.amazon.com/s3/storage-classes/glacier/
   New Amazon S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval storage class
     https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2021/11/amazon-s3-glacier-instant-retrieval-storage-class/

    S3 Storage classes
       - S3 Standard
         - 99.99% availability
         - 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s)
         - stored redundantly across multiple devices in multiple facilities (>= 3 AZs)
            - designed to sustain the lost of 2 facilities concurrently
         - 1st byte latency: millisec
       - S3 IA (infrequently accessed)
         - for data accessed less frequently, but requires rapid access when needed
         - lower fee than S3, but charged a retrieval fee
            - low per-GB storage price and a per-GB retrieval fee
         - 1st byte latency: millisec
         - use cases: long-term storage, backups, and disaster recovery files
         - minimum storage duration: 30 days
         - 99.99% availability
         - 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s)
       - S3 One Zone IA (infrequently accessed)
         - data is stored redundantly within a single AZ
         - lower cost option for infrequently accessed data (20% less than S3-IA),
           but do not require multiple availability & data resilience
         - great for long-lived, infrequently accessed, non-critical data
         - minimum storage duration: 30 days
         - 1st byte latency: millisec
         - 99.5% availability (due to data being stored in 1 AZ)
         - 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s)
       - S3 Glacier
         - provides long-term data archiving with retrieval times that range from 1 minute to 12 hours
         - great for storing historical data only accessed a few times per year
         - secure, durable, and lower cost storage classes for data archiving
         - you pay a fee each time you access your data
         - use only for archiving data
         - minimum storage duration: 90 days
         - 99.99% availability
         - 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s)
       - S3 Glacier Deep Archive
         - archives rarely accessesd data with a default retrieval time of 12 hours, e.g., financial
           records that may be accessed once or twice per year
         - minimum storage duration: 180 days
         - lowest cost storage class
         - retrieval time of 12 hours is acceptable
         - 99.99% availability
         - 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s)
       - S3 Intelligent Tiering
         - 2 tiers: frequent and infrequent access
         - uses machine learning to optimize your storage costs
         - optimize costs by automatically moving data to the most cost-effective access tier
         - optimizes costs adds montly fee of $0.0025 per 1K objects
         - 1st byte latency: millisec
         - 99.99% availability
         - 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s)

    Note: Now you can choose from 3 S3 Glacier storage classes:
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazonglacier/latest/dev/introduction.html
     Glacier Instant Retrievel
       - for archive data that needs to be immediately accessed
     Glacier Flexible Retrievel (formly Glacier)
       - for archive data that does not require immediate access but needs the flexibility to retrieve
         large sets of data at no cost
       -  data be access as little as 1 to 5 min using expedited retrieval or 5 to 12 hours for
          free bulk retrieval
     Glacier Deep Archive
       - for long-lived archive use cases at the lowest cost
       - 12 hour default retrival time

    S3 Charges:
      - Storage
      - requests
      - storage management pricing
      - data transfer pricing
      - transfer acceleration
      - cross region replication

  Exam Tips

    S3 Storage classes
       - S3 Standard
         - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
         - Suitable for most workloads, e.g., websites, content distribution, mobile and gaming
           applications, and big data analytics
       - S3 IA (infrequently accessed)
         - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
         - long-term, infrequently accessed critical data, e.g. backups, data stored for disaster
           recovery, etc.
         - minimum storage duration: 30 days
       - S3 One Zone IA (infrequently accessed)
         - 99.5% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 1 AZ
         - long-term, infrequently accessed, non-critical data
         - minimum storage duration: 30 days
       - S3 Glacier
         - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
         - long-term data archiving that occasionally needs to be accessed within a few hours or minutes
         - minimum storage duration: 90 days
       - S3 Glacier Deep Archive
         - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
         - rarely accessed data archiving with a defualt retrieval time of 12 hours
         - use case: financial records for regulatory purposes.
         - minimum storage duration: 180 days

       - S3 Intelligent Tiering
         - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
         - unknown or unpredicatable access patterns.
         - minimum storage duration: 30 days
         - uses machine learning to optimize your storage costs
         - optimize costs by automatically moving data to the most cost-effective access tier

       Read S3 FAQ before taking the exam
         e.g.; https://aws.amazon.com/s3/faqs/

---------------------------------------------------
4.3 Securing S3 Buckets


 S3 Security by Default
   Private
   - by default, all newly create buckets are PRIVATE.
   Bucket Owner
     - by default, only the bucket owner can upload new files, read files, delete files, etc.
   No Public Access
     - by default, no public access

   Bucket Policies
     - You can setup access to your buckets using Bucket policies (work at bucket level)
     - the permissions granted by the policy apply to all objects within the bucket
     - AWS provides a policy generator tool to build out policy that you need
     - written in JSON, e.g. bucket policy to provide public read access
         {
         "Version":"2012-10-17",
         "Statement":[
            {
              "Sid":"PublicRead",
              "Effect":"Allow",
              "Principal": "*",
              "Action":"s3:GetObject",
              "Resource":["arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/*"],
            }
           ]
          }

   S3 Bucket Access Control Lists
     - applied at object level
     - can apply different permissions for different objects within a bucket
     - we can define which accounts or groups are granted access and also the type of
       access, e.g., read, write, or full control

   S3 Access Logs
     - buckets can configured to create access logs (not enabled by default)
     - log all requests made to the S3 bucket
     - e.g., log every time a user makes a request to upload a file, read a file, or delete a file
     - access log can be sent to another bucket and even another bucket in another account


  Exam tips:

    Securing S3
      S3 Secure by Default
        - by default, all newly created buckets are private
      Control Access to Buckets
        Bucket Policies:             applied at the bucket level
        Access Control Lists (ACLs): Applied at the object level
      Access Logs
        - S3 buckets can be configured to create access logs, which log all requests made to the S3 bucket
        - can be written to another bucket or even another bucket in another account
        - not enabled/configured by default

4.4 S3 Bucket Policies Demo

  1. Create an S3 Bucket
    - review the options available in S3

    -> S3 console -> S3 -> create bucket -> Bucket name "pathalldemobkt" use defaults -> create bucket

      - by default, public access of your buckets are blocked
      - ownership - ACL disabled, ACLs enabled
      - Public Access options
      - bucket option
         - bucket versioning
         - default encryption
         - bucket key
      - Advanced settings
         - object Lock (default: disabled)

  2. Upload a file
     - from our local machine

      -> upload a pic
      -> select pic -> Open -> opens pic
      -> examine objects in bucket -> each has a URL
      https://<bucketName>.s3.amazonaws.com/<objectName>
      select pic -> click on URL -> fails to open with "access denied" because it does not have public access

  3. Configure a bucket policy
    - Use a bucket policy to enable public acces to our file

      -> bucket -> Permission -> "block public access" -> edit -> unselect "Block all public access" -> save changes

      Note: -> Permissions Overview: access : Bucket and object not public


      -> bucket -> Bucket Policy -> Edit -> Policy Generator ->
            in AWS Policy Generator ->
                1. Select Policy Type: S3 Bucket Policy
                2. Add Statements: Effect: Allow, Principal: * (to allow public) , Acition: GetObject,
                   ARN: arn:aws:s3:::pathalldemobkt/* (s3 bucket ARN)
                   -> click on Add Statement
                3. Generate Policy
                   -> click on Generate Policy
                   -> copy generated policy

       # back Bucket Policy Edit:
          paste generate policy to Policy
            {
              "Id": "Policy1686943023655",
              "Version": "2012-10-17",
              "Statement": [
                {
                  "Sid": "Stmt1686942990072",
                  "Action": [
                    "s3:GetObject"
                  ],
                  "Effect": "Allow",
                  "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::pathalldemobkt/*",
                  "Principal": "*"
                }
              ]
            }
            -> ignore error
            -> Save Changes

            # refresh screen then:
             Note: -> Permissions Overview: access : Public

             -> Bucket -> pic object -> click on url -> now pic is visible


  Exam Tips

      S3 Bucket Policy
      - all newly created buckets are private by default
      - control access using bucket policy
      - must disable "Block Public" before ACL or Bucket Policy will provide public access

4.5 Create a Static Website Using Amazon S3

arn:aws:s3:::my-bucket-pathall

-------
Additional Resources:

Note: When creating the bucket, uncheck all four checkboxes on step 3 - Set Permissions. If you skip this step,
you will not be allowed to create the bucket policy later. If you made a mistake and created the bucket without
unchecking the checkboxes, you may go to Bucket > Permissions > Public access settings > Edit, and uncheck all
four restrictions. Then add a bucket policy, go to Bucket > Permissions > Bucket Policy, and add the
following JSON statement (replacing <MY_BUCKET> with your bucket name):
{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Id": "Policy1645724938586",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "Stmt1645724933619",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Principal": "*",
            "Action": "s3:GetObject",
            "Resource": "<BUCKET_ARN>/*"
        }
    ]
}

>**Note:** Ensure the trailing `/*` is present so the policy applies to all objects within the bucket.

    Click Save changes.

Ensure the trailing /* is present so the policy applies to all objects within the bucket.

-------
Learning Objectives

Optional: Run progress checks to confirm you've completed the objectives
1. Create S3 Bucket

Create an S3 bucket that begins with the name my-bucket (e.g., my-bucket-<ACCOUNT ID>) in the us-east-1 region. When creating the bucket, remember to uncheck the S3 Block Public Access settings. Upload the code for the static site. You will find the code (index.html and error.html) in the following GitHub repository: https://github.com/ACloudGuru-Resources/Course-Certified-Solutions-Architect-Associate/tree/master/labs/creating-a-static-website-using-amazon-s3
Enable Static Website Hosting

You can use Amazon S3 to host a static website. On a static website, individual web pages include s"tatic content. They might also contain client-side scripts.

To host a static website on Amazon S3, configure an Amazon S3 bucket for website hosting, and then upload your website content to the bucket.

When you configure a bucket as a static website, you must enable website hosting, set permissions, and create and add an index document.

You must ensure that the S3 Block Public Access settings are disabled on the bucket, so that the files in the bucket can be made publicly readable.


   -> S3 -> create bucket -> name: my-bucket-pathall
    -> ACLs enabled, unselect "Block all public access -> Create Bucket
    -> select bucket -> copy ARN  arn:aws:s3:::my-bucket-pathall
        -> upload "error.html" & "index.html"
        -> Properties  -> Static Website Hosting -> enable
                       -> Index Document "index.html"
                       -> Error Document "error.html" -> save changes

             -> Static Website hosting now has Bucket Website endpoint
                -> click, but 403 error

        -> go to permissions tab -> edit "bucket policy"
           -> added provided bucket policy but replace <BUCKET> with ARM:
          {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Id": "Policy1645724938586",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "Stmt1645724933619",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Principal": "*",
            "Action": "s3:GetObject",
            "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-bucket-pathall/*"
        }
    ]
}

  -> after applying bucket policy, the web site is visible


2. Apply Bucket Policy

To make objects in your bucket publicly readable, write a bucket policy that grants everyone s3:GetObject permission.

Note that AWS will not permit you to create the policy if the S3 Block Public Access settings are still enabled on the bucket.

After disabling the Block Public Access settings, you can add a bucket policy to grant public read access to your bucket. When you grant public read access, anyone on the internet can access your bucket.


   -> S3 -> create bucket -> name: my-bucket-pathall
    -> ACLs enabled, unselect "Block all public access -> Create Bucket
    -> select bucket -> copy ARN  arn:aws:s3:::my-bucket-pathall
        -> upload "error.html" & "index.html"
        -> Properties  -> Static Website Hosting -> enable
                       -> Index Document "index.html"
                       -> Error Document "error.html" -> save changes

             -> Static Website hosting now has Bucket Website endpoint
                -> click, but 403 error

        -> go to permissions tab -> edit "bucket policy"
           -> added provided bucket policy but replace <BUCKET> with ARM:
          {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Id": "Policy1645724938586",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "Stmt1645724933619",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Principal": "*",
            "Action": "s3:GetObject",
            "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-bucket-pathall/*"
        }
    ]
}

  -> after applying bucket policy, the web site is visible

4.7 Configuring Encryption

  Why Encrypt
    - A Security Best Practice
    - Data is an Asset
    - Encryption: helps protect your data against unauthorized access

  S3 Encryption Options
     Encryption In Transit is achieved by:
        - SSL/TLS (via https) (Secure Socker Layer / Transport Layer Security)
        - HTTPS
     Encryption At Rest (server side encryption) is achieved by:
         - SSE-S3: S3 Managed Keys - (server side encryption S3), using AES 256-bit encryption
           - enabled by default
         - SSE-KMS: AWS Key Management Service
           - Managed Keys - SSE-KMS (Server Side Encryption - Key Management Service)
           - User and AWS manage keys together
         - SSE-C: Server Side Encryption with Customer Provided Keys
            - (Server Side Encryption - Customer)
            - customer manages the encryption keys
     - Encryption At Rest (client side) is achieved by:
         - user encrypts file and then uploads encrypted file to S3

  Exam Tips

     Encryption in Transit
       - SSL/TLS
       - HTTPS
     Encryption At Rest (SSE: server side encryption):
       - SSE-S3 enabled by default
       SSE Types:
         SSE-S3 using AES 256-bit
         SSE-KMS
           - encryption keys managed in the Key Management Service
         SSE-C
           - customer manages the encryption keys
       Client-Side Encryption
         - user encrypts file and then uploads encrypted file to S3

4.8 Configuring Encryption

  Protecting Data Using Server-Side Encryption
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/UsingServerSideEncryption.html

  1. Create an S3 Bucket
    - Note that SSE-S3 encryption is enabled by default
  2. Review Encryption Options
  3. Configure the Use of SSE-KMS Encryption

     # Create S3 Bucket using KMS encryption:

    -> S3 console -> S3 -> create bucket -> Bucket name "pathalldemobktencrypt"
       Encryption Type: select "SSE-KMS", AWS KMS Key: "Choose from your AWS KMS Key";
        select "Create a KMS Key"
           -> On KMS Key page: Key type: Symmetric, Key Usage: Encrypt and decrypt -> Next ->
              Alias: mykmskey -> Next -> Key Administrator: select "Admin" -> Next ->
              Key Users: "cloud_user" -> Next -> Review -> Finish

       # back in S3 create bucket after refresh (next to "Create a KMS key")
         Available AWS KMS Keys: mykmskey
           -> create bucket


      -> S3 -> select "pathalldemobktencrypt" -> properties -> Default Encryption: shows SSE-KMS

      S3 Encryption Notes
        - bucket encryption type options
          - Server-side encryption with Amazon S3 managed keys (SSE-S3)
          - Server-side encryption with AWS Key Management Service keys (SSE-KMS)
          - Dual-layer server-side encryption with AWS Key Management Service keys (DSSE-KMS)
        - Bucket Key option
           - Enable and Disabled


      KMS Notes:
          Key Types:
             Symmetric:
               - A single key used for encrypting and decryptiing data or generating
                  and verifying HMAC codes
             Asymmectic:
               - A public and private key pair for encryption and decrypting data or signing and
                 verify messages

  Exam Tips:

     S3 Encryption
     Encryption in Transit
       - SSL/TLS
       - HTTPS (SSL/TLS is used when accessing a website via HTTS)
     Encryption At Rest (SSE: server side encryption):
       - SSE-S3 enabled by default
       SSE Types:
         SSE-S3 - with S3 managed keys, using AES 256-bit
         SSE-KMS
           - encryption keys managed by the AWS Key Management Service
         SSE-C
           - customer provided encryption keys
       Client-Side Encryption
         - user encrypts file and then uploads encrypted file to S3

4.9 CORS Configuration - Demo

  Cross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS)
    https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-origin_resource_sharing

  CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) Configuration Demo Architecture

                   accesses code in:
      S3 bucket1  ------------------>  S3 bucket2 (with code to access)


      S3 bucket1                 S3 Bucket2   <--- Configure CORS
      Myindexwebsite  ------>  mycorstestbucket
       contains:                 contains:
       index.html     ------>   loadpage.html


      Note:
         index.html refercence "loadpage.html" and can access it via CORS


   -> S3 -> create bucket -> name: myindexwebsite-pat
    -> ACLs disabled, unselect "Block all public access -> Create Bucket

    -> S3 -> select "myindexwebsite-pat1" bucket -> Properties -> Static Website Hosting -> Edit  ->
       Static Website hosting: Enable, Index document: index.html, Error Document: error.html -> Save Changes
       URL: http://myindexwebsite-pat1.s3-website-us-east-1.amazonaws.com


        -> Properties  -> Static Website Hosting -> enable
                       -> Index Document "index.html"
                       -> Error Document "error.html" -> save changes
        -> upload "error.html" , "index.html", & loadpage.html

        -> go to permissions tab -> edit "bucket policy"
           -> add bucket policy (instead setting ACL permissions during upload step which did not work do to
              bucket ACLs not being enabled ??) :
            {
                "Version": "2012-10-17",
                "Id": "Policy1645724938586",
                "Statement": [
                    {
                        "Sid": "Stmt1645724933619",
                        "Effect": "Allow",
                        "Principal": "*",
                        "Action": "s3:GetObject",
                        "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::myindexwebsite-pat1/*"
                    }
                ]
            }

      -> in Web brower go to S3 myindexwsite-pat1 static website URL
         -> successful

         -> myindexwebsite-pat1 -> object -> delete "loadpage.html"

   -> S3 -> create bucket -> name: mycortestbucket-pat1
    -> ACLs enabled, unselect "Block all public access -> Create Bucket

        -> Properties  -> Static Website Hosting -> enable
                       -> Index Document "index.html"
                       -> Error Document "error.html" -> save changes
        -> upload loadpage.html, Permissions -> ACL: Predefined ACLs: Grant Public-read access -> upload

        -> Verify you can access loadpage using <mycorstestbucket-pat1 URL>/loadpage.html

        copy: http://mycorstestbucket-pat1.s3-website-us-east-1.amazonaws.com/loadpage.html

        -> update "index.html" and replace "loadpage.html" with the above
        -> for myindexwebsite-pat1 bucket, delete "index.html" and upload new version


      -> in Web brower go to S3 myindexwsite-pat1 static website URL
         -> has "Welcome ..." but does not loadpage.html line
            -> to see error in firefox -> Setting -> more tools -> Web developer tools ->
                "CORS error"

       copy: http://myindexwebsite-pat1.s3-website-us-east-1.amazonaws.com

      -> bucket: mycorstestbucket-pat1 -> Permissions -> Cross-origin resource sharing (CORS) -> Edit
      add the following:
        [
            {
                "AllowedHeaders": [
                    "Authorization"
                ],
                "AllowedMethods": [
                    "GET"
                ],
                "AllowedOrigins": [
                    "http://myindexwebsite-pat1.s3-website-us-east-1.amazonaws.com"
                ],
                "MaxAgeSeconds": 3000
            }
        ]


      -> in Web brower go to S3 myindexwsite-pat1 static website URL
         -> successful

   Exam Tips:

     S3 Cross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS)
        - allows resources in 1 bucket access resource in another bucket
        - by default, resources in one bucket cannot access resources in a different bucket
        - a good way to organized your website
           - e.g., store image files in 1 bucket, java script in 2nd bucket, and html in another bucket

4.10 Overview of CloudFront

  AWS CloudFront Key Features
  https://aws.amazon.com/cloudfront/features/?whats-new-cloudfront.sort-by=item.additionalFields.postDateTime&whats-new-cloudfront.sort-order=desc

  What is CloudFront:
    - a content delivery network (CDN) is a system of distributed servers (networks) that
      deliver webpages and other web content to a user based on the geographic locations
      of the users, the origin of the webpage, and a content delivery server

  CloudFront Terminology:
    Edge Location
      - location where content will be cached
      - it will be cached for the TTL (Time to Live)
    Origin
       - the origin of all the files that the CDN will distribute.
       - this can be an S3 bucket, an EC2 Instance, and Elastic Load Balancer, or Route53
    Distribution:
      - this is the name give to the Origin and configuration settings for the content you
        wish to distribute using CloudFront CDN

        Overview of distributions:
        https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudFront/latest/DeveloperGuide/distribution-overview.html

   CloudFront Improves the Performance for you Website
     - global networ of 200+ edge location
     - can be used to deliver your entire website, including dynamic, static, streaming, and
       interactive content using a global network of edge locations
     - requests for your content are automatically routed to the nearest edge location, so
       content is delivered with the best possible performance

   CloudFront
     Optimized
       - optimized to work other AWS Web Services
     Integrated with AWS Services
       - with S3, EC2, ELB, and Route 53
     Your Own Server
       - works seamlessly with any non-AWS origin server which stores the original, definitive versions
         of your files

   Time to Live (TTL)
     - objects are cached for a period of time which is the Time to Live (TTL) time
     - the default TTL is 1 day, and when the TTL is up, the object is automatically cleared from the cache
     - you can clear an object from the cache yourself before the TTL is up, but you will be charged


   S3 Transfer Acceleration
      - CloudFront technology is also used with the S3 Transfer Acceleration service
      - enables fast, easy, and secure transfer of files over long distances between your
        end users, and an S3 bucket
      - as the data arrives at an edge location, it is routed to S3 over an optimized
        AWS network path

   CloudFront distribution types:
     - Web Distribution - typically used from Websites
     - RTMP (Real-Time Messaging Protocol) - used for media streaming

   Exam Tips:

   CloudFront
     Edge Location:
        - the location where content will be cached. This is seperate from AWS Regions/AZs
        - edge location are not just read only - you can write to them too (e.g., PUT an object on them).
        - objects are cached for the life of the TTL (time to live)
        - you can clear cached objects (invalidate cache), but you will be charged
     Origin
        - the origin of all the files that the CDN distribution will server.
        - this can be an S3 bucket, an EC2 Instance, and Elastic Load Balancer, or Route53
     Distribution:
        - this is the name give to the Origin and configuration settings for the content you
          wish to distribute using CloudFront CDN

     S3 Transfer Acceleration
       - CloudFront Edge locations are utilized by S3 transfer acceleration to reduce latency
         for S3 uploads

     Time to Live (TTL)
       - objects are cached for a period of time which is the Time to Live (TTL) time
       - the default TTL is 1 day, and when the TTL is up, the object is automatically cleared from the cache
       - you can clear an object from the cache yourself before the TTL is up, but you will be charged

---------------------------------------------------
4.11 Demo: Configuring Amazon CloudFront

   1. Create an S3 Bucket

   -> S3 -> create bucket -> name: my-cf-origin-pat
    -> ACLs disabled, unselect "Block all public access -> Create Bucket
     -> Permissions -> Bucket Policy -> Edit -> add (to add public read access):
          {
             "Id": "Policy1675093308212",
             "Version": "2012-10-17",
             "Statement": [
               {
                 "Sid": "Stmt1675093303975",
                 "Action": [
                   "s3:GetObject"
                 ],
                 "Effect": "Allow",
                 "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-cf-origin-pat/*",
                 "Principal": "*"
               }
             ]
          }
          -> Save Changes
          -> Upload cute-dog.jpg (~ 6.1MB) image


   2. Access an image from the S3 bucket

      -> my-cf-origin-pat bucket -> cute-dog.jpg -> URL


   3. Create a CloudFront Distribution

     -> go to cloudfront
        -> Create a Cloudfront distribution
        Note: previously 2 types of distribution - Web & RTMP - now just Web
        -> Origin -> Origin domain: select 'my-cf-origin-pat bucket
                  -> Origin path -> allows you to select folder in bucket
                  -> origin access: Public (default)
                  -> viewer protocol policy: Redirect HTTP to HTTPs
                  -> WAF: "Do not enable secuity protections"
                     -> Create Distribution


      -> after distribution is ready (can take 15+ min):


  Notes: CloudFront options include:
    - compress object automatically (default: yes)
    - viewer protocol policy: HTTP and HTTPS (default), Redirect HTTP to HTTPs, HTTPS
    - allow HTTP methods: GET, HEAD; GET, HEAD, OPTIONs; GET, HEAD, OPTIONs, PUT, POST,PATCH,DELETE (for read & write);
    - restrict view access (default: NO)
        - used to restrict access to CloudFront signed URLs or signe cookies
    - Cache Key and origin request includes:
       - Cache Policy (where TTL is set - default: 86400 sec [24 hrs])
       - origin request policy (where you can configure headers and cookies)
           - cloudfront can serve different versions of your object based on the HTTP/HTTPS request header
       - function associations
           - can create code to responsed to different requests
       - Price Class: Use all locations; Using only North America and Europe; Use North America, Europe, Asia,
          Middle East and Afric (where content can be serve from)
       - WAF option
       - custom SSL Certificate
       - Supported HTTP Versions: HTTP/2; HTTP/3
       - Standard logging (default: off)
       - IPv6 (default: On)



   4. Access the image using CloudFront
      - compare response time

      -> select distribution
         -> Geographic restrictions Tab -> where you can Allow/Block access to/from various countries
         -> General Tab -> copy Distribution Name
           -> in Web Browser: <Distribution Name>/cute_dog.jpg
              -> picture is loaded in web browser
           -> repeat in different Web Browser: <Distribution Name>/cute_dog.jpg
              -> loads faster

          -> go to distribution
             -> invalidation tab
                 -> use can select distribution to invalide
                    - use can invalidate individual objects, directories, everything "/*"
                       - invalidated items will no longer be cached on edge location
                       - use if item has been updated, but still in cache on edge locations

          -> go to distribution
             select distribution -> disable  -> wait for it to be disable (up to 15 min) -> then delete
              Note: cloudfront is not part of free tier


  Exam Tips:

    CloudFront
       Content Delivery Network
         - cloudfront can speed up the delivery of your static content to viewers around the world
       Origin
          - the origin of all the files that the CDN distribution will server.
          - this can be an S3 bucket, an EC2 Instance, and Elastic Load Balancer, or Route53
       Edge Location:
          - the location where content will be cached. This is seperate from AWS Regions/AZs
          - edge location are not just read only - you can write to them too (e.g., PUT an object on them).
          - objects are cached for the life of the TTL (time to live)
          - you can clear cached objects (invalidate cache), but you will be charged

       # verify it cannot be see by Object URL
          -> no longer accessible


  Exam Tips:

     Content Delivery Network
       - cloudfront can speed up the delivery of your static content to viewers around the world

     Origin Access Identity (OAI)
       - an OAI is a special CloudFront user that can access the files in our bucket and serve
         them to users
       - OAI allows us to restrict accell to the contents of our bucket, so that all users must use
         CloudFront URL instead of direct S3 URL

4.13 Understanding CloudFront AllowedMethods

   CloundFront API Reference
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cloudfront/latest/APIReference/API_AllowedMethods.html


  CloudFront AllowedMethods
    - When you create a CloudFront distribution, you need to specify which HTTP methods
      you distibution suports. Options:
        - GET, HEAD  <read only method options>
        - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS;   <read only method options> or
        - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS, PUT, POST, PATCH, DELETE  <read and write method options>

  Supported HTTTP Methods
       HTTP Method      Definition                                        Example
       ------------     ------------------------------------------        --------------
          GET           Read data; often the default method used by        Read a web page
                          HTTP clients
          HEAD          Inspect resource headers; similar to GET,          Read a web page's header
                          expect without the resouse body
          PUT           Send data to create a raws resource, or replace    Update data or change the status
                         an existing resource. Idempotent                     of a resource
          PATCH         Partially modify a resource                        Modify the contents of a shopping
                                                                             cart
          POST          Insert data; used to create or update a            Comment on a blog post
                          resource. Not Idempotent
          DELETE        Delete data                                        Remove you email address from a
                                                                             mailing list
          OPTIONS       Used to find out what other HTTP methods are       Receive a list of supported HTTP
                          supported by the given URL                          methods

  Note: Idempotent: if identical PUT method is called multiple times, it will always produce the identical result.

  Which methods should I choose to allow?
    - it depends on what you want to enable visitors to your website to do
        - if your website is read-only, then either:
             - GET, HEAD  <read only method options>
             - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS;   <read only method options> or
        - if your website is interactive (e.g, contains forms, buttons, shopping carts, etc, then:
             - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS, PUT, POST, PATCH, DELETE  <read and write method options>
           - Consider restricting access at your origin level (e.g. S3, or ECS instance) to restrict
             users from performing operations that you don't want them to (e.g. prevent users from
             deleting certain objects from your origin)

  Exam Tips

    Understanding cloudfront allowedmethods
      - 3 options to choose from
          - GET, HEAD  <read only method options>
          - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS;   <read only method options> or
          - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS, PUT, POST, PATCH, DELETE  <read and write method options>
      - Consider what users need to do on your website
      - for websites that are interactive, allow users to do anything more than read (e.g.,
        complete forms, upload data, write data, create shopping carts, etc.) you will need
        to enable the 3rd option
    CloudFront Viewer protocol policy:
      - Choose the protocol policy that you want viewers to use to access your content in CloudFront edge locations
          HTTP and HTTPS:
            - Viewers can use both protocols.
          Redirect HTTP to HTTPS:
            - Viewers can use both protocols, but HTTP requests are automatically redirected to HTTPS requests.
          HTTPS Only:
            - Viewers can only access your content if they're using HTTPS.
