4.14 Instroducing Athena

  Athena:
     - interactive query service which enable you to analyze and query data located in S3
       uinsg standard SQL (Structured Query Language)
         - serverless, nothing to provision, pay per query / per TB scanned
         - no need to set up complex Extract/Transform/Load (ETL) processes
         - works directly with data stored in S3
         - essentially, allows you to turn S3 into a database
     - can perform SQL against any number of objects, or even entire bucket paths. Its not uncommon for folks
       to perform queries on TERABYTES of data.

   Athea Uses Cases:
     Query Log Files
     - used to query log files stored in S3 (e.g. Elastic Load Balancer [ELB] logs, S3 access logs,
       cloudtrail logs, etc.)
     Perform Cost Analysis
     - Analyze AWS cost and usage reports
     Generate Business Reports
     - Generate business reports on data stored in S3
     Analyze Click-Stream data
     - Run queries on click-stream data (user click data on your website)

    Exam Tips:

      Athena Exam Tips:
        Interactive Query service
         - Athena is an interactive query service
        Standard SQL
         - It allows you to query data located in S3 using standard SQL (Structured Query Language)
        Serverless
         - Serverless - you don't need to configure any infrastructure to use Athena
        Logs
         - Commonly used to analyze log data stored in S3
         - can perform SQL against any number of objects, or even entire bucket paths. Its not uncommon for folks
           to perform queries on TERABYTES of data.


---------------------------------------------------
4.15 Demo: Working with Athena

   1 Configure a Trail in CloudTrail
     - CloudTrail will generate an audit log of all activity on our account

     -> console -> Cloudtrail -> Create Trail ->
         Trail-name: management-events, Storage location: Create new S3 bucket,
         Log file SSE-KMS: Enabled, new, AWS KMS alias: mykmskey -> Next
         Event Type: Management events -> Next -> Review -> Create trail


   2 CloudTrail Sends Logs to S3
     - Our trail will store logs it creates in a new S3 Bucket

     -> Examine new S3 bucket has sub-folders -> AWSLogs/805990902227/CloudDigest
                                                                      CloudTrail -> will be populated after ~5 min for each region

   3 Create and Athen Table
     - Use standard SQL to query the data stored in the S3 Bucket


    # create required S3 bucket to store Athena Query results (mandatory before using Athena)
      -> S3 -> Create Bucket -> myahenaresults-pat -> Create bucket

    # Athena - set up query result location
      -> Athena ->  (left tab) Query Editor -> Setting <tab> -> Manage ->  Browse S3: Bucket: myathena-results-pat -> Save

    # Athena - set up query result location
      -> Athena ->  (left tab) Query Editor ->  Editor <tab>
          Query 1: CREATE DATABASE athenadba -> Run
          click on "+"
          Query 2: <provided Athena_Query_Create_table.txt file>
              # creates at Athena table called "cloudtrail_logs" with various attributes including
                 Useridentity (includes account), event time, event source, region, etc.
                 # change last line to specify our S3 bucket
                   from: LOCATION 's3://YOUR_BUCKET_NAME/AWSLogs/YOUR_ACCOUNT_NUMBER/';
                   to: LOCATION 's3://aws-cloudtrail-logs-805990902227-52238d85/AWSLogs/805990902227/';
                  -> Run
               # refresh, then under "Tables and views" -> Tables -> new cloudtrail_logs table

          click on "+"
          Query 3: <provided Athena_Query.txt file>
                 # from cloudtrail_logs table, extract useridentity.arn, eventname, ....

  Exam Tips
     Athena
       - A query service that supports standard SQL (structured Query Language)
         - easy to configure
         - point athena to the data you want to query in S3
         - define your table schema
         - Query your data using standard SQL

4.tbd AWS Certificate Manager

  What is the AWS Certificate Manager?
    - allows you to create, manage, and deploy public and private SSL certificates for use
      with outher AWS services
    - it integrates with other services - such as Elastic Load Balancing, CloudFront Distribution,
      and API Gateway - allowing you to easily manage and deploy SSL certificates in you AWS environment

  AWS Certificate Manager Benefits
    Cost
      - no more paying for SSL certificates
      - AWS certificate Manager provisions both public and private certificates for free
      - you will still pay for the resources that utilize your certificates (such as ELB)
      - do not need to go to Verisigm, Comodo, etc and pay for certificates
    Automated Renewal and Deployment
      - Certificate Manager can automate the renewal of your SSL certificates and then automatically
        update the new certificates with ACM-integrated services, such as Elastic Load Balancing,
        CloudFront, and API Gateway
    Easier to Set up
      - Removes a lot of the manual process, such as generating a key pair or creating a certificate
        signing request (CSR)
      - you can create your own SSL certificate with just a few clicks in the Console

  Exam Tips

     AWS Certificate Manager
       Know what it is:
        - In the exam, you will get scenario-based questions around SSL certificates
          and what services you should use to integrate SSL certificates
       Supported Services
         - AWS Certificate Manager integrates with Elastic Load Balancer, CloudFront, and API Gateway
       Benefits
         - free servcie that saves time and money
         - automatically renew your SSL certificates and rotate the old certificates with new
           certificates with supported AWS services

4.16 S3 Summary

  S3 FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/s3/faqs/


  S3 (Simple Storage Service) Basics
    Object-Based
     - object based allows you to upload files
    Files up tp 5 TB
     - files can be from 0 bytes to 5 TB can be uploaded
    Unlimited storage
      - the total volume of data and the number of objects you can store is unlimited
    Not for OS or DB storage
      - not suitable to install an operating system or run a database on
    S3 Buckets
     - files are stored in buckets (similar to folders)
    Universal Namespace
      - s3 is a universal namespace - bucket names must be unique globally
      Example S3 URLs
        - bucket name
           https://<bucketname.s3.amazonaws.com/key-name  (N. Virginia)
           or
           https://<bucketname>.<awsRegion>.amazonaws.com/key-name  (other Regions)
    Uploading Files
      - HTTP 200 code if upload was successful (when using API or CLI)

   S3 Objects:
     - key (name of object)
     - value (object data)
     - version ID
     - metadata
     - subresources
        - access control lists
        - torrent


   S3 Storage classes
      - S3 Standard
        - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
        - Suitable for most workloads, e.g., websites, content distribution, mobile and gaming
          applications, and big data analytics
      - S3 IA (infrequently accessed)
        - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
        - long-term, infrequently accessed critical data, e.g. backups, data stored for disaster
          recovery, etc.
        - minimum storage duration: 30 days
      - S3 One Zone IA (infrequently accessed)
        - 99.5% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 1 AZ (multiple copies in 1 AZ)
        - long-term, infrequently accessed, non-critical data
        - minimum storage duration: 30 days
      - S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval
        - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
        - long-lived data, accessed approximately once per quarter, and needs millisecond retrieval times
        - minimum storage duration: 90 days
      - S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval
        - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
        - long-term data archiving that occasionally needs to be accessed within a few hours or minutes
        - minimum storage duration: 90 days
      - S3 Glacier Deep Archive
        - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
        - rarely accessed data archiving with a defualt retrieval time of 12 hours
        - use case: financial records for regulatory purposes.
        - minimum storage duration: 180 days

      - S3 Intelligent Tiering
        - 99.99% availability, 99.999999999% durability (11 x 9s), >= 3 AZ
        - unknown or unpredicatable access patterns.
        - minimum storage duration: 30 days
        - uses machine learning to optimize your storage costs
        - optimize costs by automatically moving data to the most cost-effective access tier

   Securing S3
     S3 Secure by Default
       - by default, all newly created buckets are private
     Control Access to Buckets
       Bucket Policies:             applied at the bucket level
       Access Control Lists (ACLs): Applied at the object level
     Access Logs
       - S3 buckets can be configured to create access logs, which log all requests made to the S3 bucket
       - can be written to another bucket or even another bucket in another account
       - not enabled/configured by default

   S3 data consistency
     - strong read after write consistency for PUTS of new objects. This behavior applies to both writes to new objects
       as well as PUT requests that overwrite existing objects and DELETE requests.
     - before, but not now: eventual consistency of overwrite PUTS and DELETES (can take time to propagate)
     - Note: Bucket configurations have an eventual consistency model. If you delete a bucket and immediately
       list all buckets, the deleted bucket might still appear in the list.


   S3 Bucket Policy
      - all newly created buckets are private by default
      - control access using bucket policy
      - must disable "Block Public" before ACL or Bucket Policy will provide public access
      - read-only bucket policy for user JohnDoe to objects that have the environment: production tag key and value:
          {
             "Version":"2012-10-17",
             "Statement":[
                {
                   "Principal":{
                      "AWS":"arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/JohnDoe"
                   },
                   "Effect":"Allow",
                   "Action":[
                      "s3:GetObject",
                      "s3:GetObjectVersion"
                   ],
                   "Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::DOC-EXAMPLE-BUCKET/*",
                   "Condition":{
                      "StringEquals":{
                         "s3:ExistingObjectTag/environment":"production"
                      }
                   }
                }
             ]
          }


   S3 Encryption
     Encryption in Transit
       - SSL/TLS
       - HTTPS
     Encryption At Rest (SSE: server side encryption):
       - SSE-S3 enabled by default
       SSE Types:
         SSE-S3 using AES 256-bit
         SSE-KMS
           - encryption keys managed in the Key Management Service
         SSE-C
           - customer manages the encryption keys
       Client-Side Encryption
         - user encrypts file and then uploads encrypted file to S3
     Enforcing Encryption with a Bucket Policy
       - explicitly deny request that do not include the x-amz-server-side-encryption parameter
         in the request header.
       - Deny requests that do not use aws:SecureTransport to enforce the uses of HTTPS/SSL


  What S3 bucket policy should I use to comply with the AWS Config rule s3-bucket-ssl-requests-only?
  -> bucket policy that denies HTTP requests (only allows HTTPS requests)
  https://repost.aws/knowledge-center/s3-bucket-policy-for-config-rule

          # denies all requests where "SecureTransport" == "false"
          {
          "Id": "ExamplePolicy",
          "Version": "2012-10-17",
          "Statement": [
            {
              "Sid": "AllowSSLRequestsOnly",
              "Action": "s3:*",
              "Effect": "Deny",
              "Resource": [
                "arn:aws:s3:::DOC-EXAMPLE-BUCKET",
                "arn:aws:s3:::DOC-EXAMPLE-BUCKET/*"
              ],
              "Condition": {
                "Bool": {                                            # Note: Bool condition!
                  "aws:SecureTransport": "false"
                }
              },
              "Principal": "*"
            }
          ]
        }


  AWS Documentation: How to Prevent Uploads of Unencrypted Objects to Amazon S3.
  -> require encryption at rest on uploaded objects
  https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/how-to-prevent-uploads-of-unencrypted-objects-to-amazon-s3/

        # below bucket policy denies uploads if "x-amz-server-side-encryption" is NULL (e.g not set)
        {
            "Version": "2012-10-17",
            "Id": "PutObjPolicy",
            "Statement": [
                {
                    "Sid": "DenyUnEncryptedObjectUploads",
                    "Effect": "Deny",
                    "Principal": "*",
                    "Action": "s3:PutObject",
                    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::bucket/*",
                    "Condition": {
                        "Null": {                                               <- condition type: NULL
                            "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption": "true"           <- if true (e.g. NULL), then deny
                        }
                    }
                }
            ]
        }


        # below bucket policy denies uploads if "x-amz-server-side-encryption" is NULL (e.g not set)
        # AND if x-amz-server-side-encryption != "AE256"
        #  Valid values to set "x-amz-server-side-encryption" are only AES256 for SSE-S3 encryption and
        #     "aws:kms" for SSE-KMS encryption
         {
             "Version": "2012-10-17",
             "Id": "PutObjPolicy",
             "Statement": [
                   {
                        "Sid": "DenyIncorrectEncryptionHeader",
                        "Effect": "Deny",                                            <- denies if condition not met
                        "Principal": "*",
                        "Action": "s3:PutObject",
                        "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::<bucket_name>/*",
                        "Condition": {
                                "StringNotEquals": {                                   <- not equal condition to "AE256"
                                       "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption": "AES256"
                                 }
                        }
                   },
                   {
                        "Sid": "DenyUnEncryptedObjectUploads",
                        "Effect": "Deny",
                        "Principal": "*",
                        "Action": "s3:PutObject",
                        "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::<bucket_name>/*",
                        "Condition": {
                                "Null": {                                             <- condition type: NULL
                                       "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption": true        <- if true (e.g. NULL), then deny
                                }
                       }
                   }
             ]
         }

   S3 Cross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS)
      - allows resources in 1 bucket to access resource (e.g. code) in another bucket
      - by default, resources in one bucket cannot access resources in a different bucket
      - a good way to organized your website
         - e.g., store image files in 1 bucket, java script in 2nd bucket, and html in another bucket

         Bucket 1:                            Bucket 2:
         myindexwebsite                       mycorstestbucket  <- enabled CORS
         index.html        ------------->     loadpage.html


  Optimizing S3 performance
    S3 prefixes
      prefix example:                                prefix
      mybucketname/folder1/subfolder1/myfile.jpg     /folder1/subfolder1

    S3 Performance
    - you can achieve a high number of requests: 3500 PUT/COPY/POST/DELETE and 5500 GET/HEAD requests per second per prefix
    - you can achieve better performance by spreading reads across different prefixes.
    - For example, if you are using 2 prefixes, you can active 11K GET/HEAD requests per second
      and 7000 PUT/COPY/POST/DELETE requests per sec

   S3 Limitations when using SSE-KMS (Server Side Encryption - Key Management Service)
     - uploading/downloading will count towards the KMS quota
     - Currently, you cannot request a quota increase for KMS
     - Region-specific quota, but it either 5500, 10k, or 30k requests per sec

   Multipart (uploads) and S3 byte-range fetches (downloads)
     - use multipart uploads to increase performance when uploading a file
     - multipart upload recommended for files over 100MB
     - multipart upload required for files over 5GB
     - use S3 Byte-range fetches to increase performance when downloading files from S3

  S3 Replication
    - originally, cross region replication (CRR), but now you can also can replicate a bucket in the same region
      (Same Region Replication: SRR)
    - you can replicate objects for one bucket to another
    - objects in existing bucket are not replicated automatically
       - however, when creating replication bucket, it now ask you if you want to replicate exist objects
    - by default, delete markers are not replicate
          - Now, it gives you an option to replicate delete markers when create replication rule
       - deleting individual versions or delete markers will not be replicated
    - SRR and CRR can be configured at the S3 bucket level, a shared prefix level, or an object level using S3 object tags
    - S3 lifecycle actions are not replicated with S3 replication

   S3 Object Ownership
     - S3 Object Ownership is an S3 bucket setting that you can use to control ownership of new objects that
       are uploaded to your buckets.
     - By default, when other AWS accounts upload objects to your bucket, the objects remain owned by the uploading account.
     - S3 Object Ownership has two settings:
         1. Object writer - The uploading account will own the object.
         2. Bucket owner preferred - The bucket owner will own the object if the object is uploaded with the
            "bucket-owner-full-control" canned ACL

   S3 controlling Access
     Customers may use four mechanisms for controlling access to Amazon S3 resources:
       1. Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies,
         - customers can grant IAM users fine-grained control to their Amazon S3 bucket or objects while
           also retaining full control over everything the users do
       2. bucket policies,
         - you can define rules which apply broadly across all requests to S3 resources, such as
           granting write privileges to a subset of Amazon S3 resources.
         - you can also restrict access based on an aspect of the request, such as HTTP referrer and IP address
       3. Access Control Lists (ACLs)
         - With ACLs, customers can grant specific permissions (i.e. READ, WRITE, FULL_CONTROL) to specific
           users for an individual bucket or object.
       4. presigned URLs (Query String Authentication)
          - you can create a URL to an Amazon S3 object which is only valid for a limited time.
          - Using query parameters to authenticate requests is useful when you want to express a
            request entirely in a URL.
          - This method is also referred as presigning a URL

   S3 Event Notification
     - The S3 notification feature enables you to receive notifications when certain events happen in your bucket.
     - To enable notifications, you must first add a notification configuration that identifies the events you want
       S3 to publish and the destinations where you want Amazon S3 to send the notifications.
       Versioning
         - If two writes are made to a single non-versioned object at the same time, it is possible that only a single
           event notification will be sent.
         - If you want to ensure that an event notification is sent for every successful write, you can enable versioning
           on your bucket. With versioning, every successful write will create a new version of your object and will also
           send event notification.

   CloudFront
     Edge Location:
        - the location where content will be cached. This is seperate from AWS Regions/AZs
        - edge location are not just read only - you can write to them too (e.g., PUT an object on them).
        - objects are cached for the life of the TTL (time to live)
        - you can clear cached objects (invalidate cache), but you will be charged
     Origin
        - the origin of all the files that the CDN distribution will server.
        - this can be an S3 bucket, an EC2 Instance, and Elastic Load Balancer, or Route53
        - You can configure a single CloudFront web distribution with multiple origins to serve different types of
          requests from multiple origins
     Distribution:
        - this is the name give to the Origin and configuration settings for the content you
          wish to distribute using CloudFront CDN

     S3 Transfer Acceleration
       - CloudFront Edge locations are utilized by S3 transfer acceleration to reduce latency
         for S3 uploads

     Time to Live (TTL)
       - objects are cached for a period of time which is the Time to Live (TTL) time
       - the default TTL is 1 day, and when the TTL is up, the object is automatically cleared from the cache
       - you can clear an object from the cache yourself before the TTL is up, but you will be charged

     Origin Access Identity (OAI)
       - an OAI is a special CloudFront user that can access the files in our bucket and serve
         them to users
       - OAI allows us to restrict accell to the contents of our bucket, so that all users must use
         CloudFront URL instead of direct S3 URL

     CloudFront CacheBehavior
       - You must create at least as many cache behaviors (including the default cache behavior) as you
         have origins if you want CloudFront to serve objects from all of the origins.
       - Each cache behavior specifies the one origin from which you want CloudFront to get objects
       - A path pattern (for example, images/*.jpg) specifies which requests you want this cache behavior to apply to.
       - When CloudFront receives an end-user request, the requested path is compared with path patterns in the order
         in which cache behaviors are listed in the distribution.

    Understanding cloudfront allowedmethods
      - 3 options to choose from
          - GET, HEAD  <read only method options>
          - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS;   <read only method options> or
          - GET, HEAD. OPTIONS, PUT, POST, PATCH, DELETE  <read and write method options>
      - Consider what users need to do on your website
      - for websites that are interactive, allow users to do anything more than read (e.g.,
        complete forms, upload data, write data, create shopping carts, etc.) you will need
        to enable the 3rd option

    CloudFront Caching
      - CloudFront can cache different versions of your content based on the values of query string parameters.
      Query string forwarding and caching options:
        None
         - Choose this option if your origin returns the same version of an object regardless of the values of query string parameters
        Forward all, cache based on whitelist option
          - should be chosen if your origin server returns different versions of your objects based on one or more
            query string parameters.
        Forward all, cache based on all
          - Choose this option if your origin server returns different versions of your objects for all query string parameters


    CloudFront Viewer protocol policy:
      - Choose the protocol policy that you want viewers to use to access your content in CloudFront edge locations
          HTTP and HTTPS:
            - Viewers can use both protocols.
          Redirect HTTP to HTTPS:
            - Viewers can use both protocols, but HTTP requests are automatically redirected to HTTPS requests.
          HTTPS Only:
            - Viewers can only access your content if they're using HTTPS.

   Certificate Management in AWS
     AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)
       - Creates and manage SSL/TLS certificates for securing your website
     Secure Connections
       - Enables secure connections to your website using HTTPS
     CloudFront
       - When using ACM with CloudFront, the certificate must be created in the us-east-1 region
     Know what it is:
      - In the exam, you will get scenario-based questions around SSL certificates
        and what services you should use to integrate SSL certificates
     Supported Services
       - AWS Certificate Manager integrates with Elastic Load Balancer, CloudFront, and API Gateway
     Benefits
       - free servcie that saves time and money
       - automatically renew your SSL certificates and rotate the old certificates with new
         certificates with supported AWS services

   CloudFront signed URLs and signed cookies
      - To create signed URLs or signed cookies, you need a signer.
     Signer:
       - A signer is either a trusted key group that you create in CloudFront, or an AWS account that
         contains a CloudFront key pair.
       - AWS recommends that you use trusted key groups with signed URLs and signed cookies
       - When you create a signer, the public key is with CloudFront and private key is used to sign a portion of URL
       - Each signer that you use to create CloudFront signed URLs or signed cookies must have a public-private key pair.
       - The signer uses its private key to sign the URL or cookies, and CloudFront uses the public key to verify the signature
       Root account
       - When you use the root user to manage CloudFront key pairs, you can only have up to two active CloudFront
         key pairs per AWS accoun
       - When you use the AWS root user, you have to use the AWS Management Console to manage CloudFront key pairs, so you
         can’t automate the process

   CloudFront Origin-request trigger to change S3 origin Region
     - With CloudFront Functions, you can write lightweight functions in JavaScript for high-scale, latency-sensitive
       CDN customizations.
     - Your functions can manipulate the requests and responses that flow through CloudFront, perform basic authentication
       and authorization, generate HTTP responses at the edge, and more
     - functions can use the value of the CloudFront-Viewer-Country header to update the S3 bucket domain name to
       a bucket in a Region that is closer to the viewer.

   Cloudfront Origin Groups
     - You can specify an origin group for your CloudFront origin if, for example, you want to configure origin
       failover for scenarios when you need high availability.
     - Use origin failover to designate a primary origin for CloudFront plus a second origin that CloudFront
       automatically switches to when the primary origin returns specific HTTP status code failure responses.
     - To get started, you create an origin group with two origins: a primary and a secondary. If the primary
       origin is unavailable, or returns specific HTTP response status codes that indicate a failure, CloudFront
       automatically switches to the secondary origin.
     - CloudFront fails over to the secondary origin only when the HTTP method of the viewer request is GET, HEAD, or OPTIONS


   Athena Exam Tips:
     Interactive Query service
      - Athena is an interactive query service
     Standard SQL
      - It allows you to query data located in S3 using standard SQL (Structured Query Language)
     Serverless
      - Serverless - you don't need to configure any infrastructure to use Athena
     Logs
      - Commonly used to analyze log data stored in S3
      - can perform SQL against any number of objects, or even entire bucket paths. Its not uncommon for folks
        to perform queries on TERABYTES of data.


  Macie:
    - uses AI to analyze data in S3 and helps identify PII (Personally Identifiable Information)
    - Can also be used to analyse CloudTrail logs for suspicious API activity
    - includes Dashboards, Reports, and Alerting
    - Great for PCI-DSS (Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard) and preventing ID theft

4.17 AWS Certified Developer - Associate - S3


Question 2

Which storage class is suitable for long-term archiving of data and supports millisecond retrieval times?
Choices:
 - Glacier Deep Archive
 - S3 Standard-Infrequent Access
 - Glacier Flexible Retrieval
 - Glacier Instant Retrieval       <- Correct Answer

S3 Standard-Infrequent Access is designed for storing long-term, infrequently accessed critical data
(e.g., backups, data store for disaster recovery files, etc.) However, it is not recommended to be used for
archiving data.

Glacier Instant Retrieval is designed for long-lived data, accessed approximately once per quarter with
millisecond retrieval time.


Question 3

You would like to configure your S3 bucket to only serve content over HTTPS/SSL and explicitly deny all unencrypted HTTP access. In your bucket policy, how should you specify the type of request that should be denied?

answer:
      {
        "Id": "ExamplePolicy",
        "Version": "2012-10-17",
        "Statement": [
          {
            "Sid": "AllowSSLRequestsOnly",
            "Action": "s3:*",
            "Effect": "Deny",
            "Resource": [
              "arn:aws:s3:::DOC-EXAMPLE-BUCKET",
              "arn:aws:s3:::DOC-EXAMPLE-BUCKET/*"
            ],
            "Condition": {
              "Bool": {
                "aws:SecureTransport": "false"
              }
            },
            "Principal": "*"
          }
        ]
      }

   answer explanation:
   Explicitly denying requests that are identified as "aws:SecureTransport": "false" would deny requests that are using HTTP
    and are unencrypted.
    AWS Documentation: What S3 bucket policy should I use to comply with the AWS Config rule s3-bucket-ssl-requests-only?
       https://repost.aws/knowledge-center/s3-bucket-policy-for-config-rule


Question 5:
What is the largest size file you can transfer to S3 using a single PUT operation?
Choices:
 - 5TB
 - 100MB
 - 1GB
 - 5GB -> Correct Answer

anwser Explaination
Individual Amazon S3 objects can range in size from a minimum of 0 bytes to a maximum of 5 terabytes. The largest object that
can be uploaded in a single PUT is 5 gigabytes. For objects larger than 100 megabytes, customers should consider using the
Multipart Upload capability.

Question 10
You would like to configure your S3 bucket to deny put object requests that do not use server-side encryption. Which
bucket policy can you use to deny permissions to upload objects, unless the request includes server-side encryption?

Correct Answer:
       ```
        {
            "Version": "2012-10-17",
            "Id": "PutObjPolicy",
            "Statement": [
                {
                    "Sid": "DenyUnEncryptedObjectUploads",
                    "Effect": "Deny",
                    "Principal": "*",
                    "Action": "s3:PutObject",
                    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::bucket/*",
                    "Condition": {
                        "Null": {
                            "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption": "true"
                        }
                    }
                }
            ]
        }
        ```
Answer Explanation:
Correct Answer
The condition above looks for a Null value for the s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption key. If this condition is true, it means
the request is Null and does not include server-side encryption. Setting the condition in the condition policy
to "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption": "true" with "Effect": "Deny" and "Action": "s3:PutObject" would deny put object
requests that do not use server-side encryption.

AWS Documentation: How to Prevent Uploads of Unencrypted Objects to Amazon S3.
  https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/how-to-prevent-uploads-of-unencrypted-objects-to-amazon-s3/


Question 16

You are using S3 in ap-northeast-1 to host a static website in a bucket called "acloudguru". What would the new URL endpoint be?

Choices:
 - http://acloudguru.s3-website-ap-northeast-1.amazonaws.com  -> Correct Answer
 - http://acloudguru.s3-website-ap-southeast-1.amazonaws.com
 - https://s3-ap-northeast-1.amazonaws.com/acloudguru/
 - http://www.acloudguru.s3-website-ap-northeast-1.amazonaws.com

Depending on your Region, your Amazon S3 website endpoint follows one of these two formats:

    s3-website dash (-) Region - http://bucket-name.s3-website-Region.amazonaws.com
    s3-website dot (.) Region - http://bucket-name.s3-website.Region.amazonaws.com

The Asia Pacific (Tokyo) region ap-northeast-1 uses the website endpoint s3-website-ap-northeast-1.amazonaws.com.
Hence, the correct URL is http://acloudguru.s3-website-ap-northeast-1.amazonaws.com.

References:

    Website endpoints
      https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/WebsiteEndpoints.html
    Amazon S3 Website Endpoints
      https://docs.aws.amazon.com/general/latest/gr/s3.html#s3_website_region_endpoints

Chapter 5 Introduction to Serverless

---------------------------------------------------
5.1 Serverless 101

  What is serverless
    - aws handles the infrastructure management tasks so that you can focus on writing code

  AWS handles the management tasks:
    - capacity provisioning
    - patching OS
    - auto scaling
    - high availability

  Serverless Competitive Advantages
    - speed to market
       - by eliminating the overhead of managing servers, you ca release code quickly and
         get your application to market sooner
    - Lower costs
      - you never pay for over-provisioning
      - serverless applications are event-driven and you are only charged when your code is executed
    - Focus on your Application
      - AWS offers a range of serverless technologies which integrate seamlessly. Allowing you to focus
        on buiding your great application

  AWS Serverless Technologies include
    Lambda
      - enables you to run code as functions without provisioning any servers
      - free tier: 1st one million requests per month are free
    SQS (Simple Queue Service)
      - a message queuing service that allows you to decouple and scale your application
    SNS (Simple Notification Service)
      - a message service for sending text messages, mobile notifications, and emails
    API Gateway
      - Allows you to create, publish, and secure APIs at any scale
    DynamoDB
      - fully managed NoSQL database
    S3
      - object storage and [static] web hosting

   Example of Serverless Architecture - the A Cloud Guru Website

               |---> (credit card payments
               |  |-> Firebase (NoSQL database - from GCP)
               |  |
               / /               -> Lambda
      ACG Website -> API Gateway -> Lambda
                \ \              -> Lambda
                |  |-> S3 (videos)
                |-> Auth0 (Authentitication service)


  Exam Tips

    Serverless 101
       Serverless
         - enables you to build scalable applications quickly without managing any servers
       Low Cost
        - serverless applications are event-driven and you are only charged when your code is executed
       AWS Handles the heavy Lifting
         - AWS handles capacity provisioning, patching OS, auto scaling, high availability
         - you can focus on writing code and builing your application instead of configuring servers

5.2 Introducing Lambda

  Lambda Documentation [Using Lambda with outher services]
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/lambda-services.html
  AWS::Lambda::Function
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/aws-resource-lambda-function.html
  Lambda@Edge
    https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/edge/



  What is Lambda
    Severless Compute
      - serverless compute service that lets you run code without provisioning or managing the underlying server
      - takes care of everything required to run your code, including the runtime environment
    Supported Languages
      - Java, Go, PowerShell, Node.js, C#, Python, and Ruby
    Enterprise Features
      - Auto-scaling and high availability are already baked-in to the Lambda service

  Lambda Pricing
    - you are charged based on the number of request, their duration, and the amount of memory used by your
      Lambda Function
    Requests
      - the 1st 1 million requests per month are free
      - $0.20 per month per 1 million requests
    Duration
      - you are charged in millisecond increments
      - the price depends on the amount of memory your allocate to your Lambda function
    Price per GB-second
      - $0.00001667 per GB-second
      - example: a funciotn that uses 512MB and runs for 100ms: 0.5GB x 0.1s = 0.05 GB-second
                                                                0.05 GB-sec x  $0.00001667 / GB-sec = $0.0000000083
       - the 1st 400K GB-sec per month are free

  Lambda is Event-Driven
     Event-Driven
       - Lambda functions can be automatically triggered by other AWS services or called directly from any
         web or mobile application
     Triggered by Events
       - these events could be changes made to the data in an S3 bucket, or DynamoDB table
     Triggered by User Requests
       - you can use API gateway to configure an HTTP endpoint allowing you to trigger your function at any
         time using an HTTP request

  Amazon Alexa and Echo
    - many alexa skills run on Lambda, and are triggered by your voice

  AWS Services that can invoke Lambda Functions
    Lambda Triggers include
      - DynamoDB
      - Kinesis
      - SQS
      - Application Load Balancer
      - API Gateway
      - Alexa
      - CloudFront
      - S3
      - SNS
      - SES
      - CloudFormation
      - CloudWatch
      - CodeCommit
      - CodePipeline
      - EventBridge


  Exam Tips

    Lambda
      Extremely Cost Effective
        - pay only when you code executes
      Continuously Scaling
        - lambda scales automatically
      Event-Driven
        - Lambda functions are triggered by an event or action (e.g. change in S3 bucket or DynamoDB table)
      Independent
        - lambda functions are independent. Each event will trigger a single function
      Serverless Technology
        - Lambda, API Gateway, DynamoDB, S3, SNS, SQS, ...
      Lambda Triggers
        - be aware of the services that can trigger a Lambda function

5.3 Create a Lambda Function Using the AWS Console


About this lab

In this hands-on lab, you'll write a Node.js Lambda function that checks a URL (e.g. www.amazon.com) and returns
the status code that signifies if the website is up and running or down. During this lab, you'll have the
opportunity to explore the Lambda console, function code, execution roles, test events, and execution results.
AWS Lambda allows you to write concise functions and only worry about your code. Since Lambda is serverless,
AWS manages the underlying infrastructure for you.

Learning objectives
  - Author Node.js Lambda Function in the AWS Console
  - Create a Test Event and Execute Lambda
  - Verify That CloudWatch Has Captured Function Logs


Author Node.js Lambda Function in the AWS Console

    In the AWS Management Console, type Lambda into the search bar at the top of the page, and then click on Lambda
    from the drop-down menu.

    In the AWS Lambda console, click Create function in the top right.

    On the Create function page, leave Author from scratch selected.

    For Function name, type in the name holURLChecker.

    Leave the other defaults, and then expand Change default execution role by clicking the arrow next to it.

    For Execution role, leave Create a new role with basic Lambda permissions selected.

    Click Create function at the bottom of the page.

    Your Lambda function should now be created.

    Scroll down to the Code source section of the page.

    Right-click on index.mjs and rename it to index.js. Now replace the function code with the code from the GitHub repository.

    Navigate to your tab with the lab GitHub open and copy the following index.js code:
    const https = require('https')
    let url = "https://www.amazon.com"

    exports.handler = function(event, context, callback) {
    https.get(url, (res) => {
          callback(null, res.statusCode)
      }).on('error', (e) => {
        callback(Error(e))
      })
    }

    Return to the Lambda function page in your other tab. Under Code source, delete the existing index.js code, and then paste in the code you just copied.

    Make sure your file is named index.js.

    Click Deploy in the Code source menu bar above it.

    You have now updated your function and deployed the changes.

Create a Test Event and Execute Lambda

    In the Code source menu bar, click the down arrow next to Test, and click Configure test event.

    In the Configure test event popup window, for the Event name, type myTestEvent.

    Leave the other default values.

    Click Save.

    You have now created your test event.

    To test the Lambda function, click Test again in the Code source menu bar.

    Review the output response.

    You should see a response code of 200, which means the website is up and running. You can also see your function logs and billed duration displayed.

    Return to the index.js tab under Code source.

    Optionally, change the URL in quotations next to let url = to different (existing or non-existing) website URLs to test out other response codes.

Verify That CloudWatch Has Captured Function Logs

    Click the Monitor tab above Code source.

    Click View logs in CloudWatch.

    This will open a new tab, taking you to the CloudWatch Managment Console.

    In the CloudWatch Management Console, click on the most recent log stream in the Log streams section.

        Note: If you tested multiple URLs in the last objective, you will see multiple log streams displayed here. If you just tested the one URL, you will just see one log stream. In that case, click the one log stream displayed.

    Notice the data below Log events, under Message.

    You should see the START and END Request Id, as well as the Billed Duration.

5.4 Understanding API Gateway

  AWS - API Gateway Structure
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/api/API_Operations.html
  Setting up a stage using the API Gateway Console
    https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/developerguide/stages.html

   What is an API
      - API stands for Application Programming Interface
      - we use APIs to interact with web applications, and applications use APIs to communicate
        with each other

   What is API Gateway
     publish,  Maintain, and Monitor APIs
       - a fully managed service that allows you to easily publish, create, maintain, monitor,
         and secure your API
     A Front Door
       - it allows you to put a safe "front door" on your application
       - allows outside world to talk to my AWS resources in a safe, secure, and monitor manner
       - allows you to be who, what, when restrictions around access to your resources
     Supported API Types
       RESTful APIs
       - RESTful APIs are optimized for stateless, serverless workloads
       Websocket APIs
         - Websocket APIs are for real-time, two-way, stateful communication, e.g., chat apps
         - In a WebSocket API, the client and the server can both send messages to each other at any time.

   RESTful APIs
     RESTfull APIs
       - REST (REpresentational State Transfer)
       - optimized for serverless and web applications
       - Stateless
     Support JSON
       - JSON (JavaScript Object Notation)
       - a notation language that uses key-value pairs

   API Gateway
     - provides a single endpoint for all client traffic interacting with the backend of your application
     What can API Gateway do?
       - it allows you to connect to applications running on Lambda, EC2, or Elastic Beanstalk and services
         like DynamoDB and Kinesis
     Supports Multiple Endpoints and Targets
       - send each API Endpoint to a different target
     Supports Multiple versions of your API
       - allows you to maintain multiple versions of your API, so you can have different versions for your
         development, testing, and production environments


   API Gateway Notable features
     Serverless
       - cost effective and scaleable
     CloudWatch
       - API Gateway logs API calls, latencies, and error rates to CloudWatch
     Throttling
       - API Gateway helps you manage traffic with throttling so that backend operations
         can withstand traffic spikes and denial of service attacks

  Exam Tips
     API Gateway
       API Gateway
         - an API is like the front door to your application
         - API gateway provides an endpoint to your applications running in AWS
       Serverless
         - low cost and scales automatically
       Throttling
         - you can throttle API Gateway to prevent your application from being overloaded
           by too many requests
       CloudWatch
         - everything is logged to CloudWatch
         - For example, API Calls, latencies, and errors are logged

5.5 Building a Serverless Website - Demo

  Architecture Overview

     Lambda
     (Run our Python Code)
                                                     User -> S3              API         Lambda
     API Gateway                                             index.html  --> Gateway --> hellocloudguru.py
     (A trigger to invoke our Lambda Function)

     S3
     (Host the website)


  Demo
    -> Console -> Lambda -> Create Function -> select "Author from scratch", name: mylambdafcn, runtime: python 3.8
       Execution Role: "Create a new role with basic Lambda permissions" (default: just allows sending logs to cloudwatch)
       -> Create Function

       -> mylambdafcn -> code tab -> replace code with

          def lambda_handler(event, context):
          print("In lambda handler")

          # writes content of "body" and returns 200 if successful
          resp = {
              "statusCode": 200,
              "headers": {
                  "Access-Control-Allow-Origin": "*",
              },
              "body": "Keep being awesome Cloud Gurus!"
          }

          return resp

          -> Deploy
          -> + Add Trigger -> Trigger Configuration: API Gateway, Intent: "Create a new API", API Type: HTTP API,
              Security: Open -> Additional Settings: select "Cross-origin resource sharing (CORS) -> Add

          -> Triggers -> API Gatewy -> click on API Endpoint"
                                     -> in new web tab, should show you the mylambdafcn body content

         # set up S3 Web site

         -> Console -> S3 -> create bucket -> Bucket Name: mywebsite-files-pat;
            Object Ownership: "ACLs enabled"; Block Public Access ...: unselect "Block all public access"
            -> Create Bucket

            # what does the provided "index.html do:
	        <html>
			<head>
				<script> <!-- starts java script -->
                                        <!-- myFunction calls API Gateway via HTTP request to the API Gateway URL -->
					function myFunction() {
						var xhttp = new XMLHttpRequest();
						xhttp.onreadystatechange = function() {
							if (this.readyState == 4 && this.status == 200) {
							document.getElementById("my-demo").innerHTML = this.responseText;
							}
						};
						xhttp.open("GET", "MY_API_GATEWAY_ENDPOINT_URL", true);
						xhttp.send();

					}

				</script>
			</head>
		<body>
                        <!-- Body section display "Hello Cloud Gurus" with a "Click me" to execute myFunction  -->
			<div align="center">
				<br>
				<br>
				<br>
				<br>
				<h1> <span id="my-demo">Hello Cloud Gurus!</span></h1>
				<button onclick="myFunction()">Click me</button>
			</div>

			</body>
		</html>
            # enable static Web site hosting for your S3 bucket


            -> select "mywebsite-files-pat" bucket -> Properties <tab> -> Static Website hosting -> Edit
               Static Website Hosting: Enable, Index Document: index.html, Error Document: error.html
               -> Save changes

            # modified provided "index.html" and replace "MY_API_GATEWAY_ENDPOINT_URL" with your Lambda API Gateway API Endpoint

            # example: from:
                xhttp.open("GET", "MY_API_GATEWAY_ENDPOINT_URL", true);
            #  To:
            	xhttp.open("GET", "https://z2xl9pk9wh.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/default/mylambdafcn", true);

            # upload files to your bucket
            -> select "mywebsite-files-pat" bucket -> Objects <tab> -> Upload -> Permissions: Predefined ACLs: "Grant public-read access"
            Add files: "error.html" "index.html" -> Upload

            # click S3 static website URL, then click on "Click me"

  Exam Tips


    Serverless Website
      Lambda
      (Run our Python Code)
                                                      User -> S3              API         Lambda
      API Gateway                                             index.html  --> Gateway --> hellocloudguru.py
      (A trigger to invoke our Lambda Function)

      S3

5.6 Version Control with Lambda - Demo


  What is $LATEST
    - When you create a Lambda function, there is only one version: $LATEST
    - When you upload a new version of the lambda code, this version will become $LATEST

  Managing multiple Versions
    Multiple Versions
      - you can create multiple versions of your function code and use aliases to reference
        the version you want to use
    Use Case
      - in a development environment, you might want to maintain a few versions of the same function
    Alias
      - an alias points to a specific version of the function code

  Lambda Aliases

       User ----> S3        --------> Lambda version 1 : Alias: Prod
                  bucket    --------> Lambda version 2 : Alias: Test

       Reference alias using Amazon Resource Name (ARN)
           arn:aws:lambd:us-east-1:<account>:function:mylambda:Test -> Version2
           arn:aws:lambd:us-east-1:<account>:function:mylambda:Prod -> Version1
           arn:aws:lambd:us-east-1:<account>:function:mylambda:$LATEST

        - if your application uses and alias, remember to update the ARN that you are using if
           you want to use the new code

  Quick Demo
    1 Create a functino
      - publish version 1, and create an alias called Prod


    -> Console -> Lambda -> Create Function -> select "Author from scratch", name: my-lambda-function, runtime: Node.js 16.x
       Execution Role: "Create a new role with basic Lambda permissions" (default: just allows sending logs to cloudwatch)
       -> Create Function

       # upload version 1
       -> my-lambda-function -> code source -> upload from -> zip file -> Version1.zip -> Save

       # test lambda function
       -> my-lambda-function ->  test -> test event action "Create new event", Event Name: te1 -> Save
       -> my-lambda-function -> code source -> click on "Test"
          -> Response should be:
          {
             "statusCode": 200,
             "body": "\"Hello from Lambda!This is version1!\""
           }
       # examine "Function ARN:
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:012345678901:function:my-lambda-function
           - if it ends with the function's name, then it is a unqualified function and defaults to $LATEST

       # publish version 1 and create an Prod alias
       -> my-lambda-function -> Action -> Publish New Version ->  Version description: Version 1 of my code -> Publish
          Version 1 has ARN
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:012345678901:function:my-lambda-function:1
           -> Action -> Create Alias -> Name: Prod, Version: 1 -> Create
           Prod Alias has ARN:
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:012345678901:function:my-lambda-function:Prod


    2 Upload Version 2
      - upload version 2, publish version 2, and create an alias called Test

       # upload version 2
       -> my-lambda-function -> code source -> upload from -> zip file -> Version2.zip -> Save
       -> my-lambda-function -> code source -> click on "Test"
          -> Response should be:
          {
             "statusCode": 200,
             "body": "\"Hello from Lambda!This is version2!\""
           }

       # examine "Function ARN:
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:012345678901:function:my-lambda-function
           - if still it ends with the function's name, so it is the unqualified function and defaults to $LATEST

       # publish version 2 and create Test alias
       -> my-lambda-function -> Action -> Publish New Version ->  Version description: Version 2 - my test code -> Publish
          Version 2 has ARN
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:012345678901:function:my-lambda-function:2
           -> Action -> Create Alias -> Name: Prod, Version: 2 -> Create
           Prod Alias has ARN:
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:012345678901:function:my-lambda-function:Test


    3 Review
      - review the ARNs and aliases that we created

       Note: Create Alias has "weighted alias" option where can shift traffic between 2 versions
       -> my-lambda-function -> aliases <tab> -> shows available aliases
       -> my-lambda-function -> Versions <tab> -> shows available versions


  Exam Tips
     Lambda Versions and Aliases
        $LATEST
          - $LATEST is always the the last version of code you upload to Lambda
        Versioning and Aliases
          - use Lambda versioning and aliases to point to your applications to a specific version if you
            don't want to use $LATEST
        Example ARNs
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:<account>:function:my-lambda-function:Prod
           arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:<account>:function:my-lambda-function:$LATEST
        Update Your Aliases
          - if your application uses an alias instead of $LATEST, remember that it will not automatically use the
            new code when you upload it

5.7 Lambda Concurrent Executions Limits


   Concurrent Executions
     - not necessary to memorize lots of limits for the exam
     - be aware that there is a concurrent execution limit for Lambda
     - Safety feature to limit the number of concurrent executions across all functions in a given
       regions per account
     - Default is 1000 concurrent executions per region, and you can request AWS support to increase this limit
     - TooManyRequestsException error message
     - HTTP Status Code: 429
     - Request throughput limit exceeded

   Concurrent Executions
     - if you have many Lambda functions running in the same region and you suddenly start seeing
       new invocation requests being rejected, then you may nave hit your limit
     - At ACG, our daily uses is ~6.5M lambda invocations per day in us-east01
     - request an increase on this limit by submitting a request to the AWS Support Center
     - Alternatively, you can configure Reserved concurrency. This guarantees that a set number of executions
       which will always be available for your most critical lambda function, however this also acts as a limit
         - if you set a reserved concurrency on a specific lambda function, then that lambda function will not
           be able to exceed the concurrent executions above the specified reserved concurrently limit

  Exam Tips

      Lambda Concurrent Execution Limit
         - know that a limit exists - default: 1000 concurrent executions per second per regions
         - if you hit the limit you will start to see invocations being rejected - 429 HTTP status code
         - the remedy is get limit raised by AWS Support
         - Alternatively, you can configure Reserved concurrency. This guarantees that a set number of executions
           which will always be available for your most critical lambda function, however this also acts as a limit

5.8 Lambda and VPC Access - Demo

  Lambda and VPC Access - Example Use Case
    - Lambda function needs to access resources in a private VPC
      - examples:
          - read or write data to an RDS database
          - shut down an EC2 instance in automated response to a security alert
    - to enable lambda VPC access, you need to allow the function to connect
      to the private VPC

                  Private VPC
                |------------------|
                |  Private Subnet  |
      lambda -> | |--------------| |
                | | EC2    RDS   | |
                | |--------------| |
                |  Security Group  |
                |------------------|

  VPC Configuration Information
    - need to provide these 3 configuration items to your lambda function
    VPC
      - specify the VPC that your function needs to access
    Subnet
      - select the subnets that Lambda will use
    Security Group
      - choose the security groups that enable the required access

  Walkthrough - Demo

   # create lambda function
   -> console -> Lambda -> Create Function -> "Auther from scatch", Function Name: vpc-demo -> create function

   # configure lambda function's role to have VPC Access permissions
   -> console -> Lambda -> select "vpc-demo" function -> Configuration <tab> -> Permissions <left tab>
      -> Under Execution Role, select Role Name "vpc-demo-role-xxxx
      -> Add Permissions -> Attach Policies -> search for "VPCAccess" add "AWSLambdaVPCAccessExecutionRole" -> Add Permissions

      "AWSLambdaVPCAccessExecutionRole" policy:
	    {
	        "Version": "2012-10-17",
	        "Statement": [
	            {
	                "Effect": "Allow",
	                "Action": [
	                    "logs:CreateLogGroup",
	                    "logs:CreateLogStream",
	                    "logs:PutLogEvents",
	                    "ec2:CreateNetworkInterface",
	                    "ec2:DescribeNetworkInterfaces",
	                    "ec2:DeleteNetworkInterface",
	                    "ec2:AssignPrivateIpAddresses",
	                    "ec2:UnassignPrivateIpAddresses"
	                ],
	                "Resource": "*"
	            }
	        ]
	    }

   # Configurate Lambda with VPC, Subnet, Security
   -> console -> Lambda -> select "vpc-demo" function -> Configuration <tab> -> VPC <left tab> -> Edit
      VPC: select VPC, Subnet: select subnets, Security Group: select SG (that has appropriate permissions, e.g.
        allows RDS connections)

  Exam Tips

    Lambda and VPC Access
      Enable Lambda to Access VPC resources
        - it is possible to enable lambda to access resources that are inside a private VPC
      VPC Configuration Information
        - need to provide the Lambda function the VPC ID, private subnet IDs, and Security Group ID(s)
      Network Access
        - Lambda creates ENI (Elastic Network Interfaces) using IPs from the private subnets.
        - need to add "AWSLambdaVPCAccessExecutionRole" policy to the Lambda Function's Role so it can
          create the ENI
        - The security group needs to allow your function to access the resources in the VPC

---------------------------------------------------
5.9 Example Serverless Architectures


  Characteristics of Event-Driven Architecture
    Asynchronous
      - Events and asynchronous communication used to loosely couple application components
      - That is, and event or message might trigger an action, but no response is expected or required
    Loosely Coupled
      - Services and components operation and scale independently of each other
    Single Purpose Functions
      - stateless functions performing a short-lived task

  Characteristics of Event-Driven Architecture - building applications
    AWS Services
      - Used as building blocks for applications
    Minimal Maintenance
      - Many AWS services provide inherent scalability, performance, and reliability
    Best Practices
      - Avoid writing extra code to do something that AWS already offers as a service

  Characteristics of Event-Driven Architecture - types of components
    Event Source:
       e.g. a object is upload to S3; a DynamoDB table is updated
    Event Router (between Event Source and Event Distination)
      - EventBridge to coordinate activities (e.g. running multiple lambda functions)
    Event Destination:
       - SNS, Lambda

  Banking Application
                                                                                      --> Lambda        SQS
                                                                                      |   Overdraft  -> Offer
     Customer     API         Lambda fcn                  DynamoDB                    |   Increase      Increase
     withdraw  -> Gateway  -> to process withdrawal  ->   alert due     -> EventBridge                  Overdraft
     request                  and update DB table         to overdraft                |
                                                                                      |--> Lambda         SNS
                                                                                           Overdraft   -> Phone notification
                                                                                           Notification   using overdraft
  Image Processing
                                      DynamoDB
                                  |-> Store Image
                                  |   Metadata
     S3           Lambda fcn      |
     Image    --> To process     -|
     Upload       resize image    |   S3                       CloudFront
                                  |-> ProcessedImages   <----- All procesesed images
                                                               are served by CF

  Processing Streaming Data from Social Media Events

      Social   in real-time     Kinesis        Lambda Fcn    places       DynamoDB
      Media   ----------------> Firehose  ---> Processes    ------------> Table
      Events   loaded time                     image data    result


  Note:
      Eventbridge (formally CloudWatch Events)
        - is a serverless event bus that was built on top of the existing AWS CloudWatch Events API
        - enables developers to integrate many of the AWS services through events.
           - For example, if there is a change in the state of an EC2 instance, a CloudWatch Event can trigger an event to
             AWS Lambda to take an action.
        - EventBridge extends this functionality beyond the AWS ecosystem, letting you brings together your own (legacy)
          applications, SaaS (Software-as-a-Service), and AWS Services.
        - It can stream real-time data from various event sources like PagerDuty, Symantec, and routes to various targets
          (AWS services) like SQS, Lambda, and others.


  Exam Tips

    Example of Serverless Architectures
      Event Driven and Asynchronous
        - An event or message might trigger an action, but no response is expected or required
      Build Blocks
        - Think of AWS services as building blocks that can be integrated together to create
          an application
      Event-driven architecutres are Loosely Coupled
        - SQS allows you to queue messages.
        - EventBridge help you handle events and route them to application components
      Flexibility and Scalability
        - Services and components operate and scale independently of each other

---------------------------------------------------
5.10 Step Function Demo

  Step Functions FAQs
    https://aws.amazon.com/step-functions/faqs/

  Overview of Step Functions
    - Provide a visual interface for serverless applications, which enables you to build, and run
      serverlesss applications as a series of steps
    - Each step in your application executes in order, as defined by your business logic
    - The output from one step can be the input to the next step

  Orchestration for Serverless Applications
     - manage the logic of your application
     - including sequencing, error handling, and retry logic, so your application executes in order,
       and as expected
     - Step functions also log the state of each step, so when thing do go wrong, you can diagnose
       and debug problems quickly

  Sequential Workflows
   - step happen sequentially
   - output from one step (task) is the input to the next step

                 Lambda fcn1     Lambda Fcn2     Lamdba Fcn3     Lambda Fcn4
                 Decode          Generate        Remove          Tokenize
      Start  --> base64     ---> Statistics  --> Special     --> and Count   --->  End
                 string                          Characters

  Parallel Workflows (steps)
                                Lambda fcn B1
                            |-> Extract Metadata   -->|
                            |                         |
               Lambda fcn A |   Lambda Fcn B2         |     Lambda Fcn C
     Start --> Process      |-> Resize Image       -->| --> Load in Database -> End
               Photo        |                         |
                            |   Lambda Fcn B3         |
                            |-> Facial Recognition -->|

  Branching Workflows
                                                                      Lambda 4A
                                                                 |--> Notify     -->|
                 Lambda fcn1     Lambda Fcn2     Lamdba Fcn3     |    Success       |
                 Trigger         Get Test        All Tests       |                  | --> End
      Start  --> CodeBuild  ---> Results     --> Passed?     --> |    Lambda 4B     |
                 build                                           |--> Notify     -->|
                                                                      Failure

  Demo - Sample Project

    -> console ->  Step Functions -> (open left menu) State machines -> Create state machine ->
       -> Choose authorizing method: "Run a sample Project", select "Job Poller"

       in Definintion:
          -> includes state machine language used
          -> includes workflow diagram

          -> Next -> Deploy resources

              -> will use cloudformation to deploy resources - takes ~10min
              # after it is deployed


              -> select "open in new brower tab" ->  Start execution


       Notes:
          State machines are defined using Amazon States Language
           https://docs.aws.amazon.com/step-functions/latest/dg/concepts-amazon-states-language.html
           -> includes state diagram showing progress of out job
           -> click on task in diagram, and it shows related code in "step detail"
              - inputs & outputs
           -> Under "Events", it shows the event history and lambda functions being scheduled


  Exam Tips

    Step Functions
      Visualize
        - Great way to visualized your serverless application
      Automated
        - Step functions automatically trigger and track each step.
        - The output of one step is often the input to the next step
      Logging
        - Step functions log the state of each step, so if something goes wrong you can track
          what went wrong, and where.
