6.8 DynamoDB Provisioned Throughput

    DynamoDB Provisioned Throughput - Measured Capacity Units
      Specify Requirements
        - When you create your table, you can specify your requirements in terms of read capacity
          and write capacity units
      Write Capacity Units
        - 1 x write capacity unit = 1 x 1KB write per second
      Read Capacity Units
        - 1 x read capacity unit = 1 x strongly consistent read of 4KB per second
        OR
        - 1 x read capacity unit = 2 x eventually consistent read of 4KB per second (default)

    DynamoDB Example Configuration
      - if you configure 5 x read capacity units and 5 x write capacity units, then:
           5 x 4KB strongly consistent reads           = 20KB read per second
           OR Twice as many eventually consistent read = 40KB read per sec
           AND  5 x 1KB writes                         = 5KB writes per second

    Strongly Consistent Read Capacity Challenge
      requirements
        - your application needs to read 80 items per second
        - each item is 3KB is size
        - strongly consistent reads
        ---------------------

    First, calculate how many 4KB read capacity units are needed for each read:
      - calculate the size of each item / 4KB
      - 3KB / 4KB = 0.75
      - Round-up to the nearest whole number, each read operation will need 1 x read capacity unit
      - multiply by the number read operations per second = 80 read capacity units required
        for strongly consistent reads

    Eventually Consistent Read Capacity Challenge
        1/2 strongly consistent read = 40 read capacity units

    Write Capacity Challenge
      requirements
        - your application needs to write 100 items per second
        - each item is 512B is size

    First, calculate how many 1KB write capacity units are needed for each write:
      - calculate the size of each item / 1KB
      - 0.5KB / 1KB = 0.5
      - Round-up to the nearest whole number, each write operation will need 1 x read capacity unit
      - multiply by the number write operations per second = 100 write capacity units required

  Exam Tips

     DynamoDB Provisioned Throughput
       - provisioned throughput is measured in capacity units
       Write Capacity Units
         - 1 x 1KB per second
       Strongly Consistent Read Capacity Units
         - 1 x 4KB per second
       Eventually Consistent Read Capacity Units (default)
         - 2 x 4KB per second

---------------------------------------------------
6.9 DynamoDB On-Demand Capacity

  DynamoDB On-Demand Capacity
    - Charges apply for reading, writing, and storing data
    - instantly scales up and down based on the activity of your application
      (rather than you specify a set amount of read/write capacity)
    Great for:
      - unpredicable workloads
      - new applications where you don't know the use pattern yet
      - when you want to pay for only what you use (pay per request)

  Which Pricing model should you use?
    On-Demand Capacity
      - unknown workloads
      - unpredictable application traffic
      - spiky, short-lived peaks
      - a pay-per-use model is desired
      - it might be more difficult to predict the cost
    Provisioned Capacity
      - read and write capacity requirements can be forecasted
      - predicatable application traffic
      - Application traffic is consistent or increases gradually
      - You have more control over the cost

  Exam Tips

     DynanoDB On-Demand Capacity vs Provisioned Capacity
       Understand the difference
         - know when to use each pricing model
       On-Demand Capacity
         - unpredictable application traffic
         - pay-per-use model
       Provisioned Capacity
         - read and write capacity requirements can be forecasted
         - Application traffic is consistent or increases gradually
          - where you want to have more control over the cost

---------------------------------------------------
6.10 DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX)

  DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX)
    - a fully managed, cluster in-memory cache for DynamoDB
    - delivers up to a 10X read peformance improvement
    - microsecond performance for millions of requests per second
    - ideal for read-heavy and bursty workloads like auction applications, gaming, and retail sites
      during Black Friday promotions

  How does DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX) work?
    - a write-through caching service
    - data is written to the cache and the backend store at the same time
    - allows you to point your DynamoDB API calls at the DAX cluster
    - if the item you are querying for is in the cache (cache hit), DAX returns the result
    - if the item is not available (cache miss), then DAX performs an eventually consistent
      GetItem operation against DynamoDB and returns the result of the API call and
      writes it to the DAX cache

  Benefits of DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX)
    - reduces the read load on DynamoDB tables
    - may be able to reduce the provisioned read capacity on your table and save money
      on your AWS bill

  When is DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX) not suitable?
    - Caters for eventually consistent reads only
    - not suitable for:
          - applications that require strongly consistent reads
          - applications which are mainly write-intensive
          - applications that do not perform many read operations
          - applications that do not require microsecond response times

  Exam Tips

  DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX)
    In-Memory Cache
      - provides in-memory caching for DynamoDB tables
    Improves response
      - improves response times for eventually conistent reads only
    Write Operations
      - Data is written to the cache and backend store at the same time
    API Calls
      - point your API calls at the DAX Cluster instead of your DynamoDB table
    Cache Hit
      - if the item you are querying is in the DAX Cache, DAX will return it
    Not Suitable
       - not suitable for write-intensive applications, or applications that require strongly
         consistent reads

---------------------------------------------------
6.11 DynamoDB TTL - Demo

  DynamoDB Time To Live (TTL)
    - defines an expiry time for your data
    - expired items marked for deletion
    - great for removing irrelevant or old data (e.g. session data, event logs, and temporary data)
    - reduces the cost of your table by automatically removing data which no longer relevant

  DynamoDB TTL - Epoch time
     - DynanmoDB TTL is expressed in Epoch Time (aka UNIX time, or POSIX Time)
     - the number of seconds that passed since 12 am on Jan 1, 1970
     - Epoch Time converter website
       https://www.epochconverter.com/
     - when TTL time expires (TTL Epoch time < Epoch CurrentTime), the item will be expired and marked
       for deletion in the next 48 hours. Youc can filter out expired items from your queries and scans

   Demo

     -> in cloudshell

       # determine user
       $  aws iam get-user
       -> console -> IAM -> check users dynamoDB permissions -> if needed, add "AmazonDynamoDBFullAccess"

       # create dynamodb SessionData table with AttributeName: UserID, AttributeType: Number:
       #   PrimaryKey: UserID, keyType=HASH  (Note: HASH means primary key)
       aws dynamodb create-table --table-name SessionData --attribute-definitions \
       AttributeName=UserID,AttributeType=N --key-schema \
       AttributeName=UserID,KeyType=HASH \
       --provisioned-throughput ReadCapacityUnits=5,WriteCapacityUnits=5

       # upload table items file after downloading from lesson resources
       Actions -> Upload file -> items.json

        # dynamodb table with items in "items.json" file
        $ aws dynamodb batch-write-item --request-items file://items.json

        -> console -> DynamoDB -> tables -> SessionData -> verify 3 items in table
           -> update TTL epoch time in 3 items to some time after current time


        -> console -> DynamoDB -> tables -> SessionData -> Action -> Turn on TTL (before managed ttl?)
           TTL attribute Name: ExpirationTime -> Run Preview (test some times)
           -> Turn on TTL

           -> Additionl setting <tab> -> Time to Live -> shows enabled, etc.

  Exam Tips

    DynamoDB Time To Live (TTL)
      TTL
        - defines an expiry time for your data. Once expired, and item is marked for deletion
      Use Cases
        - great for removing irrelevant or old data (e.g. session data, event logs, and temporary data)
      Helps You Save Money
        - reduces the cost of your table by automatically removing data which is no longer relevant

---------------------------------------------------
6.12 DynamoDB Streams


  DynamoDB Streams
    Time Ordered Sequence
      - time-ordered sequence of item-level modifications in a table (e.g., insert, update, delete)
        - first-in, first-out streams of your data
    logs
     - DynamoDB streams are encrypted at rest and stored for 24 hour
    Dedicated Endpoints
      - Accessed using a dedicated endpoint
    Primary Key
       - by default, the primary key or the item being modified, upated, or deleted is recorded
         (min amount of data that can be recorded)
    Images
      - Before and after images can be captured
    Use Cases:
      - Audit or archive transactions, trigger an event based a particular transaction, or replicate
        data across multiple tables


  DynamoDB Streams Overview
                           AWS SDK
                          |---------|
                          |DynamoDB |      DynamoDB
                    |---> |API      |----> Endpoint
                    |     |         |
        Application |     |         |
                    |     |DynamoDB |      DynamoDB
                    |---> |Streams  |----> Streams   ---> Event data held for 24 hrs
                          |API      |      Endpoint
                          |---------|

  DynamoDB Streams - An Event Source For Lambda
    Near Real-Time
      - Applications can take actioins based on content of the stream
      - A DynamoDB stream can be an event source for Lambda
      - Lambda polls the DynamoDB stream and executes based on an event


  Using DynamoDB Streams to Trigger a Workflow
    - an invoice and payment system which recording invoice data into a DynamoDB table
    - each time a new invoice entry is made in the DynamoDB table, there is a Lambda function
      which reads the DynamoDB string to check for new transactions. When it sees a new event,
      it is sending a notifcation via SNs which creates a message in the SQS queue.
      Payment application polls SQS and processes the message and generates a payment request
      or adds a item to a customer bill

        |- Lambda Fcn  ---->  SNS ---> SQS <--- payment application ---> Payment request
        v
      DynamoDB
      Streams
      endpoint

  Exam Tips

    DynamoDB Streams
      Time Ordered Sequence of Modifications
        - time-ordered sequence of item-level modifications in a DynamoDB tables (e.g., insert, update, delete)
      Encrypted and Stored
        - Data is stored for 24 hours only
      Lambda Event Source
        - can be used as an event source for Lambda, so you can create applications that take
          actions based on events in DynamoDB table

---------------------------------------------------
6.13 Provisioned Throughput Exceeded and Exponential Backoff


  Provisioned Throughput Exceeded
    ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
      - your request rate exceeds the read / write capacity provisioned on your DynamoDB table
    Using the AWS SDK
      - the SDK will automatically retry the requests until successful
    Not Using the AWS SDK
      - reduce your concurrent request frequency; or
      - implement exponential backoff yourself

  Exponential Backoff
    Overloaded Components
      - in addition to simple retries, all AWS SDKs use exponential backoff
      - Uses progressively longer waits between consective retries, for improved flow control
      - example:

        succesful -> ... ->    200 ms -> 2nd -> 100 ms ->  1st  -> 50 ms ->  failed -> DynamoDB
         Request                         retry              retry             request   Table

      - if after 1 minute this doesn't work, your request size may be exceeding the throughput for
        your read/write capacity

  Exam Tips

   Provisioned Throughput Exceeded and Exponential Backoff
    Provisioned Throughput Exceeded
      ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
        - error means the number of read or write requests is too high
      Exponential Backoff Improves Flow Control
        - Exponential backoff improves flow by retrying requests using progressively long waits
        - if after 1 minute this doesn't work, your request size may be exceeding the throughput for
          your read/write capacity
      Every AWS SDK
        - Expontential backoff is a feature of every AWS SDK and applies to many services within
          AWS (e.g., S3 Buckets, CloudFormation, SES)
      Not Using the AWS SDK
        - need to handle it yourself - for example, reduce your concurrent request frequency;
        - implement exponential backoff yourself

---------------------------------------------------
6.14 DynamoDB Summary

  DynamoDB
    What is DynamoDB?
     - a low latency NoSQL database
    Data Models
      - supports both document and key-value data models
      - Supported document formats are JSON, HTML, and XML
    Consistency Models
      Eventually consistent reads (by default)
        - consistency across all copies of the reached within a second
        - repeating a a read after a short time should return the updated data
        - Best read peformance
      Strongly consistent reads
        - returns a result that reflects all writes that receive a successful response
          prior to the read
        - Writes are reflected across all 3 locations at once
        - Best for read consistency
      ACID Transactions
        - DynamoDB Transactions provide the ability to perform ACID Transactions (Atomic,
          Consistent, Isolated, Durable)
        - ACID basically means all or nothing
          - All: transaction succeeds across 1 or more tables
          - Nothing: whole transaction fails

    Note: High Cardinality: high cardinality refers to a column that can have many possible values.

    DynamoDB Features
      - consists of tables, items (rows or records in database), and attributes (columns in the table)
      2 Types of Primary Keys
         - must be unique identifier of your data item in dynamoDB table
         - each primary key attribute must be defined as type string, number, or binary
           Note: Your applications must encode binary values in base64-encoded format before sending them to DynamoDB
         Partition Key
           - based on a unique attribute
           - e.g. customer_ID, product_ID, email address, vehicle registration number
         Composite Key (partition key + sort key)
            - used when partition key is not unique in your table
            - example - Formum Posts where uses post multiple message so user id alone is not unique
                 Combination of:
                    - User_ID
                    - Sort key (post timestamp)


   DynamoDB Access Control
     - Fine-Grained Access Control with IAM
     IAM Condition parameter
       - dynambodb:LeadingKeys allows user to access only the items where the partition
         key value matches their User_ID

     DynamoDB example policy

       {

         "Version": "2012-10-17",
         "Statement": [
           {
            "Sid" : "AllowAccessToOnlyItemsMatchingUserId",        <---- Statement Identifier
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "dynamodb:GetItem",
                "dynamodb:PutItem",                                 <---- Defines the action that
                "dynamodb:UpdateItem"                                     the policy allows
            ],
            "Resource": ["arn:aws:dynamodb:us-east-1:*:*:table/HighScores"],
            "Condition": {
                "ForAllValues:StringEquals": {                             Allows user to access only
                    "dynamodb:LeadingKeys": [                        <---- the items where the partition
                        "${www.mygame.com:user_id-name-3}"                 key value matches their user ID
                    ],
                    "dynamodb:Attributes": [
                       "UserId",
                       "GameTitle",                                   <---- Defines the attributes that
                       "TopScore",                                          that the policy applies to
                       "TopScoreDateTime"
                     ]
                  },
                  "StringEqualsIfExists": {
                  "dynamodb:Select": "SPECIFIC_ATTRIBUTES"
                  }
                  }
           }
           ]
           }

  DynamoDb Secondary Indexes
   - index enable faster queries on specific data columns
   - give you a different view of your data based on the alternative paritition / sort key
   Local Secondary Index
     - same partition key and different sort key to your table
     - must be created when you create your table
     - use the RCU and WCU of the main table, so you can't provision more RCU and WCU to the LSI
   Global Secondary Index
     - different partition key and different sort to your table
     - can be created at any time
     - the provisioned write capacity for a global secondary index should be equal or greater than the write
       capacity of the base table since new updates will write to both the base table and global secondary index

   DynamoDB Scan vs Query
     - For queries and scans, use the "ProjectExpression" (--project-expression) parameter to refine the results
       - also can use filter to refine your results
     DynamoDB Filtering:
       KeyConditionExpresion (--key-condition-expression):
         - filtering based on partion key and sort key attributes
       FilterExpresion (--filter-expression):
         - filtering results of query based on attributes of non-partition and non-sort key attributes
       ProjectionExpression (--projection-expression)
         - filtering attributes from the results
     DynamoDB Scan
       - a scan operation examines every item in the table and by default returns all data attributes
     DynamoDB Query
        - find items in a table
        - using only the primary key attribute, you provide the primary key name and a distinct
          value to search for
        Query  Results
          Sort Key
            - sorted by in ascending order by the sort key, if there is one
          Reverse Order
            - set "ScanIndexForward" parameter to "false" to reverse the order

     Increase performance of query or scan
       - reduce the impact of a query or scan by setting a smaller page size
       - isolate scan operations to specific tables and segregate them form your mission-critical traffic
       - try parallel scans rather than the default sequential scans
       - A query operation is generally more efficient than a scan
       - design tables in a way that you can use "Query", "Get" or "BatchGetItem" APIs


   DynamoDB Provisioned Throughput
     - provisioned throughput is measured in capacity units
     Write Capacity Units
       - 1 x 1KB per second
     Strongly Consistent Read Capacity Units
       - 1 x 4KB per second
     Eventually Consistent Read Capacity Units (default)
       - 2 x 4KB per second

   DynanoDB On-Demand Capacity vs Provisioned Capacity
     Understand the difference
       - know when to use each pricing model
     On-Demand Capacity
       - unpredictable application traffic
       - pay-per-use model
     Provisioned Capacity
       - read and write capacity requirements can be forecasted
       - Application traffic is consistent or increases gradually
       - where you want to have more control over the cost

  DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX)
    In-Memory Cache
      - provides in-memory caching for DynamoDB tables
    Improves response
      - improves response times for eventually conistent reads only
    Write Operations
      - Data is written to the cache and backend store at the same time
    API Calls
      - point your API calls at the DAX Cluster instead of your DynamoDB table
    Cache Hit
      - if the item you are querying is in the DAX Cache, DAX will return it
    Not Suitable
       - not suitable for write-intensive applications, or applications that require strongly
         consistent reads

  DynamoDB Time To Live (TTL)
    TTL
      - defines an expiry time for your data. Once expired, and item is marked for automatic deletion
    Use Cases
      - great for removing irrelevant or old data (e.g. session data, event logs, and temporary data)
    Helps You Save Money
      - reduces the cost of your table by automatically removing data which is no longer relevant


  DynamoDB Streams
    Time Ordered Sequence of Modifications
      - time-ordered sequence of item-level modifications in a DynamoDB tables (e.g., insert, update, delete)
    Encrypted and Stored
      - Data is stored for 24 hours only
    Lambda Event Source
      - can be used as an event source for Lambda, so you can create applications that take
        actions based on events in DynamoDB table

  DynamoDB Global Tables
   Managed multi-master, multi-region replication
     - great for globally distributed applications
     - can reduce latency for global users by reducing the distance between the client and the DynamoDB endpoint
     - based on DynamoDB streams
     - need dynamoDB streams to be turned on to enable Global Tables
     - multi-region redundancy for disaster recovery or high availability
     - built-in to DynamoDB, no application rewrite needed
     - replication latency under 1 sec in most cases

  [DynamoDB] Provisioned Throughput Exceeded and Exponential Backoff
   Provisioned Throughput Exceeded
     ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
       - error means the number of read or write requests is too high
     Expontential Back improves Flow Control
       - Exponential backoff improves flow by retrying requests using progressively long waits
       - if after 1 minute this doesn't work, your request size may be exceeding the throughput for
         your read/write capacity
     Every AWS SDK
       - Expontential backoff is a feature of every AWS SDK and applies to many services within
         AWS (e.g., S3 Buckets, CloudFormation, SES)
     Not Using the AWS SDK
       - need to handle it yourself - for example, reduce your concurrent request frequency;
       - implement exponential backoff yourself


   DynamoDB CLI Commands
     - know how to use each of the common commands listed in this lesson
     - Understand that the CLI commands are making calls to a DynamoDB API
     - the correct IAM permissions are required to make the API call
        e.g.  User --> get-item
                       GetItem   -> requires IAM permissions


     AWS CLI           Coresponding
     Command           API Call                  Usage
     --------------    -----------    ------------------------------------------------------------------
     create-table      CreateTable    Create a new table
     put-item          PutItem        Adds a new item into a table or replaces an old item with a new one
     get-item          GetItem        Returns a set of attributes for an item with the given primary key
     batch-get-item    BatchGetItem   Returns the attributes of one or more items from one or more tables.
                                      You identify requested items by primary key
     update-item       UpdateItem     Allows you to edit the attributes of an existing item (e.g., add or delete attributes)
     batch-write-item  BatchWriteItem Puts or deletes multiple items in one or more tables.
     update-table      UpdateTable    Used to modify a table (e.g. modify the provisioned throughput settings of the table)
     list-tables       ListTables     Returns a list of tables in your account
     describe-table    DescribeTable  Returns information about the table (e.g. current status, creation data,
                                      primary key, and indexes)
     scan              Scan           Reads every item in a table and returns all items and attributes
                                      Use a FilterExpression to return fewer items
     query             Query          Queries the table based on a partition key value that you provide
     delete-item       DeleteItem     Allows you to delete an item based on its primary key
     delete-table      DeleteTable    Deletes a table including all of its items


   DynamoDB Conditional Writes
     - By default, the DynamoDB write operations (PutItem, UpdateItem, DeleteItem) are unconditional: Each operation
       overwrites an existing item that has the specified primary key.
     - DynamoDB optionally supports conditional writes for these operations. A conditional write succeeds only if the
       item attributes meet one or more expected conditions. Otherwise, it returns an error
     - example API:
         aws dynamodb update-item \
            --table-name ProductCatalog \
            --key '{"Id":{"N":"1"}}' \
            --update-expression "SET Price = :newval" \
             --condition-expression "Price = :currval" \
            --expression-attribute-values file://expression-attribute-values.json

            where expression-attribute-values.json file contains
                {
                    ":newval":{"N":"8"},
                    ":currval":{"N":"10"}
                }
  DynamoDB Export to S3
    - The DynamoDB Export to S3 feature is the easiest way to create backups that you can download locally
       or use with another AWS service.
    - To customize the process of creating backups, you can use Amazon EMR or AWS Glue.
    options:
      - Use AWS Data Pipeline to export your table to an S3 bucket in the account of your choice and download locally
      - Use Hive with Amazon EMR to export your data to an S3 bucket and download locally
      - Use AWS Glue to copy your table to Amazon S3 and download locally

     Note:
       AWS Data Pipeline
         - a managed Extract, Transform, Load (ETL) service for automating movement and transformation of your data

6.15 AWS Certified Developer - Associate - DynamoDB Quiz

Question 11

Your application is storing customer order data in a DynamoDB table. You need to run a query to find all the orders
placed by a specific customer in the last month. Which attributes would you use in your query?

Choices:
 - The Partition Key of OrderDate and Sort Key of CustomerName
 - The Partition Key of CustomerID and a Sort Key of OrderDate  <- Correct Answer
 - The Partition Key of OrderDate and a Sort Key of CustomerID
 - A composite Primary Key made up of the CustomerName and OrderDate

CustomerID is the best choice as it will be a unique value. To identify all the orders placed in the last month by a
customer, you should use OrderDate as the Sort Key. The Partition Key of CustomerID would also be more unique than CustomerName.


Question 12

What is the API call to retrieve multiple items from a DynamoDB table?

Choices
 - BatchGetItems
 - GetItems
 - BatchGet
 - BatchGetItem  <- Correct Answer

This is an invalid DynamoDB API call. Reference: Amazon DynamoDB API Reference
Correct Answer

The BatchGetItem operation returns the attributes of one or more items from one or more tables.
Reference: Amazon DynamoDB API Reference
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/APIReference/API_Operations.html


Question 15
What does the error "ProvisionedThroughputExceededException" mean in DynamoDB?

choices:
 - The DynamoDB table is unavailable.
 - You exceeded your maximum allowed provisioned throughput for a table or for one or more global secondary indexes. <- Correct Answer
 - The DynamoDB table has exceeded the allocated space.
 - Your EC2 instance has run out of CPU or memory

This error means that you have exceeded your maximum allowed provisioned throughput for a table or for one or more
global secondary indexes.


Question 20

Using the AWS Console, you are trying to scale DynamoDB past its pre-configured maximums. Which service limits can you
increase by raising a ticket to AWS support?

Choose 2
Choices:
 - Local Secondary Indexes
 - Global Secondary Indexes per table  <- Correct Answer
 - Item Sizes
 - Provisioned throughput limits  <- Correct Answer


There is an initial quota of 20 global secondary indexes per table. To request a service quota increase,
see https://aws.amazon.com/support.
Reference: Service, Account, and Table Quotas in Amazon DynamoDB
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Limits.html

AWS places some default quotas on the throughput you can provision. These are the quotas unless you request a higher amount. To request a service quota increase, see https://aws.amazon.com/support.
Reference: Service, Account, and Table Quotas in Amazon DynamoDB
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Limits.html

Question 26

In order to address performance considerations and achieve faster response times, which of the following best practices can be used for Querying and Scanning Data in Amazon DynamoDB large tables?

Choose 2
 - Reduce the page size to return fewer items per results page.   <- Correct Answer
 - Scan using the 'FilterExpression' parameter to discard items that you do not want to appear in the results.
 - Set your queries to be eventually consistent.
 - Run parallel scans if the table size is 20 GB or larger, the table's provisioned read throughput is not being fully used,
   and sequential Scan operations are too slow.   <- Correct Answer

Correct Answer

A smaller page size means uses fewer read operations and creates a "pause" between each request which reduces the impact of a
query or scan operation. A larger number of smaller operations can allow other critical requests to succeed without throttling.
Reference: Best Practices for Querying and Scanning Data.
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/bp-query-scan.html

For large tables, a parallel scan can complete much faster than a sequential one, if the table size is 20 GB or larger,
the table's provisioned read throughput is not being fully used, and sequential Scan operations are too slow.
Reference: Best Practices for Querying and Scanning Data.
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/bp-query-scan.html


Question 18

Your application is storing customer order data in a DynamoDB table. You need to run a query to find all the orders
placed by a specific customer in the last month. Which attributes would you use in your query?

Choices:
 - A composite Primary Key made up of the CustomerName and OrderDate
 - The Partition Key of CustomerID and a Sort Key of OrderDate         <- Correct Answer
 - The Partition Key of OrderDate and Sort Key of CustomerName
 - The Partition Key of OrderDate and a Sort Key of CustomerID

CustomerID is the best choice as it will be a unique value. To identify all the orders placed in the last month by
a customer, you should use OrderDate as the Sort Key. The Partition Key of CustomerID would also be more unique
than CustomerName.

Question 21

In DynamoDB, a scan operation is used to:

Choices:
 - Find items in a table based on the Primary Key values
 - Return all data attributes for every item in the table or index         <- Correct Answer
 - Find items in a table based on the Sort Key attribute
 - Return the entire contents of a table filtered on the Primary Key attribute

A Scan operation in Amazon DynamoDB reads every item in a table or a secondary index. By default, a Scan operation
returns all of the data attributes for every item in the table or index. You can use the ProjectionExpression parameter
so that Scan only returns some of the attributes, rather than all of them. Working with Scans in DynamoDB.

Question 27

In order to address performance considerations and achieve faster response times, which of the following best practices
can be used for Querying and Scanning Data in Amazon DynamoDB large tables?

Choose 2
- Reduce the page size to return fewer items per results page.                 <- Correct Answer
- Set your queries to be eventually consistent.
- Run parallel scans if the table size is 20 GB or larger, the table's provisioned read throughput is not being fully
  used, and sequential Scan operations are too slow.                           <- Correct Answer
- Scan using the 'FilterExpression' parameter to discard items that you do not want to appear in the results.

A Query operation performs eventually consistent reads, by default.

A smaller page size means uses fewer read operations and creates a "pause" between each request which reduces the
impact of a query or scan operation. A larger number of smaller operations can allow other critical requests to
succeed without throttling.
Reference: Best Practices for Querying and Scanning Data.
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/bp-query-scan.html

For large tables, a parallel scan can complete much faster than a sequential one, if the table size is 20 GB or larger,
the table's provisioned read throughput is not being fully used, and sequential Scan operations are too slow.
Reference: Best Practices for Querying and Scanning Data.
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/bp-query-scan.html

  Chapter 7 KMS and Encryption on AWS

---------------------------------------------------
7.1 KMS 101

  KMS (Key Management Service)
    - AWS Key Management Service
    Managed
      - Managed service that makes it easy for you to create and control the encryption keys
        used to encrypt your data
    Integrated
      - Seamlessly integrated with many AWS services to make encrypting data in those services
        as easy as checking a box
    Simple
      - With KMS, it is simple to encrypt your data with encryption keys that you manage

  KMS Use Cases
    - Whenever you are dealing with sensitive information
    - sensitive data that you want to keep secret, e.g., Customer data, financial data,
      DB passwords, secrets, credentials

  KMS Integrates With Most Other AWS Services
     Integrated services include: S3, RDS, DynamoDB, Lambda, EBS, EFS, CloudTrail, Developer Tools
       - lambda can be used to encrypt and decrypt data provide it has access to the KMS key
       - cloudtrail includes an audit of all the KMS accesses

  CMK (Customer Master Key)
    CMK
      - can encrypt / decrypt data up to 4KB
     CMK is used
       - to generate / encrypt / decrypt the Data Key
     Data Key
       - used to encrypt / decrypt your data
     Envelope Encryption
        - refers to using the CMK key to generate the Data Key which is then used to
          encrypt / decrypt your data

---------------------------------------------------
7.2 Creating a CMK - Demo


1. Create Group and Users to Manage CMK

   -> Console -> IAM -> Group -> Create Group -> Group Name: myKMSGroup, policies: AdminstratorAccess
     -> Create Group

   -> Console -> IAM -> Group -> Create User -> User Name: myKeyManager, console access, custom password,
     -> Next -> Add user to group, user group: myKMSGroup -> next ->  Create User

   -> Console -> IAM -> Group -> Create User -> User Name: myKeyUser, console access, custom password,
     -> Next -> Add user to group, user group: myKMSGroup -> next ->  Create User


   -> Console -> KMS -> Create Key -> Configure Key: Key Type: Symmetric, Key Usage: Encrypt and decrypt,
         Key Material Origin: KMS, Regionality: Single-region -> Next -> Alias: MyCMK  -> Next
         Key Administrators: myKeyManager -> Next -> Define Key Usage permissions: Key Users:  myKeyUser
         -> Next ->


  Note:
     Key Type:
        Symmetric:  A single key used for encrypting and decrypting data or generating and verifying HMAC codes.
                    - only users with valid AWS KMS credentials can encrypt and decrypt using synmetric key
                    - for course, focus is on symmetic because it is covered in exam
                    - symmetric can also be used for generationg HASH based message authentication code (HMAC code).
                      Used when you want to determine authenticity of a message, e.g. JWT Token, JSON Web Token,
                      Tokenized credit card info
        Asymmetric: A public and private key pair used for encrypting and decrypting data or signing and verifying messages.
                    - if encryption requirement is outside KMS, you need to use asymmetric keys
                    - public key used to encrypt data, and private key is used to decrypt data
     Key Usage:
       Encrypt and decrypt: Use the key only to encrypt and decrypt data.
       Sign and verify:     Key pairs for digital signing. Uses the private key for signing and the public key for verification.

     Key Material Origin:
       - Key material origin is a KMS key property that represents the source of the key material when creating the KMS key.
       KMS - recommended
         - AWS KMS creates and manages the key material for the KMS key.
       External (Import Key material)
         - You create and import the key material for the KMS key.
       AWS CloudHSM (Cloud Hardware Security Module) key store
         - AWS KMS creates the key material in the AWS CloudHSM cluster of your AWS CloudHSM key store.
       External key store
         - The key material for the KMS key is in an external key manager outside of AWS.

    Regionality
      - Create your KMS key in a single AWS Region (default) or create a KMS key that you can replicate into multiple AWS Regions
      Single-region key
        - Never allow this key to be replicated into other regions
      Multi-region key
        - Allow this key to be replicated into other regions
        - if you want to encrypt in one region and decrypt in a another region

    [Key] Alias: used to reference the key (instead of a long key ID)


    Generate Key Policy (based on Key Admin and Key User):
            {
            "Id": "key-consolepolicy-3",
            "Version": "2012-10-17",
            "Statement": [
                {
                    "Sid": "Enable IAM User Permissions",
                    "Effect": "Allow",
                    "Principal": {
                        "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::732108167437:root"
                    },
                    "Action": "kms:*",
                    "Resource": "*"
                },
                {
                    "Sid": "Allow access for Key Administrators",
                    "Effect": "Allow",
                    "Principal": {
                        "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::732108167437:user/myKeyManager"
                    },
                    "Action": [
                        "kms:Create*",
                        "kms:Describe*",
                        "kms:Enable*",
                        "kms:List*",
                        "kms:Put*",
                        "kms:Update*",
                        "kms:Revoke*",
                        "kms:Disable*",
                        "kms:Get*",
                        "kms:Delete*",
                        "kms:TagResource",
                        "kms:UntagResource",
                        "kms:ScheduleKeyDeletion",
                        "kms:CancelKeyDeletion"
                    ],
                    "Resource": "*"
                },
                {
                    "Sid": "Allow use of the key",
                    "Effect": "Allow",
                    "Principal": {
                        "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::732108167437:user/myKeyUser"
                    },
                    "Action": [
                        "kms:Encrypt",
                        "kms:Decrypt",
                        "kms:ReEncrypt*",
                        "kms:GenerateDataKey*",
                        "kms:DescribeKey"
                    ],
                    "Resource": "*"
                },
                {
                    "Sid": "Allow attachment of persistent resources",
                    "Effect": "Allow",
                    "Principal": {
                        "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::732108167437:user/myKeyUser"
                    },
                    "Action": [
                        "kms:CreateGrant",
                        "kms:ListGrants",
                        "kms:RevokeGrant"
                    ],
                    "Resource": "*",
                    "Condition": {
                        "Bool": {
                            "kms:GrantIsForAWSResource": "true"
                        }
                    }
                }
            ]
        }

        -> console -> KMS -> AWS Managed keys -> shows all keys managed
            each AWS service previously used will have a KMS key

---------------------------------------------------
7.3 CMK Summary

  Exam Tips

    Customer Master Key (CMK)
       Alias
         - your application can refer to the alias when using the CMK
       Creation Date
         - the date and time when the CMK was created
       Key State
         - Enabled, disabled, pending deletion, unavailable, not authorized
       Key Material
         - Customer-provided or AWS-Provided
       Stay inside KMS
          - CMK cannot be exported outside KMS

    Set Up CMK
       - Create alias and description
       - Choose key material option (generated by KMS, customer provided, CloudHSM)
       - Key Administrative Permissions
         - IAM users and roles that can administer (but not use) the key through the KMS API
       - Key Usage Permissions
         - IAM Users and roles that can use the key to encrypt and decrypt data

    AWS-Managed CMK
      - AWS-provided and AWS-Managed CMK
      - Used on your behalf with the AWS services (e.g. S3, RDS, ...) integrated with KMS

    Customer-Managed CMK
      - you create, own and manage yourself

    Data Key
     - Encryption keys that you can used to encrypt data, including large amounts of data
     - You can used the CMK to generate, encrypt, and decrypt data keys

---------------------------------------------------
7.4 Unstanding KMS API Calls - Demo

  AWS CLI Documentation for KMS commands
  https://awscli.amazonaws.com/v2/documentation/api/latest/reference/kms/index.html#cli-aws-kms

  -> create access/secret access keys for myKeyUser

  1. Create EC2 instance


      -> ec2 -> launch instance -> name: keydemo, AL2, t2.micro, key pair: none,
         Network: vpc: default, subnet: default, Auto-assign public IP: enable;
         Create SG: launch-wizard-1, SSH anywhere  ;
         -> Create Instance

  2. log in to EC2 instance and configure AWS CLI with myKeyUser credentials
    -> Cloudshell -> Connect
    # create secret.txt file
    $  echo 'Hello Cloud Guru!' > secret.txt

    # configure AWS CLI usint myKeyUser credentials
    $ aws configure
    AWS Access Key ID [None]: <accessKey>
    AWS Secret Access Key [None]: <secretKey>
    Default region name [None]: us-east-1
    Default output format [None]: json


  3. Create "secret.txt" file and use MyCMK key to encrypt


    # get MyCMK Key ID from console
    # in provided commands, replace YOURKEYIKEHERE witht KMS Key Id

    # encrypt the file
    #  encrypt with Ciphertext and base64 encoding
    aws kms encrypt --key-id <MyCMK Key ID> --plaintext fileb://secret.txt --output text --query CiphertextBlob | base64 --decode > encryptedsecret.txt

    # decrypt the file
    # note: keyId was not specified, so it is using metadata in encrypted file to determine keyId
    # decrypt using cyphertext with a base64 decoding
    aws kms decrypt --ciphertext-blob fileb://encryptedsecret.txt --output text --query Plaintext | base64 --decode > decryptedsecret.txt
    # verify the decryption
    cat decryptedsecret.txt

    # re-encrypt
    # Note: can use re-encrypt to unencrypt with one CMK and re-encrypt with different CMK
    # unencrypt in memory then create a new encrypted file , this time text file instead of base64
    $ aws kms re-encrypt --destination-key-id <MyCMK Key ID> --ciphertext-blob fileb://encryptedsecret.txt | base64 > newencryption.txt


  4. Enable KMS Key rotation

   # enable KMS Key rotation on annual basis:
   $ aws kms enable-key-rotation --key-id <MyCMK Key ID>

   # verify key-rotation is enabled
   $ aws kms get-key-rotation-status --key-id <MyCMK Key ID>
    {
        "KeyRotationEnabled": true
    }


  5. Generated a Data Key (required to encrypt file > 4KB)

  # generate an AES 256 bit Data Key
  $ aws kms generate-data-key --key-id <MyCMK Key ID> --key-spec AES_256

     {
        "Plaintext": "GmYpxdGjYj814hPG4Cq4B/NtcqQ/YQP7Pswd+mPjPM4=",
        "KeyId": "arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:012345678901:key/<MyCMK Key ID>",
        "CiphertextBlob": "AQIDAHjGUDIUd6081/AkLQIml+qRRi8gPaQbJaEqi7chYCy7VQFxR5RvsGKQLpnOKOwUqWjrAAAAfjB8BgkqhkiG9w0BBwagbzBtAgEAMGgGCSqGSIb3DQEHATAeBglghkgBZQMEAS4wEQQM8BJGrfAZohPOi014AgEQgDvLTG/T76XPRRIh99ahpwUgN4FQy3EwTUL0sTZk5CkRWN8em7ysSUy1Zz5S3ZGaGTgKIszH/hrz//RXgg=="
     }

     # returns a plaintext (unencrypted, but base64 encoded) Data Key and Ciphertext encrypted Data Key

  Exam Tips

    KMS API Calls
      aws kms encrypt
        - encrypts plaintext into ciphertext the by using customer Master Key (CMK)
      aws kms decrypt
        - decrypts ciphertext that was encrypted by an AWK KMS customer master key (CMK)
      aws kms re-encrypt
        - decripts ciphertext (in-memory) and then re-encrypts it entirely with AWS KMS
        - use when you change the CMK or manually rotate the CMK
      aws kms enable-key-rotation
        - enables automatic key rotation every 365 days
      aws kms generate-data-key
        - used CMK to generate a data key to encrypt data > 4KB
        - returns a plaintext (unencrypted, but base64 encoded) Data Key and Ciphertext (encrypted) Data Key

  Note: AWS KMS does not store or manage data keys, and you cannot use KMS to encrypt or decrypt with data keys.
        To use data keys to encrypt and decrypt, use the AWS Encryption SDK

---------------------------------------------------
7.5 Exploring Envelope Encryption

  Envelope Encryption
    - A process for encrypting your data. It applies to files > 4KB

  Envelope Encryption Process

     AWS KMS
    |-------| GenerateDataKey                      Encrypts
    |  CMK  | ----------------->     Data Key     --------->  Your Data
    |-----\-|      API            (Envelope Key)
           \
            \------> Encrypts    -----------------> Encrypted data key is stored with the Data.
                                                    KMS does not store the Data Key
                       Data Key

  The KMS Decryption Process

     |----------------|                AWS KMS
     | Encrypted      |               |-------|
     | Data Key    <----------------- | CMK   |                               /----------------------> Delete
     | (Envelope Key)\|               |-------|                              /                         Plain Text
     |                \ KMS API                          Plain Text --------/                          Data Key
     | Encrypted      |\-> KMS API --------------------> Data Key       -------->|
     | Your  \        |             Decrypt Data Key      (Envelope Key)         | Decrypt     Your
     | Data   \       |                                                          |----------->  Data
     |---------\------|                                                          | Data
                \--------------------------------------------------------------->|


  Why Envelope Encryption?
     - When not encrypt data using CMK directly?
     Network
       - When you encrypt data directly with KMS, it must be transferred over the network
     Performance
       - With envelope encryption, only the Data Key goes over the network, not your data
     Benefits
       - The Data Key is used locally in your application or AWS service, avoiding the need to
         transfer large amounts of data to KMS

  Exam Tips

    Envelope Encryption
      - encrypting the key that encrypts our data
      - The CMK is used to encrypt the data key (aka envelope key)
      - The Data Key encrypts our data
      - Used for encrypting anything over 4KB
      - Using envelope encryption avoids sending all your data into KMS over the network
      - GenerateDataKey API Call is used by KMS to create an encrypted Data Key (plus a plaintext Data Key)
         - kms generate-data-key CLI calls GenerateDataKey API
         - kms generate-data-key returns both a plaintext (unencrypted) Data Key and Ciphertext (encrypted) Data Key

---------------------------------------------------
7.6 Enabling and Disabling KMS Key Rotation - Demo

  1. Create a Customer Master Key (which is customer managed key)
    - accept all the default


   -> Console -> KMS -> Create Key -> Configure Key: Key Type: Symmetric, Key Usage: Encrypt and decrypt,
         Key Material Origin: KMS, Regionality: Single-region -> Next -> Alias: mykmskey  -> Next
         Key Administrators: Admin (Role) -> Next -> Define Key Usage permissions: Key Users:  cloud_user
         -> Next ->


  2. After the Key is Created, enable key rotation
     - Click the Key Rotation tab, select the option automatical key rotation every year, and click Save

     -> KMS -> select "mykmskey -> Key Rotation <tab> -> check "Automaticall rotate this KMS key every year" -> Save

  3. Disable Key Rotation
    - deselect the Key Rotation option, and click Save

     -> KMS -> select "mykmskey -> Key Rotation <tab> -> uncheck "Automaticall rotate this KMS key every year" -> Save

  Exam Tips

    Enabling and Disabling KMS Key Rotation
      - automatic rotation is available for CMKs
      - KMS will rotate the cryptographic material on a yearly basis
      - it saves previous versions of the cryptographic material so that you can still decrypt the files that
        were previously encrypted

7.7 Certificate Management in AWS


  What is the AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)?
    - creates manage, and deploy public and private SSL (Secure Socket Layer) /
      TLS (Transport Layer Security) certificates
    - can be used with AWS Services like Elastic Load Balancer, CloudFront, API Gateway and
      Web Applications

  What are SSL/TLS Certificates
    - Digital certificates are used to verify the authenticity of a website
    - enable a secure connection between visitors and the website
    - used to encrypt data in transit

  ACM and CloudFront
    Example Use Case
      - A company's website hosted on EC2 instances and served by CloudFront that needs
        to host a content securely and protect user data using SSL/TLS encryption
        User ----> HTTPS ----> CloudFront  ---> EC2
      - CloudFront has its own default certificates, but a company may have a requirement
        to manage your own certificates. For example, if you have your own domain name
        that you may want to use with CloudFront
    CloudFront Settings
      - in the CloudFront settings, you can optiomally associate a custom SSL cerntficate using ACM
      - With CloudFront, you MUST uses us-east-1 region to generate your ACM certificate

  Exam Tips

     Certicate Management in AWS
       AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)
         - creates and manage SSL/TLS certificates for securing your website
       Secure Connection
         - Enables a secure connection to your website using HTTPS
       CloudFront
         - When using ACM with CloudFront, the certificates must be created in the
           us-east-1 region

7.8 KMS (Key Management Service) Summary

  Customer Master Key (CMK)
     Alias
       - your application can refer to the alias when using the CMK
     Creation Date
       - the date and time when the CMK was created
     Key State
       - Enabled, disabled, pending deletion, unavailable, not authorized
     Key Material
       - Customer-provided or AWS-Provided
     Stay inside KMS
        - CMK cannot be exported outside KMS

  Set Up CMK
     - Create alias and description
     - Choose key material option (generated by KMS, customer provided, CloudHSM)
     - Key Administrative Permissions
       - IAM users and roles that can administer (but not use) the key through the KMS API
     - Key Usage Permissions
       - IAM Users and roles that can use the key to encrypt and decrypt data

  AWS Managed CMK
    - AWS-provided and AWS-Managed CMK
    - Used on your behalf with the AWS services (e.g. S3, RDS, ...) integrated with KMS
    - CMKs in your account that are created, managed, and used on your behalf by an AWS Service
      that is integrated with AWS KMS

  Customer-Managed CMK
    - you create, own and manage yourself
    - used to encrypt and decrypt files up to 4KB and generate the Data Key

  Data Key
   - Encryption keys that you can used to encrypt data, including large amounts of data
   - You can used the CMK to generate, encrypt, and decrypt data keys

  Envelope Encryption
    - encrypting the key that encrypts our data
    - The CMK is used to encrypt the data key (aka envelope key)
    - The Data Key encrypts our data
    - Data Key is Used for encrypting anything over 4KB
    - Using envelope encryption avoids sending all your data into KMS over the network
    - GenerateDataKey API Call is used by KMS to create an encrypted Data Key (plus a plaintext Data Key)
       - kms generate-data-key CLI calls GenerateDataKey API
       - kms generate-data-key returns both a plaintext (unencrypted) Data Key and Ciphertext (encrypted) Data Key


  KMS API Calls
    aws kms encrypt
      - encrypts plaintext into ciphertext the by using customer Master Key (CMK)
    aws kms decrypt
      - decrypts ciphertext that was encrypted by an AWK KMS customer master key (CMK)
    aws kms re-encrypt
      - decripts ciphertext (in-memory) and then re-encrypts it entirely with AWS KMS using a CMK
        that your specify
      - e.g., use when you change the CMK or manually rotate the CMK
    aws kms enable-key-rotation
      - enables automatic key rotation every 365 days
    aws kms generate-data-key
      - used CMK to generate a data key to encrypt data > 4KB
      - returns a plaintext (unencrypted, but base64 encoded) Data Key and Ciphertext (encrypted) Data Key

    Note: AWS KMS does not store or manage data keys, and you cannot use KMS to encrypt or decrypt with data keys.
          To use data keys to encrypt and decrypt, use the AWS Encryption SDK

  Enabling and Disabling KMS Key Rotation
    - automatic rotation is available for CMKs
    - KMS will rotate the cryptographic material on a yearly basis
    - it saves previous versions of the cryptographic material so that you can still decrypt the files that
      were previously encrypted

   Certicate Management in AWS
     AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)
       - creates and manage SSL/TLS certificates for securing your website
     Secure Connection
       - Enables a secure connection to your website using HTTPS
     CloudFront
       - When using ACM with CloudFront, the certificates must be created in the
         us-east-1 region

   Note: IAM is used as a certificate manager only when you must support HTTPS connections in a Region that is
         not supported by ACM. IAM securely encrypts your private keys and stores the encrypted version in IAM
         SSL certificate storage. IAM supports deploying server certificates in all Regions, but you must obtain
         your certificate from an external provider for use with AWS.

7.8 AWS Certified Developer - Associate - KMS and Encryption on AWS Quiz


Question 4

Which of the following statements about AWS managed keys are correct?

Choose 2
 - The AWS managed key is used to encrypt and decrypt text files.
 - The data key is used to encrypt and decrypt the customer master key.
 - You cannot use AWS managed keys in cryptographic operations directly.   <- Correct Answer
 - Envelope encryption is used to protect your encryption key.             <- Correct Answer

AWS managed keys cannot be used to directly encrypt and decrypt files. You cannot manage AWS managed keys,
rotate them, or change their key policies. Customer managed keys can be used with the KMS command to encrypt
files when key usage rights are given.

Correct Answers

You can view AWS managed keys and their key policies in your account and audit their use in AWS CloudTrail logs.
However, you cannot manage, rotate, or change their key policies. AWS managed keys are created and managed by AWS
for specific services, such as Amazon S3, Amazon EBS, and Amazon RDS. These services use AWS managed keys to
encrypt your data, but you cannot use them directly yourself.

Reference: Customer Master Keys (CMKs).
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/concepts.html#master_keys

When you encrypt your data, your data is protected, but you have to protect your encryption key. One strategy
is to encrypt it. Envelope encryption is the practice of encrypting plaintext data with a data key and then
encrypting the data key under another key.

Reference: Envelope encryption.
https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/concepts.html#enveloping


Question 8

Which of the following statements is correct in relation to KMS?

Choose 2
 - You cannot export (copy out of the AWS KMS service in plaintext) your customer master key.  <- Correct Answer
 - KMS encryption keys are single-Region by default.                                           <- Correct Answer
 - You can export (copy out of the AWS KMS service in plaintext) your customer master key.
 - KMS encryption keys are global by default.

CMKs are created and used only within the service to help ensure their security, enable your policies to be
consistently enforced, and provide a centralized log of their use.

Single-Region is the default option when creating KMS keys, which are only stored and used in the Region they
are configured. KMS multi-Region encryption keys can be created using advanced options and can be replicated
into other Regions.

AWS Key Management Service FAQs > Security.
https://aws.amazon.com/kms/faqs/#Security

  Chapter 8 Other AWS Services

---------------------------------------------------
8.1 Simple Queue Service (SQS)

  Poll-Based Messaging
    - one resource sends messages to SQS queue, another resource retrieve messages when it is ready (polls queue)

  What is SQS
    - A message queue service
    - enables web service applications to quickly and reliably queue messages that one
      component in the application generates for another component to comsue
    - a queue is a temporary repository for messages awaiting processing

  SQS Examples
    Meme Website
                                                 Initial
                                                 Process           Poll for
              up load                            Requst            requests
        Photo --------> S3 bucket -----> Lambda ----------> SQS  <---------- EC2
                           ^                                                  |
                           |       process photo and upload to S3 bucket      |
                           |--------------------------------------------------|


  SQS Visibility Timeout
    - default Visibility timeout is 30 sec, min is 0 sec, max is 12 hours
    - after backend resource receives the message, the SQS queue locks out the message for the
      Visibility Timeout period (no other resource has access to the message during the lock out)
    - if backend does NOT respond in the Visibility Timeout period to purge the message, the message
      reappears in the SQS queue and it is available for other backend resources

  SQS Features
    Decouple Application Components
      - Decouple the components of an application so they run independently, easing message
        management between components
    Store Messages
      - any component of a distributed application can store messages in the queue
      - Messages can contain up to 256KB of text in any format (e.g. XML, JSON, plaintext)
    Retrieve Messages
      - any component can later rettrieve the messages programmatically using the AWS SQS API
    A buffer between components
      - a buffer between the component receiving the data for processing and the component
        producing and saving the data
    Resolving Scheduling Issues
      - the queue resolves issues that arise if the producer is producing work faster than the
        consumer can process it, or if the producer or consumer are only intermittently connected
        to the network

  SQS Key Facts
    Pull-Based
      - SQS is a pull-based, not pushed based
    256 KB
      - messages are up to 256KB in size
    Text Data
      - always text data including XML, JSON, and unformated text
    Guarantee
      - guarantee messages will be processed at least once
    Retention Period
      - default is 4 days
      - can be set between 1 minutes and 14 days
      - after message retention period, message exceeding this period are purged


  Exam Tips

    Simple Queue Service (SQS)
      - SQS is a distributed message queuing system
      - allows us to decouple components of an application so that they are independent
      - pull-based (via polling), not pushed based

---------------------------------------------------
8.2 Understanding SQS Queue Types

  Two types of SQS Queues
    Standard Queues
      - default which provide best-effort ordering
    FIFO (First In First Out) Queues
      - messaging ordering strickly preserved

  Standard Queues
    Unlimited Transactions
      - nearly unlimited number of transactions per second
    Guarantee
      - Guarantee that a message is delivered at least once
    Best-Effort Ordering
      - provide best-effort ordering which ensures messages are generally delivered in the same
        order as they are sent
      - Occasionally (because of the highly distributed architecture that allows high throughput),
        more than one copy of a message might be delivered out of order

  FIFO Queues
    First-in-First-Out Delivery
      - the order in which messages are sent and received is stricly preserved
    Exactly-Once Processing
      - a message is delivered once and remains available until a consumer processes and
        deletes it
      - Duplicates are not introduced
    300 TPS Limit
      - limited to 300 Transactions Per Second (TPS), but have all the capabilities of
        standard queues


  Exam Tips

    SQS Queue Types
      Standard Queue
        - Best-effort ordering
        - Messages delivered at least once
        - Occasional duplicates
        - the default queue type
      FIFO Queue
        - First-In-First-Out message order is strickly preserved
        - messages are delivered once
        - no duplicates
        - good for banking transactions which need to happen in a strick order

---------------------------------------------------
8.3 SQS Settings


  SQS FAQs
  https://aws.amazon.com/sqs/faqs/

  SQS short and long polling
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSSimpleQueueService/latest/SQSDeveloperGuide/sqs-short-and-long-polling.html

  SQS visibility timeout
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSSimpleQueueService/latest/SQSDeveloperGuide/sqs-visibility-timeout.html

  Visibility Timeout
    - default Visibility timeout is 30 sec, min is 0 sec, max is 12 hours
    - the amount of time that the message is invisible in the SQS queue after a reader
      picks up the message
    - Increase it if the task takes more than 30 seconds

  Short Polling versus Long Polling
    Short Polling
      - short polling: check for message, if no message, then disconnect
      - returns a response immediately even if the message queue being polled is empty
      - This can result in a log of empty responses if nothing is in the queue
      - short polling is the default
    Long Polling
      - long polling:  check for message, if no message, Wait for a wait time period (max 20 sec)
        of time before disconnecting
      - periodically polls the queue
      - doesn't return a response until a message arrives in the message queue or the
        long poll times out
      - can save money
      - long polling is more efficient with your cost and CPU cycles
      - in most cases, you should use long polling

  Exam Tips

    SQS Settings

      Visibility Timeout
      - default Visibility timeout is 30 sec, min is 0 sec, max is 12 hours
      - the amount of time that the message is invisible in the SQS queue after a reader
        picks up the message
      - Increase it if the task takes more than 30 seconds

     Short Polling
       - a response is returned immediately even if no messages are in the queue
       - there is a cost per response

     Long Polling
       - long polling:  check for message, if no message, Wait for a wait time period (max 20 sec)
         of time before disconnecting
       - most cost effective
       - in most cases, you should use long polling

---------------------------------------------------
8.4 SQS Delay Queues & Large Messages


  SQS Delay Queues - postpone delivery of new messages
    - Postpone delivery of new messages to a queue for a number of seconds
    - messages sent to the delay queue remain invisible to consumer for the duration
      of the delay period
   - Default delivery delay is 0 seconds, maxium is 900 second (15 min)
   - Delivery Delay (e.g. The ReceiveMessage call sets WaitTimeSeconds to 0)

   - for standard queues, changing the setting doesn't affect the delay of messages
     already in the queue, only new messages
   - for FIFO queues, this affects the delay of messages already in the queue


  When to use a Delay SQS Queue
    - large distributed applications which may need to introduce a delay in processing
    - you need to apply a delay to an entire queue of messages
    - e.g., adding a a delay of a few seconds to allow for updates to your sales and stock control
      databases before sending a notification to a customer confirming an online transaction

  Best Practice for Managing Large SQS Messages using S3
    - For large messages - 256KB to 2 GB in size
    - use S3 to store the messages
    - Use SQS Extended Client Library for Java to manage messages in S3
    - (You'll also need the AWS SDK for Java) - provide an API for S3 bucket and object operations

    SQS + SQS Extended  +   AWS SDK   ----------------> S3
          Client Library    For Java
          for Java

  SQS Extended Client Library for Java allows you to:
    - specify if messages are always stored in S3 or just messages over 256 KB
    - send a message which references a message object stored in S3
    - Get a message object from S3
    - Delete a message object from S3

  Managing Large SQS Messages in S3
    You need:
      - AWS SDK for Java
      - SQS Extended Client library for Java
      - An S3 bucket
    Cannot use:
      - AWS CLI
      - AWS Management Console / SQS
      - SQS API
      - Any other AWS SDK

  Exam Tips

    SQS Delay Queues and Large Messages
      SQS Delay Queues
        - postpone delivery of new messages
        - messages in Delay Queues remain invisible for the duration of the delivery delay period (0 - 900 sec)
        - Large distributed applications which may need to introduce a delay in processing

       Managing Large Messages in S3
         - Store large messages - 256 KB to 2GB in S3
        You need:
          - AWS SDK for Java
          - SQS Extended Client library for Java
          - An S3 bucket
        Cannot use:
          - AWS CLI, AWS Management Console / SQS, SQS API, Any other AWS SDK

          SQS + SQS Extended  +   AWS SDK   ----------------> S3
                Client Library    For Java
                for Java

8.5 Triggering AWS Lambda from Amazon SQS

About this lab

In this hands-on AWS lab, you will learn how to trigger a Lambda function using SQS. This Lambda function will process
messages from the SQS queue and insert the message data as records into a DynamoDB table.

Learning objectives
 - Create the Lambda Function
   - name th function SQSDynamoDB using Python 3.x and lambda execution role
 - Create the SQS Trigger
   - in the new lambda function, add a trigger that selects SQS and messages
 - Copy the Source Code into the Lambda Function
   - copy the code provided an paste it into the lambda_function.py. Deploy the function
 - Log In to the EC2 Instance and Test the Script
   - long in to the EC2 instance and run the send_message.py script to trigger the lambda function
 - Confirm Messages Were Inserted into the DynamoDB Table
   - Go to DynamoDB and open the message table to confirm there are item populated theure



Create the Lambda Function

    In the search bar on top, enter lambda.
    From the search results, select Lambda.
    Click the Create function button.
    On the Create function page, select Author from scratch.
    Under Basic Information, set the following parameters for each field:

    Function name: Enter SQSDynamoDB.
    Runtime: Select Python 3.9 from the dropdown menu.
    Architecture: Select x86_64.

    Under Permissions, expand Change default execution role.
    Select Use an existing role.
    Under Existing role, select lambda-execution-role from the dropdown menu.
    Click the Create function button.

Create the SQS Trigger

    Click the + Add trigger button.
    Under Trigger configuration, click the Select a source dropdown menu.
    From the menu, select SQS.
    Under SQS queue, click the search bar and select Messages.
    Ensure that the checkbox next to Activate trigger is checked.
    Click Add.

Copy the Source Code into the Lambda Function

    Under the + Add trigger button, click the Code tab.
    On the left side, double-click on lambda_function.py.
    Delete the contents of the function.
    Navigate to the link below to the source code for lambda_function.py:

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ACloudGuru-Resources/SQSLambdaTriggers/master/lambda_function.py. 6. Copy the code. 7. Return to the AWS console and paste the code into the lambda_function.py code box. 8. Click the Deploy button.
Log In to the EC2 Instance and Test the Script

    In the search bar on top of the AWS console, enter sqs.

    From the search results, select Simple Queue Service.

    Click Messages.

    Click the Monitoring tab to monitor our SQS messages.

    In the search bar on top, enter ec2.

    From the search results, select EC2 and open it in a new browser tab or window.

    Under Resources, click Instances (running).

    In the existing instance available, click the checkbox next to its name.

    Click the Connect button at the top.

    Click Connect at the bottom to open a shell and access the command line.

    In the shell, become the cloud_user role:
    su - cloud_user

    View a list of files available to you:
    ls

    View the contents of the send_message.py file:
    cat send_message.py

    Start sending messages to our DynamoDB table from our Messages SQS queue with an interval of 0.1 seconds:
    ./send_message.py -q Messages -i 0.1

    After a few seconds, hit Control + C to stop the command from continuing to run.

Confirm Messages Were Inserted into the DynamoDB Table

    Return to the browser tab or window with the Messages queue in Amazon SQS open. You may have to wait a few minutes to see results showing up in the tables, but you should soon see a spike in the table Number of Messages Received.
    In the search bar on top, enter dynamodb.
    From the search results, select DynamoDB.
    In the left-hand navigation menu, select Tables.
    Select the Message table.
    In the top-right corner of the page, click Explore table items and review the list of items that were inserted from our script, sent to SQS, triggered Lambda, and inserted into the DynamoDB database.

8.6 Simple Notification Service

  Amazon SNS message delivery retries
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sns/latest/dg/sns-message-delivery-retries.html

  Amazon SNS FAQs
     https://aws.amazon.com/sns/faqs/

  Simple Notification Service (SNS)
    - a web service that makes it easy to set up, operate, and send notifications from the Cloud
    - messages sent from an application can be immediately delivered to subscribers or other applications

  SNS
    - notification service
    Push Notfications
      - to devices (e.g. Apple, Google, Fire OS, Windows, Android)
    SMS (Short Message Service - text message) and Email
      - SMS text messages or email or SQS queues or any HTTP endpoint
    Lambda
      - trigger lambda functions to process the information in the message, publish to another
        SNS topic, or send the message to another AWS service

  SNS - how does it work
    - a pub-sub model - publish and subscribe model
      - applications PUBLISh or PUSH messages to a TOPIC
      - Subscribers RECEIVE messages from a TOPIC

  A pub-sub model
    - Notifications are delivered using a push mechanism that eliminates the need to periodically
      check or poll for new information and updata
                                                         Subscribers
      online                                       |-->  Lambda
      banking    -------> SNS    -------> Topic ---|-->  SQS Queue
      app                 Queue                    |-->  email
                                                   |-->  mobile Device

   SNS Topic
     - it is an access point, allowing recipients to subscribe to, and receive identical copies
       of the same notification
     - SNS delivers appropriately-formatted copies of the messages to each subscriber (e.g. iOS,
       Android, SMS, etc.)

    SNS Durability
       - Durable Storage
         - prevents messages from being lost
         - all messages published to SNS are stored redundantly across multiple AZs

    SNS Benefits
      Instantaneous
        - instantaneous, push-based delivery (no polling)
      Simple
        - simple API and easy integration with applications
      Flexible
        - flexible message delivery over multiple transport protocols
      Inexpensive
        - inexpensive, pay-as-you-go model with no up-front costs
      Easy to Configure
        - web-based AWS Management Console offers the simplicity of a point-and-click interface
      Managed Service
        - With all the high availability and durability features needed for a production environment

  SNS versus SQS
    SNS
      - messaging service
      - SNS is push-based
      - think push notifications
    SQS
      - messaging service
      - SQS is pull-based
      - think polling the queue for messages

  Exam Tips

    Simple Notfication Service (SNS)
      Notifications
        - scalable and highly available notfication service which allows us to send
          push notification from the cloud
      Message Formats
        - SMS text messages, email, SQS queues, or any HTTP endpoint
      Pub-Sub
        - publish-subscrber (pub-sub) model whereby users subscribe to topics,
        - pushd mechanism, rather than a pull (or poll) mechanism

8.7  SES vs SNS

  SES (Simple Email Service)
    Scalable and highly available email
     - designed to help marketing teams and application developers send marketing notifications,
       and transactional emails to their customers using a pay-as-you-go model
    Send and Receive Email
      - can also be used to receive emails with incoming mails delivered to an S3 Bucket
    Trigger Lambda and SNS
      - incoming emails can be used to trigger Lambda Functions and SNS notifications

  SES Use Cases
    Automated emails
      - notification that there is a new post in a discussion forum that you moderate
    online purchases
      - purchase confirmations, shipping notifications, and order status updates
    Marketing Emails
      - Marketing communications, advistisements, newsletters, special offers, new products,
        and Black Friday deals

  SES vs SNS
    SES
      - email messaging service (only email)
      - can trigger a Lambda function or SNS notification
      - can be used for both incoming and outgoing emails
      - an email address is all that is required to start sending messages
    SNS
      - Pub/sub messaging service; formats include SMS, HTTP, SQS, and email
      - can trigger a Lambda function
      - can fanout messages to a large number of recipients (replicate and push messages to
        multiple endpoints and formats)
      - consumers must subscribe to a topic to receive the notifications

  Exam Tips

    SES vs SNS
      SES (Simple Email Service)
        - SES is email only
        Send and receive emails
          - SES can be used for incoming and outgoing email
        Not Subscription-Based
          - it is not subscription-based, you only need to know the email addess (where to send the emails)
      SNS (Simple Notification Service)
        Multiple Formats
          - Pub/sub messaging service; formats include SMS, HTTP, SQS, and email
        Push Notification
          - push notifications only. Not for receiving notifications
        Pub/Sub model
          - publish / subscribe model
          - consumers must subscribe to a topic to receive the notifications
         Fanout
           - can fanout messages to a large number of recipients (e.g. to multple SQS queues,
             HTTP endpoints, and email addresses
