8.8 Kinesis 101


  Kinesis
    - a family of services that enables you to collect, process, and analyze streaming data in real time
    - allows you to build custom applications for your own business needs

  Why named Kinesis
    - kinesis is originally a Greek word, meaning movement or motion
    - Kinesis deals with data that is in motion, or streaming data

  Kinesis Streaming Data
    - data generated continuously by thousands of data sources that typically send in the data
      records simultaneously and in small kilobytes
      - examples:
         - financial transactions
         - stock prices
         - game data (as the gamer plays)
         - social media feeds
         - location-tracking data (Uber)
         - IoT sensors
         - clickstream data
         - log files

  Kinesis Four Core Services
    Kinesis Streams
      - stream data and video to allow you to build custom applications that process data in real time
      - Two Services:
        Data Streams and Video Streams
    Kinesis Data Firehose
      - Capture, transform, and load data streams into AWS data stores to enable near real-time analytics
        with BI (business Intelligence) tools
    Kinesis Data Analytics
      - analyze, query, and transform streamed data in real time using standard SQL
      - store the results in an AWS data store (e.g., S3, Redshift, Amazon Open Search)


  Kinesis Data Streams
    - 24-hours (default) with a maximum of 365 day retention
    - data stored in shards

                                    Kinesis                          AWS
       Data Producers:              Streams            Consumers     Data Store
                                   |-------|
       EC2                 ------> | shard |   ----->  EC2    |--->   DynamoDB
       mobile devices      ------> | shard |   ----->  EC2    |--->   S3
       compute devices     ------> | shard |   ----->  Lambda |--->   EMR (Elastic Map Reducer)
       IoTs                ------> | shard |   ----->  Lambda |--->   Redshift
                                   |-------|

  Kinesis Shards
    - only applies to Kinesis data streams
    - Kinesis streams are made up of Shards
    - each shard is a sequence of one or more data records and provides a fixed unit of capacity
       - 5 read transactions per second
       - The max total read rate is 2MB per second
       - 1K records writes per second
       - The max total write rate is 1MB per second
    -  Data records are composed of a sequence number, a partition key, and a data blob, which is an immutable sequence of bytes


  Kinesis Data Streams and Shards
    - the data capacity of the stream is determined by the number of shards
    - if the data rate increases, you can increase the capacity on your streams by increasing
      the number of shards

  Kinesis Partition Key
    - The partition key is used by Kinesis Data Streams to distribute data across shards
    - Kinesis Data Streams segregates the data records that belong to a stream into multiple shards, using the
      partition key associated with each data record to determine the shard to which a given data record belongs.
    - For the given use-case, as the partition key is not distributed enough, then all the data will get skewed at
      a few specific shards and not leveraging the entire cluster of shards.

  Kinesis Data Streams
    All in Order
      - each record in the stream has a unique sequence number that identifies it
      - with Kinesis data streams, the order of the records is maintanined

  Kinesis Agent
    - Kinesis Agent is a stand-alone Java software application that offers an easy way to collect and send data to Kinesis Data Streams.
    - The agent continuously monitors a set of files and sends new data to your stream.
    - The agent handles file rotation, checkpointing, and retry upon failures.
    - It delivers all of your data in a reliable, timely, and simple manner.
    - It also emits Amazon CloudWatch metrics to help you better monitor and troubleshoot the streaming process.
    - You can install the agent on Linux-based server environments such as web servers, log servers, and database server

  Kinesis Data Streams Encryption
    AWS KMS for data at rest
      - Server-side encryption is a feature in Amazon Kinesis Data Streams that automatically encrypts data before
        it's at rest by using an AWS KMS customer master key (CMK) you specify. Data is encrypted before it's written
        to the Kinesis stream storage layer and decrypted after it's retrieved from storage.
      - Server-side encryption encrypts incoming data only after encryption is enabled
     HTTPS for in-transit
       - Encryption in flight with HTTPS endpoint

  Kinesis Data Streams Enhanced Fan-Out and an HTTP/2 data retrieval
    - By default, the 2MB/second/shard output is shared between all of the applications consuming data from the stream.
    - You should use enhanced fan-out if you have multiple consumers retrieving data from a stream in parallel.
    - With enhanced fan-out developers can register stream consumers to use enhanced fan-out and receive their own
      2MB/second pipe of read throughput per shard, and this throughput automatically scales with the number of shards in a stream.

  Kinesis Video Streams
    - securely stream video from connected devices to AWS
    - videos can be used for analytics and machine learning
    - use to build applications for real-time video processing or batch-oriented video analytics


  Kinesis Data Firehose
    - no data retention, and no shards, no EC2 consumers, BI tools

                                    Kinesis
       Data Producers:              Firehose        AWS Data Store
                                   |-------|
       EC2                 ------> |Lambda*|   ----->  S3     ---->   Redshift
       mobile devices      ------> |       |   ----->  S3
       compute devices     ------> |       |   ----->  Open Search
       IoTs                ------> |       |
                                   |-------|
                                    * Optionally, can use Lambda function to process or analyze the data in real time

  Kinesis Data Analytics
    - real time analytics using SQL queries

                                    Kinesis
       Data Producers:              Firehose
                                   |--------|
       EC2                 ------> |Kinesis |
       mobile devices      ------> |Streams |           Kinesis       SQL         |--> Redshoift
       compute devices     ------> |        |   ----->  Analytics  +  Queries  ---|--> S3
       IoTs                ------> |Kinesis |                                     |--> OpenSearch
                                   |Firehose|
                                   |--------|

  Exam Tips

    Kinesis 101

      Kinesis Streams
        - Capture and store streaming videos and data
        - Consumer application process and analyze the data in real time

      Kinesis Firehose
        - capture, transform, and load data continuously into AWS Data stores
        - Existing BI (Business Intelligence) applications and tools can be used for near real-time
          analysis of the stored data

      Kinesis Analytics
        - Real-time analytics using standard SQL on data received by Kinesis Data Streams and Kinesis
          Data Firehose
        - Stores the processed data in AWS data stores (e.g., S3, Redshift, or OpenSearch)

  Note: STS: Security Token Service: AWS provides STS as a web service that enables you to request temporary,
        limited-privilege credentials for users.

  Architecture Overview
    - using CloudFormation (solution Deployment)

    EC2 (Java apps - producer & consumer)

       (capture steaming data with 2 shards)

8.10 Kinesis Shards and Consumers


  Kinesis Recap
    - Kinesis allows you to collect and analysis real time streaming data like unstructured data like
      clickstream data,  audio data, video data, application logs
    - A kinesis data stream is a set of shards
    - A shard is a sequence of data records in a stream, each data record has a unique sequence number
    - 24-hours (default) with a maximum of 365 day retention

                                    Kinesis
                                    Data                             AWS
       Data Producers:              Streams            Consumers     Data Store
                                   |-------|
       EC2                 ------> | shard |   ----->  EC2    |--->   DynamoDB
       mobile devices      ------> | shard |   ----->  EC2    |--->   S3
       compute devices     ------> | shard |   ----->  Lambda |--->   EMR
       IoTs                ------> | shard |   ----->  Lambda |--->   Redshift
                                   |-------|


  Kinesis Shards
    - only applies to Kinesis streams
    - the data capacity of your stream is the sum total capacity of it shards
    - per shard:
       - 5 reads transactions per second, up to a  2MB per second
       - 1K write records per second, up to a  1MB per second
    - As your data rate increases, you need to increase the number of shards (resharding)

  But what about the consumers?
    - kinesis Client Library runs on the consumer instances
       - EC2 + Kinesis Client Library
    - Tracks the number of shards in your stream
    - Discovers new shards when you reshard

  Kinesis Client Library (KCL)
    - The KCL ensures that every shard there is a record processor
    - manages the number record processors relative to the number of shards & consumers
    - if you have only one consumer instance, the KCL will create all the record processors on a single instance
    - If you have 2 or more consumers instances, KCL will load balance and create equal number of record processors
      on each instance


  But what about the consumers?

    Example 1
       Data Producers:              Streams            Consumers
                                   |-------|
       EC2                 ------> | shard |   <----|
       mobile devices      ------> | shard |   <----|  EC2 + KCL
       compute devices     ------> | shard |   <----|   -> record processors x 4
       IoTs                ------> | shard |   <----|
                                   |-------|

    -  1 x Kinesis data stream, 4 shards               - 1 x consumer, processing 4 shards
                                                       - 4 x record processors are running on consumer


    Example 2
       Data Producers:              Streams            Consumers
                                   |-------|
       EC2                 ------> | shard |   <----|  EC2 + KCL
       mobile devices      ------> | shard |   <----|   -> record processors x 2
       compute devices     ------> | shard |   <----|
       IoTs                ------> | shard |   <----|   EC2 + KCL
                                   |-------|             -> record processors x 2

    -  1 x Kinesis data stream, 4 shards               - 2 x consumers, processing 4 shards
                                                       - 2 x record processors are running on each consumer,
                                                         KCL handles the load balancing betweeen the 2 consumers

  But what about scaling out the consumers?
     - With KCL, generally you should ensure the number of instances does not exceed the number shards
       (except for failure and standby)
     - You never need multiple instances to handle the processing load of 1 shard
     - However, one worker/consumer can process multiple shards

  But what about scaling out the consumers?
    - it's fine if the number of shards exceeds the number of instances
    - don't think that just because your reshard, that you need to add more instances
    - instead, CPU Untilization is what shoud drive the the quantity of consumer instances,
      NOT the number of shards in your Kinesis stream
    - Use auto Scaling group, and base scaling decisions on CPU load on your consumers



  But happens when I reshard?

       Data Producers:              Streams            Consumers
                           ------> |-------|
       EC2                 ------> | shard |   <----|  EC2 + KCL
       mobile devices      ------> | shard |   <----|   -> record processors x 3
       compute devices     ------> | shard |   <----|
       IoTs                ------> | shard |   <----|   EC2 + KCL
                           ------> |-------|             -> record processors x 3

    -  1 x Kinesis data stream, 6 shards               - 2 x consumers, processing 6 shards
                                                       - 3 x record processors are running on each consumer,
                                                         KCL handles the load balancing betweeen the 2 consumers

  Exam Tips

    Kinesis Shards
      - The Kinesis Client Library (KCL) running on your consumers creates a record processor for
        each shard that is being consumed by your instance
      - If you increase the number of shards, the KCL will add more record processors on your consumers
      - CPU utilization is what should drive the quantity of consumer instances you have, not the
        number of shards in your Kinesis stream
      - Use an Auto Scaling Group, and base scaling decisions on CPU load on your consumers

8.11 Introducing Elastic Beanstalk

  Elastic Beanstalk supported platforms
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticbeanstalk/latest/dg/concepts.platforms.html

 What is PaaS (Platform As A Service)?
    - a single-stop application deployment model
    - you bring your code, and provider builds everything for your, deploys your application,
      and manages it going forward

  Elastic Beanstalk
    - the AWS PaaS tool for deploying and scaling web application and services
    Supported Languages
      - Java, .NET, PHP, Node.js, Python, Ruby, & Go
    Supported Platforms
      - Apache Tomcat, Docker Containers
    Developer Benefits
      - Focus on writing code and don't worry about any of the underlying infrastructure needed
        to run the application

                                       Provisions                                Monitoring
           Your Code  ---> Elastic     |----------->  Elastic Load Balancer      CloudTrail
                           Beanstalk   |----------->  EC2  + Apache Tomcat       CloudWatch
                                       |----------->  S3                         Health Dashboards
                                       |----------->  Databases

  What can Elastic Beanstalk handle
     Infrastructure
       - provisioning infrastructure, load balancing, Auto Scaling, and application health monitoring
     Application Platform
      - Installation and management of the application stack, including patching and updates to your
        operating system and application platform
     You are in Control
       - you have complete administrative control of the AWS resources
       - no additional charges for using Elastic Beanstalk

  Lets Developers Develop
    - Developers don't have to be sysadmins
    - you don't need to worry about any of the underlying infrastructure needed to run the application
    - fastest and simplest way to deploy your application in AWS

  Exam Tips

    Elastic Beanstalk
      Deploy and Scale
        - deploy and scales your web applications, including the web application server platform
      Supported Programming Languages
        - Java, .NET, PHP, Node.js, Python, Ruby, & Go
      Managed Platforms
        - Apache Tomcat (for starting Web Applications), Docker (for Containized applications)
      Provision AWS Resources
        - provisions the AWS resources for you, e.g., EC2, RDS, S3, Elastic Load Balancers, Auto Scaling Groups, etc
      Systems Administration
        - OS and application server updates
        - monitoring (intregates with CloudTrail & Cloudwatch), metrics, and health checks are all included
      Administrative Control
        - Can fully manage the EC2 instances for you, or you can take full adminitrative control

---------------------------------------------------
8.12 Updating Elastic Beanstalk


  Deployment Updates Options
    All at Once
      - deploys to all hosts concurrently
    Rolling updates
      - deploys the new version in batches
    Rolling with Additional Batch
      - Launches an additional batch of instances. Then deploys the new version in batches
    Immutable
      - deploys the new version to a fresh group of instances before deleting the old instances
    Traffic Splitting
      - installs the new version of a new set of instances, like an immutable deployment
      - forwards a percentage of incoming client traffic to the new application version for evaluation


  All at Once Deployment
    - deploys to all instances simultaneously
    - you will experience a total outage
    - not ideal for mission-critical production systems

  All at Once Deployment - Rolling Back
    - if update fails, you will need to roll back the changes by re-deploying the original version
      to all all your instances. Resulting in another outage to get back to the previous version

  All at Once Deployment - Use Case
    - only use for development or test environment
    - NOT for a production environment

  Rolling Deployment Policy
    - deploys the new version in batches
    - each batch is taken out of service whil the deployment takes place
    - your environment capacity will be reduced by the number instances in a batch while the
      deployment takes place
    - not ideal for performance sensitive systems
    - maybe fine for a local library website where performance is not critical, but not good for
      big e-commerence website

   Rolling Deployment Policy - Rolling Back
     - if the update fails, you need to perform an additonal rolling update to roll back changes


  Rolling with Additional Batch
    - Elastic Beanstalk launches an additional batch of instances
    - Deploys the new version in batches
    - Maintains full capacity throughout the deployment
    - could be a good option because you will not experience an degraded performance during deployment
      process, BUT rolling back after a failure is going to take some time. so not feasible for 24/7
      critical applications

  Rolling with Additional Batch - Rolling Back
    - if the update fails, you need to perform an additional rolling update to roll back the changes


  Immutable Deployment
    - existing deployment must not be changed
    - deploys the new version to a fresh group of instances
    - only when the new instances pass their health checks, should the old instances be terminated
    - preferred approach for mission critical systems because it does not impact the live environment

  Immutable Deployment - rolling back
    - if a deployment fails, just delete the new instances and make sure your load balancers is pointing
      at the old instances

   Traffic Splitting
     - enables Canary Testing on production environment by directing a small percentage to the new version
     - installs the new version of a new set of instances, like an immutable deployment
     - forwards a percentage of incoming client traffic to the new application version for for a specified
       evaluation period
     - if the new instances stay healthy, Elastic Beanstalk forwards 100% of the traffic to them, and
       terminates the old instances


  Exam Tips

    Elastic Beanstalk Deployment Update Types
      All At Once update
        - involves a service interruption
        - rolling back requires further All at Once update
      Rolling update
        - reduced capacity during deployment
        - rolling back requires a further Rolling Update
        - only suitable for non-performance critical applications (e.g. local library website)
      Rolling with Additional Batch update
        - maintains full capacity
        - rolling back requires a further Rolling Update
        - maintains performance during deployment, so not the best option if you need to roll back quickly
      Immutable update
        - Maintains full capacity
        - to roll back, delete the new instances
        - Preferred option for mission critical production systems
      Traffic Splitting update (newer option)
        - Performs an Immutable deployment and then splits the traffic between the old and new deployment,
          enabling Canary Test
        - Another Preferred option for mission critical production systems
      Blue/Green deployment
        - deploy the new version to a separate Beanstalk environment, and then swap the CNAMEs (via Route 53)
          of the two environments to redirect traffic to the new version instantly.
        -  require that your environment runs independently of your production database, if your application uses one

---------------------------------------------------
8.13 Deploying an Application with Elastic Beanstalk - Demo

Architecture Overview

              upload              Provision                                Web Browser
   PHP Code   ------>  Elastic       |------->  EC2 + Apache HTTP Server   <----------  User
   (zip file)          Beanstalk  ---|------->  Security Group
                                     |------->  Load Balancer

  # Deploying An Application With Elastic Beanstalk - Demo
  # https://learn.acloud.guru/course/aws-certified-developer-associate/learn/d36a1363-452a-8007-e874-54f19b10c0b7/baf22738-24ef-2c52-39e1-9576859a3e64/watch

  # To create your example application, you'll use the 'Create application' console wizard. It creates an Elastic
  # Beanstalk application and launches an environment within it. An environment is the collection of AWS resources
  # required to run your application code.

  # To create a service role for Elastic Beanstalk:

  1. Open the Identity and Access Management (IAM) console.
  2. Click 'Roles'.
  3. Click 'Create role'.
  4. Under 'Use cases for other AWS services', choose 'Elastic Beanstalk', select 'Elastic Beanstalk - Customizable', and click 'Next'.
  5. Click 'Next'.
  6. For 'Role name', type 'CustomServiceRoleForElasticBeanstalk'.
  7. Click 'Create role'.

  # To create an EC2 instance profile for Elastic Beanstalk:

  1. Open the Identity and Access Management (IAM) console.
  2. Click 'Roles'.
  3. Click 'Create role'.
  4. Under 'Common use cases', choose 'EC2'.
  5. Click 'Next'.
  6. Select the 'AWSElasticBeanstalkReadOnly' policy name.
  7. Click 'Next'.
  8. For 'Role name', type 'CustomEC2InstanceProfileForElasticBeanstalk'.
  9. Click 'Create role'.

  # To create an example application:

  1. Open the Elastic Beanstalk console.
  2. Click 'Create application'.
  3. For Application name enter 'Hello Cloud Gurus'.
  4. For Platform, choose 'PHP'.
  5. Under 'Application code', select 'Upload your code', type 'v1' under 'Version label', select 'Local file' > 'Choose file' and upload the 'aws-cda-EBS_Demo_V1.zip' file available under the RESOURCES section of the lesson. Click 'Next'.
  6. For 'Use an existing service role', choose 'CustomServiceRoleForElasticBeanstalk'.
  7. For 'EC2 instance profile', choose CustomEC2InstanceProfileForElasticBeanstalk'.
  8. Click 'Next'.
  9. On the 'Set up networking, database, and tags - optional' page, click 'Next'.
  10. On the 'Configure instance traffic and scaling - optional' page, click 'Next'.
  11. On the 'Configure updates, monitoring, and logging - optional' page, under 'System' choose 'Basic', and under 'Managed platform updates' > 'Managed updates', uncheck the 'Activated' box. Click 'Next'.
  12. The Review page displays a summary of all your choices. Choose 'Submit' at the bottom of the page.
  13. Wait for the 'Successfully launched environment: HelloCloudGurus-env' event and check the URL available under 'Environment overview' > 'Domain'.


 -> Web Browser -> EC2 Public IP -> see Hello Cloud Guru page
     -> Click anywhere -> takes you to: AWS This week - A Cloud Guru

Alternatively:
   -> Elastic Beanstalk -> Environments -> HelloCloudGuru-env -> Domin -> cliik on domain


   -> Console -> Elastic Beanstalk -> Environment: HelloCloudGuru.env -> expand -> configuration

   PHP Code   ------>  Elastic       |------->  EC2 + Apache HTTP Server + PHP   <----------  User

8.15 Advanced Elastic Beanstalk

  Extending Elastic Beanstalk Linux platforms
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticbeanstalk/latest/dg/platforms-linux-extend.html

  Elastic Beanstalk FAQs
  https://aws.amazon.com/elasticbeanstalk/faqs/

  Amazon Linux AMI (AL1) platform retirement FAQ
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticbeanstalk/latest/dg/using-features.migration-al.FAQ.html


  Customizing Elastic Beanstalk
    - You can customize your Elastic Beanstalk environment in a number different ways
      - The configuraion is different for Amazon Linux 1 and Amazon Linux 2
          Note: On July 18, 2022 Elastic Beanstalk retired all platform branches based on Amazon Linux
               AMI (AL1).
  Pre-Amazon Linux 2 (Amazon Linux 1)
    Configuration File
      - Define packages to install, create Linux users, and groups, run shell commands, enable
        services, and connfige load balancers
    YAML or JSON
      - these configuration files can be written in YAML or JSON format
    Constraints
      - the configuration files must have a .config extension and be inside a folder called
        .ebextentions in the top-level directory of your application source code bundle

        example:
          - configures an application health check URL that will be used by a load balancer, which
            will make an HTTP request to the specified path to check if the instances are healthy
        myhealthcheckurl.config example
        {
          "option_settings" :
          [
            {
              "namespace" : "aws:elasticbeanstalk:application",
              "option_name" : "My Application Healthcheck URL",
              "value" : "/healthcheck"
            }
          ]
        }

  Amazon Linux 2
     AWS Elastic Beanstalk Developer Guide:
       "on Amazon Linux 2 platform, we highly recommend that you use 'Buildfile', 'ProcFile', and
       'platform hooks' whenever possible to configure and run custom code on your environment
       instances during instance provisioning"

  Customizing Amazon Linux 2 Environments - Buildfile
     Buildfile
       - for commands that run for short periods, and then exit upon task completion, e.g., running
         a shell script
     Root Directory
       - Create your 'Buildfile' in the 'root' directory of your application source
     Format
       <process_name>:<command>
       make: ./build.sh  <- reference a script in your source code bundle

  Customizing Amazon Linux 2 Environments - Procfile
     Procfile
       - long-running applications processes (e.g. commands to start and run your application)
       - Elastic Beanstalk expects processes defined in the 'Procfile' to run continuously. It monitors
         and restarts any processes that terminate
      Root Directory
        - create a file called 'Procfile' in the 'root' directory of your application source
      Format
        <process_name>:<command>
          web: bin/myserver
          app: bin/myapp

  Customizing Amazon Linux 2 Environments - Platform Hooks
    - custom scripts or executable files that your would like Elastic Beanstalk to run at a choosen
      stage of the EC2 provisioning process
    - scripts are stored in dedicated directories in your application source code, e.g.:
      .platform/hooks/prebuild
         - files that you want Elastic Beanstalk to run before it builds, sets up, and configures the
           application and web server
      .platform/hooks/predeploy
          - files that you want to run after it sets up and configures the application and web server
            but before it deploys them to the final runtime location
      .platform/hooks/postdeploy
          - files that you want to run after Elastic Beanstalk deploys the application
          - the last deployment step


  Exam Tips

    Advanced Elastic Beanstalk
      - you can customize your Elastic Beanstalk environment in a number of ways
      Amazon Linux 1
        the '.ebextenions' folder
          - located in the top-level directory of your application source code bundle
          - files must have a '.config' extension (e.g., myhealthcheckurl.config)
          Note: On July 18, 2022 Elastic Beanstalk retired all platform branches based on Amazon Linux
               AMI (AL1).
      Amazon Linux 2
        Buildfile
          - create a 'Buildfile' in the root directory of your application source for commands that
            exit upon completion (e.g. shell script)
        Procfile
          - Create a 'Procfile' for long-running processes (e.g. custom commands to start your application)
        Platform Hooks
          - custom scripts or executable files that your would like Elastic Beanstalk to run at a choosen
            stage of the EC2 provisioning process
               .platform/hooks/prebuild
               .platform/hooks/predeploy
               .platform/hooks/postdeploy

8.16 RDS & Elastic Beanstalk

  Integrating RDS With Elastic Beanstalk
    - Elastic Beanstalk supports 2 ways of integrating an RDS database with your Beanstalk environment
       1. Deploy RDS inside Elastic Beanstalk
       2. Deploy RDS outside Elastic Beanstalk

  Option 1: Launch RDS Within Elastic Beanstalk
    - launch the RDS instance from within the Elastic Beanstalk console
    - it is created within your Elastic Beanstalk environment
    - if you terminate the environment, the RDS database will also be terminated
    - it is a good option for Dev and Test deployments, not so good for Prod

  Option 2: Launch RDS outside Elastic Beanstalk
    - don't use Elastic Beanstalk to create your RDS database
    - instead, use the RDS console or AWS CLI
    - it allows your to tear down your application environment without affecting the
      database instance
    - this is the preferred approach for Production systems

  Connecting to an Outside Database
    1. Add an additional Security Group to your environment's Auto Scaling Group
       to allow our Elastic Beanstalk EC2 instances connect to our databases
       (e.g. port 3306 for MySQL)
    2. Provide connection string information to your application servers using Elastic Beanstalk
       environment properties
       e.g.: Connection String:
       mysql-instance1.<account#>.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com  <- Amazon Resource Name for RDS DB
       DB password: <password>

       -> Console -> Elastic Beanstalk -> select Beanstalk-env -> Configuration <left tab> ->
         -> Category: Software -> Edit -> Environment Properties
         e.g.
         Name: RDS_HOSTNAME  Value:  mysql-instance1.<account#>.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com


  Exam Tips

    RDS and Elastic Beanstalk
      - Two options for Deploying RDS When Working with Elastic beanstalk
      RDS inside Beanstalk
        - Quick and easy to add your database and get started
        - When you terminate the Elastic Beanstalk environment, the database will also be terminated
        - Suitable for Dev and Test environments only
      RDS outside Beanstalk
        - you can tear down the application stack without impacting the database
        - Additonal configuration steps required - Security Group, and Connection information as
          Beanstalk environment properties
        - the connection string/information would include the RDS Amazon resource Name and DB Password

            mysql-instance1.<account#>.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com  <- Amazon Resource Name for RDS DB
            DB password: <password>
        - Suitable for Production Environments because it gives you the flexibility to tear down your
          beanstalk environment without loosing the RDS database

   Windows Web Application Migration Assistant for AWS Elastic Beanstalk (github respository)

  Scenario
    - Imagine your have a '.NET' application running on a Windows Server in your own data center

        load balancing, auto scaling, application health monitoring, and apply patches/updates
    Interactive PowerShell Utility
      - enables you to migrate a '.NET' application, or entire website from Windows servers

    Open Source

        https://github.com/awslabs/windows-web-app-migration-assistant


  Exam Tips

      Windows Web Application Migration Assistant For Elastic Beanstalk
        - Formerly named '.NET Migration Assitant'
        - Open-source, interactive PowerShell utility that migrates .NET applications from on-premise
          Windows servers to Elastic Beanstalk

8.18 Other AWS Services Summary

  Simple Queue Service (SQS)
    - SQS is a distributed message queuing system
    - allows us to decouple components of an application so that they are independent
    - pull-based (via polling), not pushed based

  SQS Queue Types
    Standard Queue
      - Best-effort ordering
      - Messages delivered at least once
      - Occasional duplicates
      - the default queue type
    FIFO Queue
      - First-In-First-Out message order is strickly preserved
      - messages are delivered once
      - no duplicates
      - good for banking transactions which need to happen in a strick order
      message group ID (MessageGroupId)
        - ensures messages in FIFO queue are processed one by one
        - messages the belong to the same message group are always processed one by one,
          in a strict order relative to the message group (however, messages that below to
          a different message group might be processed out of order)
      message deduplication ID (MessageDeduplicationId)
        - If a message with a particular message deduplication ID is sent successfully, any messages sent
          with the same message deduplication ID are accepted successfully but aren't delivered during the
          5-minute deduplication interval
      max received messages
        - When you request messages from a queue, you can't specify which messages to retrieve. Instead, you
          specify the maximum number of messages (up to 10) that you want to retrieve

  SQS Settings

    Visibility Timeout
    - Visibility timeout default is 30 sec, min is 0 sec, max is 12 hours
    - the amount of time that the message is invisible in the SQS queue after a reader
      picks up the message
    - Increase it if the task takes more than 30 seconds

   Short Polling
     - a response is returned immediately even if no messages are in the queue
     - there is a cost per response

   Long Polling
     - long polling:  check for message, if no message, Wait for a wait time period (max 20 sec)
       of time before disconnecting
     - most cost effective
     - in most cases, you should use long polling

  SQS Standard Queue handling message deliver more than once
    - If you are receiving multiple messages with the same message ID, you can use DynamoDB to record
      the message IDs of messages that were already successfully processed and have your Lambda function
      check the DynamoDB table before processing each message.

  SQS Delay Queues and Large Messages
    SQS Delay Queues
      - postpone delivery of new messages
      - messages in Delay Queues remain invisible for the duration of the delivery delay period (0 - 900 sec)
      - Large distributed applications which may need to introduce a delay in processing

     Large SQS Messages
       - Large SQS Message (256KB - 2GB)
       - Use S3 to Store large SQS messages - 256 KB to 2GB
      You need:
        - AWS SDK for Java provides an API for S3 operations (e.g. GET and DELETE)
        - SQS Extended Client library for Java to manage the large messages
        - An S3 bucket
      Cannot use:
        - AWS CLI, AWS Management Console / SQS, SQS API, Any other AWS SDK

        SQS + SQS Extended  +   AWS SDK   ----------------> S3
              Client Library    For Java
              for Java

   SQS CLI                  Coresponding
   Command                  SQS API Call                  Usage
   --------------           -----------              ------------------------------------------------------------------
   add-permission           AddPermission            Adds permission for a specific principal to a queue. It enables queue
                                                        access to be shared
   change-message-Visbility ChangeMessageVisibility  Sets a new value for the visibility timeout of a specified message
   create-queue             CreateQueue              Creates a new FIFO or standard queue
   delete-message           DeleteMessage            Removes a specified message from the specified queue
   delete-queue             DeleteQueue              Deletes the queue specified by the QueueUrl, regardless of its content
   get-queue-url            GetQueueUrl              Returns the URL of an existing Amazon SQS queue (input: Queue Name)
   receive-message          ReceiveMessage           Enables the specified queue to be used to retrieve one or more messages
                                                      (up to ten). Long-poll support is enabled by the WaitTimeSeconds parameter.
   send-message             SendMessage              Sends a message to the queue specified
   set-queue-attributes     SetQueueAttributes       Sets the value of one or more queue attributes (e.g. DelaySeconds, Policy,
                                                       ReceiveMessageWaitTimeSeconds, VisibilityTimeout, etc)

  Simple Notfication Service (SNS)
    Push Notifications
      - scalable and highly available notfication service which allows us to send
        push notification from the cloud
    Message Formats
      - SMS text messages, email, SQS queues, or any HTTP endpoint
    Pub-Sub
      - publish-subscrber (pub-sub) model whereby user/consumers must subscribe to topics,
      - pushd mechanism, rather than a pull (or poll) mechanism
    Fanout
      - can fanout messages to a large number of recipients (e.g. to multiple SQS queues, HTTP
        endpoints, and email addresses)
    SNS Message Filtering
      - By default, an Amazon SNS topic subscriber receives every message that's published to the topic.
      - To receive only a subset of the messages, a subscriber must assign a filter policy to the topic subscription.
      - A filter policy is a JSON object containing properties that define which messages the subscriber receives

  Simple Email Service (SES)
    - Great for promotional and marketing emails
    Email Only
    - SES is only for email
    Send and Receive Emails
      - SES can be used for incoming and outgoing email
    Not Subscription Based
      - It is not subscription based; you only need to known the email address
    Email Only


  SNS vs SQS
    SNS
      - messaging service
      - SNS is push based
      - Think push notification
    SQS
      - messaging service
      - SQS is pull based
      - think polling the queue for messages


  SES vs SNS
    SES (Simple Email Service)
      - SES is email only
      Send and receive emails
        - SES can be used for incoming and outgoing email
      Not Subscription-Based
        - it is not subscription-based, you only need to know the email addess (where to send the emails)
    SNS (Simple Notification Service)
      Multiple Formats
        - Pub/sub messaging service; formats include SMS, HTTP, SQS, and email
      Push Notification
        - push notifications only. Not for receiving notifications
      Pub/Sub model
        - publish / subscribe model
        - consumers must subscribe to a topic to receive the notifications
       Fanout
         - can fanout messages to a large number of recipients (e.g. to multple SQS queues,
           HTTP endpoints, and email addresses


  Kinesis 101

    Kinesis Streams
      - Capture and store streaming videos and data for real-time processing and analysis
      - Consumer application process and analyze the data in real time

    Kinesis Firehose [aka Kinesis Data Firehose]
      - capture, transform, and load data continuously into AWS Data stores (or other service
        providers, such as Datadog or Splunk)
      - Existing BI (Business Intelligence) applications and tools can be used for near real-time
        analysis of the stored data
      - the Kinesis data ingestion cost along with the per hour shards cost would be more than the corresponding
        cost incurred while using Kinesis Firehose.
      Purpose
        - data transfer tool to get information to S3, Redshift, Elasticsearch, or Splunk
        - limited endpoints that you can send the data to
      Kinesis Data Firehost Architecture
        - handles the scaling for you
        - handles building out the consumer for you
        - limited 3rd party endpoint support (e.g. Splunk)

                                    Optional
                   Kinesis          Transform       | Elasticsearch / opensearch
          input -> Data Firehose ->| Bult-in     -->| S3
                                   | Lambda         | Redshift
                                                    | Splunk

    Kinesis Analytics
      - Real-time analytics using standard SQL on data received by Kinesis Data Streams and Kinesis
        Data Firehose
      - Stores the processed data in AWS data stores (e.g., S3, Redshift, or OpenSearch [formerly
        / derived from Elastic Search])


  Kinesis Shards
    - Kinesis Streams are made up of shards
    - Each shard is a sequence of one or more data records and provides a fixed unit of capacity
    - the data capacity of the stream is determined by the number of shards
    - if the data rate increases, you can increast the capacity on your stream by increasing the
      number of shards

  Kinesis Shards and Consumers
    - The Kinesis Client Library (KCL) running on your consumers creates a record processor for
      each shard that is being consumed by your instance
    - If you increase the number of shards, the KCL will add more record processors on your consumers
    - Generally, the number of consumer instances should not exceed the number of shards (except for
      failover purposes)
    - An increase in the number of shards does not require an increase in the number of consumers
    - Use an Auto Scaling Group and drive scaling actions for your EC2 consumer instances based on the
      CPU utilization
    - CPU utilization is what should drive the quantity of consumer instances you have, not the
      number of shards in your Kinesis stream
    - Use an Auto Scaling Group, and base scaling decisions on CPU load on your consumers

  Elastic Beanstalk
    Deploy and Scale
      - deploy and scales your web applications, including the web application server platform
      - Elastic Beanstalk uses AWS CloudFormation to launch the resources in your environment and
        propagate configuration changes.
    Supported Programming Languages
      - Java, .NET, PHP, Node.js, Python, Ruby, & Go
    Managed Platforms
      - Apache Tomcat (for starting Web Applications), Docker (for Containized applications)
    Provision AWS Resources
      - provisions the AWS resources for you, e.g., EC2, RDS, S3, Elastic Load Balancers, Auto Scaling Groups, etc
    Systems Administration
      - OS and application server updates
      - monitoring (intregates with CloudTrail & Cloudwatch), metrics, and health checks are all included
    Administrative Control
      - Can fully manage the EC2 instances for you, or you can take full adminitrative control
    Upgrade to new version
      - Bundle your update application code, Dockerfile (if using docker) into a zip file, then upload and deploy it using the
        Elastic Beanstalk console.
    AWS Elastic Beanstalk supports the following languages and development stacks:
     https://github.com/Kalpesh14m/AWS-Learning-With-Elastic-BeanStalk
     - Apache Tomcat for Java applications
     - Apache HTTP Server for PHP applications
     - Apache HTTP Server for Python applications
     - Nginx or Apache HTTP Server for Node.js applications
     - Passenger or Puma for Ruby applications
     - Microsoft IIS 7.5, 8.0, and 8.5 for .NET applications
     - Java SE
     - Docker

  Elastic Beanstalk Deployment Update Types
    All At Once update
      - involves a service interruption
      - rolling back requires further All at Once update
    Rolling update
      - reduced capacity during deployment
      - rolling back requires a further Rolling Update
      - only suitable for non-performance critical applications (e.g. local library website)
    Rolling with Additional Batch update
      - maintains full capacity
      - rolling back requires a further Rolling Update
      - maintains performance during deployment, so not the best option if you need to roll back quickly
    Immutable update
      - Maintains full capacity
      - to roll back, delete the new instances
      - Preferred option for mission critical production systems
    Traffic Splitting update (newer option)
      - Performs an Immutable deployment and then splits the traffic between the old and new deployment,
        enabling Canary Test
      - Another Preferred option for mission critical production systems
    Blue/Green deployment
      - deploy the new version to a separate Beanstalk environment, and then swap the CNAMEs (via Route 53)
        of the two environments to redirect traffic to the new version instantly.
      -  require that your environment runs independently of your production database, if your application uses one

  Elastic Beanstalk Worker Environments
    Long running tasks
    - If your Elastic Beanstalk application performs operations or workflows that take a long time to complete,
      you can offload those tasks to a dedicated worker environment.
    - Decoupling your web application front end from a process that performs blocking operations is a common way
      to ensure that your application stays responsive under load
    repetivtive task
      - With periodic tasks, you can also configure the worker daemon to queue messages based on a cron schedule
      - For a worker environment, you need a cron.yaml file to define the cron jobs and do repetitive tasks

  Elastic Beanstalk running multiple versions
    - You can create and manage separate environments for development, testing, and production use, and you can
      deploy any version of your application to any environment. Environments can be long-running or temporary.
    - When you terminate an environment, you can save its configuration to recreate it later.
    - If you add another environment to your application you can simply deploy by using:
           eb deploy <environment-name>

  Elastic Beanstalk Saving Configurations
    - You can save your Beanstalk environment's configuration as an object in S3 that can be applied to other environments
      during environment creation, or applied to a running environment.
    - Saved configurations are YAML formatted templates that define an environment's platform version, tier, configuration
      option settings, and tags.

  RDS and Elastic Beanstalk
    - Two options for Deploying RDS When Working with Elastic beanstalk
    RDS inside Beanstalk
      - Quick and easy to add your database and get started
      - When you terminate the Elastic Beanstalk environment, the database will also be terminated
      - Suitable for Dev and Test environments only
      - The only way  only way to recover the database data before it gets deleted by Elastic Beanstalk
        is to make a snapshot of the database before it gets deleted
    RDS outside Beanstalk
      - you can tear down the application stack without impacting the database
      - Additonal configuration steps required - Security Group, and Connection information as
        Beanstalk environment properties
      - the connection string/information would include the RDS Amazon resource Name and DB Password

          mysql-instance1.<account#>.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com  <- Amazon Resource Name for RDS DB
          DB password: <password>
      - Suitable for Production Environments because it gives you the flexibility to tear down your
        beanstalk environment without loosing the RDS database

   Elastic Beanstalk application version lifecycle policy
     - A lifecycle policy tells Elastic Beanstalk to delete application versions that are old, or to delete
       application versions when the total number of versions for an application exceeds a specified number
     - Elastic Beanstalk applies an application's lifecycle policy each time you create a new application
        version, and deletes up to 100 versions each time the lifecycle policy is applied

   Running X-ray daemon on Elastic Beanstalk EC2's
     - To relay trace data from your application to X-Ray, you can run the X-Ray daemon on your Elastic Beanstalk environment's EC2 instances.
     - Elastic Beanstalk platforms provide a configuration option that you can set to run the x-ray daemon automatically.
     - You can enable the x-ray daemon in a configuration file in your source code or by choosing an option in the Elastic Beanstalk console.
     - When you enable the configuration option, the daemon is installed on the instance and runs as a service.

8.19 AWS Certified Developer - Associate - Other AWS Services Quiz


Question 9

Your EC2 instances download jobs from an SQS queue. However, they are taking too long to process the
messages. What API call can you use to extend the length of time to process the jobs?

Choices:
 - SetMessageVisibility
 - ExtendMessageTime
 - AlterMessageTime
 - ChangeMessageVisibility   <- Correct Answer

Correct Answer

Changes the visibility timeout of a specified message in a queue to a new value. The maximum allowed
timeout value is 12 hours.


Question 17

What is the maximum visibility timeout of an SQS message in a queue?

Choices:
 - 14 days
 - 12 hours  <- Correct Answer
 - 1 hour
 - 1 day

While a message is being processed it is hidden from other Workers. This can be set or reset to a
maximum of 12 hours

Question 27

What languages and development stacks are not currently supported by AWS Elastic Beanstalk?

Choices:
 - Jetty for JBoss applications               <- Correct Answer
 - Apache HTTP Server for PHP applications
 - Passenger for Ruby applications
 - Apache Tomcat for Java applications

  Chapter 9 Developer Theory

9.1 What is CI/CD

  CI/CD Whitepaper: downloaded to:
  what_is_ci_cd_lesson_9_1/practicing-continuous-integration-continuous-delivery.pdf

  What is CI/CD
    Software Development Best Practice
      - Continuous Integration, Continuous Delivery / Deployment
    Make small Changes & Automate Everything
      - Small, incremental code changes.
      - Automate as much as possible, e.g., code integration, build, test, and deployment
    Why is CI/CD so Cool?
      - modern companies like AWS, Netflix, Google, and Facebook have pioneered this approach to
        releasing code, successfully applying thousands of changes per day

  Benefits of the CI/CD Approach
    Automation is Good
      - Fast, repeatable, scalable, enables rapid deployment
    Manual is Bad!
      - Slow, error prone, inconsistent, unscalable, complex
    Small Changes
      - test each code change and catch bugs while they are small and simple to fix

  Continuous Integration Workflow
    Shared Code Repository
      - multiple developers contributing to a shared code repository like Git
      - Frequenently mergeing or integrating code updates - on a min of a daily basis
    Automated Build
      - when changes appear in the code repository, this triggers an automated build of the new code
    Automated Test
      - run automated tests to check the code locally before it is committed into the master code repository

  Continuous Delivery & Deployment Workflow
    Code is Merged
      - After successful tests, the code gets merged to the master repository
    Prepared for Deployment
      - Code is built, tested and packaged for deployment
    Manual Decision
      - Humans may be involved in the decision to deploy the code
      - This is known as 'Continuous Delivery'
    Or Fully Automated
      - the system automatically deploys the new code as soon as it has been prepared for deployment
      - This is know as 'Continuous Deployment'

  AWS Developer Tools
    CodeCommit
      - Source & version control
      - Source control service enabling teams to collaborate on code, html pages, scripts, images
        and binaries
      - fully managed private Git repository
    CodeBuild
      - Automated Build
      - Compiles source code, runs tests, and produces packages that are ready to deploy
    CodeDeploy
      - Automated Deployment
      - Automates code deployments to any instance, including EC2, Lambda, and on-premise
    CodePipeline
      - manages the workflow
      - end-to-end solution, build, test, and deploy your application every time there is a code change

 Exam Tips


    What is CI/CD
      Continuous Integration
        - integrating or merging the code changes frequently - at least once per day
        - Think CodeCommit (a privately managed Git repository)
      Continuous Delivery
        - automating teh build, test, and deployment functions
        - Think CodeBuild and CodeDeploy
      Continuous Deployment
        - Fully automated release process, code is deployed into Staging or Production as soon as
          it has successfully passed through the release pipeline
        - Think CodePipeline

  AWS Whitepaper
    - Practicing Continuous Integration & Continuous Deployment on AWS
       - explains the features and benefits of using continuous integration, continuous delivery (CI/CD)
         and AWS tooling in your software development enviroment

  CI/CD Whitepaper: downloaded to:
  what_is_ci_cd_lesson_9_1/practicing-continuous-integration-continuous-delivery.pdf
OR
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/whitepapers/latest/practicing-continuous-integration-continuous-delivery/welcome.html   -> click on "PDF"

9.2 CodeCommit 101

  CodeCommit is A Central Code Repository
    CodeCommit Repository stores:
      - Source code, binaries, images, libraries, etc
      - manages updates from multiple sources (e.g. developers)
      - tracks and manages code changes
      - maintains version history

  Using CodeCommit

     |------------------------    User1
     |CodeCommit Repository  |     -> Creates a Dev Branch
     |                       |     -> Create a local clone of Dev Branch
     |Master      Dev        |     -> Makes changes and tests them locally
     |Branch      Branch     |     -> Merges changes to Dev Branch
     |soure code  source code|     -> If no conflicts, merge Dev Branch with Master Branch
     |libraries   libraries  |
     |Images      Images     |    User2
     |binaries    binaries   |      -> working on the same file as user1 which creates a merge conflict
     -------------------------      -> Merge conflict must be resolved before file can be checked in

  Exam Tips

    CodeCommit 101
      Centralized Code Repository
        - A place to store source code, binaries, libraries, images, HTML files, etc.
        - a fully managed private Git repository
      Enables Collaroration
        - manages updates from multiple users
      Version Control
        - tracks and managed code changes
        - maintains version historyo

9.3 CodeCommit Demo

  CodeCommit Demo Objectives
    Note: Must do in own AWS account - some steps do not work in Open Cloud Sandbox
    1. Create a CodeCommit Repository
      - add a file to the repository

      -> Console -> CodeCommit -> Create Repository -> Repository Name: ccrepo1 -> Create

      ->  CodeCommit -> Repositories -> select "ccrepo1" -> Add file -> Create file ->
         content: "This is my first file in the repo"
         file name: file1.txt, Author: Pat, email: <email>, Commit Message: 1st file checkin
          -> Commit Changes

      Notes:
         - Can connect to your repository using either HTTPS or SSH

    2. Create a new branch
      - a working area in the repository

      ->  CodeCommit -> Repositories -> select "ccrepo1" -> Branches <left tab> -> Create Branch
         -> Branch Name: mybranch, Branch from: main -> Create Branch

    3. Update the file
      - Merge the Changes

         -> In "mybranch" branch -> Edit "file1.txt"
         add line: My branch 1 update , Author: Pat, email: <email>, Commit Message: my 1st branch file1 update
           -> Commit change

      ->  CodeCommit -> Repositories -> select "ccrepo1" -> Branches <left tab> -> Create pull Request ->
          Destination: main, Source: mybranch -> Compare ->
          Title: Merging Mybranch -> Create pull request -> Merge
            select "Delete source branch mybranch after merging?"

      -> Branches ->  "mybranch" has been deleted, and "file1.txt" contains mybranch change

   Note: Merge Strategy:
       Fast forward merge
         git merge --ff-only
         Merges the branches and moves the destination branch pointer to the tip of the source branch.
         This is the default merge strategy in Git.
        Squash and merge
          git merge --squash
          Combines all commits from the source branch into a single merge commit in the destination branch.
        3-way merge
          git merge --no-ff
          Creates a merge commit and adds individual source commits to the destination branch.

9.4 Configure and work with CodeCommit from CLI

About this lab
In this hands-on lab, you go through the process of configuring a CodeCommit repository from the AWS Command Line
Interface. You also go through some key prerequisite steps, such as installing Git, configuring HTTPS credentials,
and preparing the environment to work with Git and CodeCommit. This lab covers the steps that need to be taken
any time you work with CodeCommit from the CLI.

Learning objectives
 - Create a Server from an Amazon Linux 2 AMI
 - Install Git and Create IAM CodeCommit Credentials
 - Create a CodeCommit Repository
 - Clone the Repo
 - Test the Repo

----------
Create a Server from an Amazon Linux 2 AMI

    Navigate to the EC2 dashboard.
    Click on the Launch Instance dropdown menu > Launch Instance.
    Under Name and tags, name the instance webserver.
    Under Application and OS Images (Amazon Machine Image), ensure the Amazon Linux AWS tile is selected and Amazon Linux 2 AMI is selected from the dropdown menu directly underneath the tile.
    Under Instance type, click on the dropdown menu and select t3.micro.
    Under Key pair (login) > Key pair name, click on the dropdown menu and select Proceed without a key pair (Not recommended).
    Under Network Settings, click Edit.
    Under Auto-assign public IP, click on the dropdown menu and select Enable.
    Leave the other settings at default, and click Launch instance.
    Once the instance is launched, click on the instance ID to navigate to the instance; ensure the Instance state is running.

        Note: It my take a few minutes for the instance to deploy.

Install Git and Create IAM CodeCommit Credentials

    Click on the checkbox next to webserver and, in the upper right, click Connect.

    Click Connect again.

    Once connected to the instance, update the server:
    sudo yum update -y

    Install Git:
    sudo yum install git -y

    Return to the the AWS Management Console.

    Navigate to IAM (don't worry about the errors).

    In the left navigation menu, under Access management, click Users.

    Click on cloud_user.

    In the Permissions tab under Policy name, note the you already have a policy granting permission to work with CodeCommit.

    Click Add permissions > Add permissions

    Under Permission options, select Attach policies directly.

    Type CodeCommit in the filter search box, and take note of the AWSCodeCommitFullAccess policy.

    Click Cancel. (We are clicking cancel here because the cloud_user already has the needed permissions, and the lab's cloud_user account cannot be modified).

Generate an Access Key

    Click the Security credentials tab.
    Under Access keys, click Create access key.
    Under Access key best practices & alternatives, select Command Line Interface (CLI).
    Scroll down and click on the checkbox next to I understand the above recommendation and want to proceed to create an access key.
    Click Next.
    Click Create access key.
    Copy the access key and secret access key, and save them to a separate text file; you'll need them later.
    Click Done, then click Continue on the pop-up.

Generate HTTPS Git Credentials for AWS CodeCommit

    Under HTTPS Git credentials for AWS CodeCommit, click Generate credentials.
    In the Generate credentials pop-up, copy the user name and password and save them to a separate text file; you'll need them later.
    Click Close.

Create a CodeCommit Repository

    Return to the terminal window.

    Configure the AWS CLI credentials:
    aws configure

    When prompted, enter the AWS access key, then the secret access key you copied earlier.

    For the Default region name, enter us-east-1.

    For the Default output format, enter json.

    Run the following command to create a CodeCommit repository from the AWS CLI:
    aws codecommit create-repository --repository-name RepoFromCLI

Clone the Repo

    Back in the AWS console, In the search bar at the top, enter CodeCommit; right-click on it and open in a new tab.

    Click on the RepoFromCLI repository link to open it.

    Click Add file.

    Click Upload file.

    Click Choose file.

    Select any small file from your machine you don't mind uploading.

    Enter your name under Author name and your email under Email address.

    Click Commit changes.

    Return to your terminal window.

        Note: If your connection timed out, go back to the Connect to instance browser tab and click Connect again.

    Back in the CodeCommit browser tab, click on Repositories in the breadcrumb trail.

    Under Clone URL, copy the HTTPS hyperlink.

    Return to your terminal window.

    Run the following command to clone the repository to the server:
    git clone <PASTE_THE_URL>

    For the username prompt, enter the Git credentials username you copied earlier.

    For the password prompt, enter the Git credentials password you copied earlier.

    Check the clone:
    ls

    Go into the repo directory:
    cd RepoFromCLI/

    Confirm the file you uploaded earlier is in the repo:
    ls

Test the Repo

    Run the following command to create a local text file:
    vim test.txt

    Press i to enter insert mode and type the text:
    This is a test file.

    Press the Escape key, type :wq!, and press Enter.

    Confirm the test.txt file was created:
    ls

    Add the file to Git:
    git add test.txt

    Commit the file to the local repository:
    git commit -m "added test.txt"

    Verify the file was committed:
    git log

    Push the change to the CodeCommit repository:
    git push -u origin main

    Enter the Git credentials username you copied earlier for the username prompt.

    Enter the Git credentials password you copied earlier for the password prompt.

    Back in the CodeCommit browser tab, refresh the view of the RepoFromCLI repository and verify the
----------
https://git-codecommit.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/v1/repos/RepoFromCLI

9.5 CodeDeploy 101

  CodeDeploy
    Automated Deployment Service
      - works with EC2 instances, on-premises, & lambda
      - Quickly release new features
      - Avoid downtie during deployments
      - Avoid the risks associated with manual deployments

  CodeDeploy Deployment Approaches
    In-Place Deployment
      - The application is stopped on each instance and then the new release is installed
      - Also known as a Rolling Update
    Blue / Green deployment
      - new instances are provisioned and the new release is installed on the new instances
      - Blue represents the active deployment, green is the new release

  Example In-Place Deployment
     - have 3 EC2 instances running v1.0 behind a Application Load Balancer
     steps:
      - The application is stopped on the 1st instance
         - The instance will be out of service during the deployment so the capacity is reduced
         - you should configure your Elastic Load Balancer to stop sending requests to this instance!
      - CodeDeploy installs the new version (v2.0), known as the 'Revision'
      - The instance comes back into service
      - CodeDeploy continues to deploy to the next instance

  How To Roll Back In-Place Deployment?
    - If you change your mind, there's no quick fix
    - with an in-place deployment, you'll need to re-deploy the previous version which can
      be time consuming

  Example Blue/Green Deployment
     - have 3 EC2 instances running v1.0 behind a Application Load Balancer where blue represents
       the current version of our application
     Steps:
       - CodeDeploy Provisions 3 new Instances (Green represent the new 'revision' environment)
       - The new 'Revision' is deployed to the Green environment
       - The Green instances are registered with the Elastic Load Balancer
       - Traffic is routed away from the old enviornment
       - Blue Environment is eventually terminated

  How To Roll Back Blue/Green Deployment?
    - With Blue/Green, it's easy
      - Just set the Load Balancer to direct the traffic back to the original [Blue] environment
        - re-register the blue instances with the load balancer and de-register the green instances
      - only works if you didn't already terminate your old environment

  Exam Tips

  CodeDeploy 101
    CodeDeploy Deployment Approaches
      In-Place Deployment
        - The application is stopped on each instance and then the new release is installed
        - Also known as a Rolling Update
        - Capacity is reduced during the deployment
        - Lambda is not supported
        - Rolling back involves a re-deploy
        - Great when deploying the first time
      Blue/Green deployment
        - new instances are provisioned and the new release is installed on the new instances
        - Blue represents the active deployment, green is the new release
        - No capacity reduction
        - Green instances can be created ahead of time
        - Easy to switch between old and new
        - you pay for 2 environments until you terminate the old [blue] servers


---------------------------------------------------
9.6 The CodeDeploy AppSpec File

  Configuring CodeDeploy Using the AppSpec File
    Configuration file
      - Defines the parameters to be used during CodeDeploy deployment
    EC2
      - For EC2 and on-premise systems, YAML format only
    Lambda
      - YAML and JSON supported
      - File structure depends on whether you are deploying to Lambda or EC2

  EC2 AppSpec File Structure
    - 4 significant file sections:
    Version
      - reserved for future use
      - currently the only allowed value is '0.0'
    OS
      - operating system version
      - The OS version you are using, e.g. Linux, windows
    Files
      - Configuration files, packages
      - the location of any application files that need to be copied and where they should
        be copied to
    hooks
      - lifecycle event hooks
      - scripts which need to run at set points in the deployment lifecycle
      - hooks have a very specific run order
      - The hooks section for an EC2/On-Premises deployment contains mappings that link deployment
        lifecycle event hooks to one or more scripts.
      - The hooks section for a Lambda deployment specifies Lambda validation functions to run during a
        deployment lifecycle event.

  Scripts You Might Run During A [EC2] Deployment
    unzip files
      - unzip application files prior to deployment
    run test
      - run functional tests on a newly deployed application
    deal with load balancing
      - de-register and re-register instances with a load balancer

  Example EC2 appspec.yml
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/codedeploy/latest/userguide/reference-appspec-file-example.html

    version: 0.0
    os: linux                                            <---- Operating System
    files:
      - source: Config/config.txt                        <----- Files to copy during the deployment
        destination: /webapps/Config
      - source: Source
        destination: /webapps/myApp
    hooks:                                               <------ Scripts you want run at each stage of the deployment
      BeforeInstall:
        - location: Scripts/UnzipResourceBundle.sh
        - location: Scripts/UnzipDataBundle.sh
      AfterInstall:
        - location: Scripts/RunResourceTests.sh
          timeout: 180
      ApplicationStart:
        - location: Scripts/RunFunctionalTests.sh
          timeout: 3600
      ValidateService:
        - location: Scripts/MonitorService.sh
          timeout: 3600
          runas: codedeployuser

  Typical Folder Setup
    - create a directory containing all the files needed for the revision/new version of your application
      containing everything you need for the deployment
    - the directory contains:
        appspec.yml <-- must be placed in the root of the directory of your revision else deployment will fail
        /Scripts    <-- Scripts be run directory
        /Config     <-- Any configuration files directory
        /Source     <-- Application source code directory

  Exam Tips

    CodeDeploy AppSpec File
      Configuration file
        - Defines the parameters to be used during CodeDeploy deployment, e.g. OS, files, hooks
      appspec.yml
        - should be saved in the root of your revision
        - revision folder contains:
           appspec.yml <-- must be placed in the root of the directory of your revision else deployment will fail
           /Scripts    <-- Scripts be run directory
           /Config     <-- Any configuration files directory
           /Source     <-- Application source code directory
      hooks
        - lifecycle event hooks have a very specific run order
        - The hooks section for an EC2/On-Premises deployment contains mappings that link deployment
          lifecycle event hooks to one or more scripts.
        - The hooks section for a Lambda deployment specifies Lambda validation functions to run during a
          deployment lifecycle event.

---------------------------------------------------
9.7 CodeDeploy Lifecycle Event Hooks


  Example EC2 appspec.yml
     https://docs.aws.amazon.com/codedeploy/latest/userguide/reference-appspec-file-example.html

    version: 0.0
    os: linux
    files:
      - source: Config/config.txt
        destination: /webapps/Config
      - source: source
        destination: /webapps/myApp
    hooks:
      BeforeInstall:                                     <-----|- Lifecycle event hooks are run in a specific order
        - location: Scripts/UnzipResourceBundle.sh             |
        - location: Scripts/UnzipDataBundle.sh                 |
      AfterInstall:                                      <-----|
        - location: Scripts/RunResourceTests.sh                |
          timeout: 180                                         |
      ApplicationStart:                                  <-----|
        - location: Scripts/RunFunctionalTests.sh              |
          timeout: 3600                                        |
      ValidateService:                                   <-----|
        - location: Scripts/MonitorService.sh
          timeout: 3600
          runas: codedeployuser

   Lifecycle Event Hooks Phases for In-Place Deployment:
     Phase 1
       - De-register instances from a Load Balancer
     Phase 2
       - The real nuts & boths of the application deployment
     Phase 3
       - Re-register instances with the Load Balancer

    Lifecycle Event Hooks Run Order for an In-Place Deployment
      Phase 1:
        BeforeBlockTraffic
          - Tasks your want to run on instances 'before' they deregistered from a load balancer
        BlockTraffic
          - De-register instances from a load balancer
        AfterBlockTraffic
          - Tasks you want to run on instances 'after' they are deregistered from a Load Balancer
      Phase 2:
        ApplicationStop
          - Gracefully stop the application
        DownloadBundle
          - CodeDeploy agent copies the application revision files to a temporary location
        BeforeInstall
          - pre-installation scripts, e.g. backing up the current revision, decrypting files
        Install
          - copy application revision files to final location
        AfterInstall
          - post-install scripts, e.g. configuration file, permissions
        ApplicationStart
          - start any services that were stopped during ApplictionStop
        ValidateService
          - run tests to validate the service
      Phase 3:
        BeforeAllowTraffic
          - Tasks your want to run on instances 'before' they registered with a load balancer
        AllowTraffic
          - Register instances with a load balancer
        AfterAllowTraffic
          - Tasks you want to run on instances 'after' they are registered with a Load Balancer

  Exam Tips

    CodeDeploy Lifecycle Event Hooks
      Run Order
        - Lifecycle event hooks are run in a specific order known as the 'Run Order'
      In-Place Deployments
        Broadly 3 Phases:
          de-registering from a load balancer, installation, and re-registering with a Load Balancer
      Logical Flow
        - For the exam you'll need to understand the logical flow
        e.g. Installation Phase 2:
          ApplicationStop -> DownloadBundle -> BeforeInstall -> Install -> AfterInstall ->
              Application Start -> ValidateService

9.8 Deploying an Application Using CodeDeploy Lab

About this lab
In this hands-on lab, we'll deploy a simple web application to EC2 instances using CodeDeploy.

Learning objectives
 - Download the Application Code
 - Create an S3 Bucket
 - Deploy the Code Using CodeDeploy

Introduction

The following components have been pre-configured for this lab environment:
  - EC2 instances with the CodeDeploy agent installed and IAM permission to read files in S3,
  - a service role enabling CodeDeploy to deploy code to EC2, and
  - a GitHub repository containing the code you'll deploy.

-------
Download the Application Code

    In a new tab, open the GitHub repository link provided on the lab page and copy the URL.

    In the AWS Console, click the Cloud Shell icon (>_) in the upper right.

    Once the session has initialized, enter wget and paste the GitHub URL (do not run the command yet):
    wget https://github.com/ACloudGuru-Resources/course-aws-certified-developer-associate/blob/main/Code_Deploy_Demo/webapp.zip

    In the URL, replace blob with raw, then press Enter.

    Run ls to view the ZIP file.

Create an S3 Bucket

    In Cloud Shell, create a unique bucket name:
    aws s3 mb s3://<insert unique name>

    Press Enter.

    Upload the code to the bucket using the bucket name from the previous step:
    aws s3 cp webapp.zip s3://<insert bucket name>

    Type s3 into the search bar at the top of the browser, then click S3 and open in a new tab.

    Click the bucket listed under Name, then click into webapp.zip.

    Click Copy S3 URI.

Deploy the Code Using CodeDeploy

    In the AWS Console, type ec2 into the search bar at the top of the page. Click EC2.
    Under Resources, click Instances (running) to view your instances.
    Note the tag name, acg-demo-server.
    Type codedeploy in the search bar at the top of the page. Open CodeDeploy in a new tab.
    From the left-hand menu on the CodeDeploy page, select Getting started.
    Click Create application.
    Set the following parameters:
        Application name: Type mywebapp.
        Compute platform: Select EC2/On-premises.
    Click Create application.
    On the next page, click Create deployment group.
    Set the following parameters:
        Deployment group name: Type mydg.
        Service role: Select the service role that populates.
        Deployment type: Select In-place.
        Environment configuration: Click Amazon EC2 instances.
        Key: Select Name.
        Value: Select acg-demo-server.
        Deployment settings: Select CodeDeployDefault.AllAtOnce.
        Load balancer: De-select the Enable load balancing box.
    Click Create deployment group.
    Click Create deployment.
    For Revision location, copy and paste the bucket from the Cloud Shell, s3://<insert bucket name>/webapp.zip.
    Scroll to the bottom of the page and click Create deployment (it may take a few minutes to create).
    Test the deployment by navigating back to the EC2 page and click into one of your instances.
    Copy the Public IP, then open an incognito browser tab.
    Paste the IP address into the browser tab. You should see a congratulations message appear on the web page.

9.9  CodePipeline 101

  AWS CodePipeline
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/codepipeline/latest/userguide/welcome.html


  CodePipeline
    - A fully managed CI/CD Service
    Orchestrates the build, test, & deployment
      - the pipeline is triggered every time there is a change to your code
    Automated Release Process
      - fast, consistent, fewer mistakes
      - Enables quick release of new features and bug fixes
    CodePipeline integrates with:
      - CodeCommit, CodeBuild, CodeDeploy, Github, Jenkins, Elastic Beanstalk, CloudFormation,
        Lambda, Elastic Container Service (ECS)

       Note: Jenkins: open source application for building, test, and deploy your application

  Example CodePipeline Workflow
    Workflow is Defined - CodePipeline
      - the workflow begins when there is a change detected in your source code
    New Code Appears - CodeCommit
      - new source code appears in CodeCommit Repository
    Code is Built & Tested - CodeBuild
      - CodeBuild immediately compiles source code, runs tests, and produces packages
    Application Deployed - CodeDeploy
      - The newly built application is deployed into staging or production environment

  Exam Tips

    CodePipeline
      Continuous Integration (CI) / Continuous Delivery (CD) Service
        - Orchestrates your end-to-end software release process based on a workflow you define
      Automated
        - automatically triggers your pipeline as soon as a change is detected in your source code repository
      Integrates with AWS & Third-Party Tools
        - CodeCommit, CodeBuild, CodeDeploy, Github, Jenkins, Elastic Beanstalk, CloudFormation,
          Lambda, Elastic Container Service (ECS)
