9.10 CodePipeline Demo - Part 1

  Connect to your Linux instance using SSH
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/AccessingInstancesLinux.html

  Amazon EC2 key pairs and Linux instances
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/ec2-key-pairs.html#having-ec2-create-your-key-pair

  Configure the AWS CLI
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-chap-configure.html#cli-quick-configuration

  Task Breakdown
    1. Use CloudFormation to create an EC2 instance
    2. Upload version 1 of our application to S3
    3. Deploy the application using CodeDeploy
    4. Build a CodePipeline and deploy version 2
    5. Configure an automated pipeline and deploy version 3
     - code update will trigger a cloudWatch event which will trigger CodePipeline to run CodeDeploy

  Prerequisites:
    Resources
      - there are some resources that you will need to download to your local machine:
        https://github.com/ACloudGuru-Resources/course-aws-certified-developer-associate/tree/main/Code_Pipeline_Demo
         - application code
         - test file containing the commands to run

    Configure an EC2 Instance
      - we'll use CloudFormation to automatically create and EC2 instance, install the CodeDeploy agent
        and configure the IAM permissions required to run CodeDeploy and CodePipeline


   # create EC2 key pair
   -> Console -> EC2 -> Key Pairs -> Create Key Pair -> name: "cpkp", type: PEM -> Create

    # Create S3 buckets: 1. to store cloudformation template; 2; to store app code


    -> Console -> S3 -> create bucket -> Bucket Name: my-app-code-bkt;
      Object Ownership: "ACLs disabled"; Block Public Access ...: select "Block all public access"
      -> Create Bucket


    -> Console -> S3 -> create bucket -> Bucket Name: my-cf-template-bkt;
      Object Ownership: "ACLs disabled"; Block Public Access ...: select "Block all public access"
      -> Bucket Versioning: enable -> Create Bucket

      -> bucket -> my-cf-tempatel-bkt -> upload -> file: CF_Template.json -> upload

   # in cloud shell, run cloudformation command (after changing S3 URL and Key pair values):

      aws cloudformation create-stack --stack-name CodeDeployDemoStack \
      --template-url https://my-cf-template-bkt.s3.amazonaws.com/CF_Template.json \
      --parameters ParameterKey=InstanceCount,ParameterValue=1 \
      ParameterKey=InstanceType,ParameterValue=t2.micro \
      ParameterKey=KeyPairName,ParameterValue=cpkp \
      ParameterKey=OperatingSystem,ParameterValue=Linux \
      ParameterKey=SSHLocation,ParameterValue=0.0.0.0/0 \
      ParameterKey=TagKey,ParameterValue=Name \
      ParameterKey=TagValue,ParameterValue=CodeDeployDemo \
      --capabilities CAPABILITY_IAM

   #  Verify that the Cloud Formation stack has completed using (until it reports CREATE_COMPLETE):
    aws cloudformation describe-stacks --stack-name CodeDeployDemoStack --query "Stacks[0].StackStatus" --output text

    # log on to EC2 instance and verify codedeploy-agent is running
    -> EC2 -> instance -> Connect
    $ sudo service codedeploy-agent status
      The AWS CodeDeploy agent is running as PID 3807

9.11 CodePipeline Demo - Part 2

   # upload version 1 of code to S3
      -> bucket -> my-app-code-bkt -> upload -> file: mywebapp.zip (version 1) -> upload


   # create CodeDeploy application
   -> Console -> codedeploy -> getting started -> create application -> Application Name: mywebapp,
     compute platform: EC2/onpremise  -> Create application

   # create CodeDeploy deployment group
   -> Console -> codedeploy -> create deployment group -> Deployment Group Name: mydb,
      Service Role: <created by CF template>, Deployment Type: In-Place. Env Configuration: EC2 instances,
      Tag: Key: Name, Value: CodeDeployDemo (from CF), Load Balancer: uncheck "enable load balancing"
      Deployment Configuraiton: CodeDeployDefault.AllAtOnce
      -> Create deployment group

   # create CodeDeploy Deployment
   -> Console -> codedeploy -> create deployment -> Deployment Group: mydb, Revision Type: Stored in S3,
     Revision Location: <s3 URL> -> Create Deployment

   # verify by going to EC2 Public IP in web browser
   -> shows version 1


   # upload version 2 of code to S3
      -> bucket -> my-app-code-bkt -> upload -> file: mywebapp.zip (version 2) -> upload

   # uses CodePipeline to manage a new version
   -> Console -> codedeploy -> Pipeline - CodePipeline (left tab> -> Getting Started -> Create Pipeline ->
     Pipeline Name: mypipeline  Service Role: New Service Role, Role Name: <default name> -> Next ->
     Source Provider: S3, Bucket: my-app-code-bkt, S3 Object Key: mywebapp.zip,
     Change detection options: CloudWatch Events -> Next ->  Skip build stage -> Skip ->
     Deploy Provider: CodeDeploy,  Application Name: mywebapp, Deployment group: mydg -> Next -> Create

   # wait for pipeline build to complete

   # verify by going to EC2 Public IP in web browser
   -> shows version 2

   # upload version 3 of code to S3
      -> bucket -> my-app-code-bkt -> upload -> file: mywebapp.zip (version 3) -> upload

   # wait for pipeline build to complete

   # verify by going to EC2 Public IP in web browser
   -> shows version 3

   # delete pipeline
   # delete codedeploy application
   # delete CloudFormation stack

  Exam Tips

    CodePipeline
      CI/CD
        - continuous Integration / Continuous Delivery
        - orchestrates the end-to-end software release process
      Automated
        - avoids manual deployments
        - automatically triggers your pipeline as soon as a change is detected in your source code repository
      Integrates with AWS & Third-Party Tools
        - CodeCommit, CodeBuild, CodeDeploy, Github, Jenkins, Elastic Beanstalk, CloudFormation,
          Lambda, Elastic Container Service (ECS)

9.12 Setting Up a Pipeline with a Manual Approval In AWS CodePipeline Lab

About this lab

AWS CodePipeline is a native AWS solution that provides a continuous integration and continuous deployment
pipeline offering to its clients. In this hands-on lab, we will implement AWS CodePipeline to deploy AWS
infrastructure through AWS CloudFormation. We will add an action to our AWS CodePipeline that requires a
manual approval intended to have any code commits reviewed prior to being deployed.

Learning objectives
 - Create an AWS IAM Role
     - to allow CloudFormation to act on our behalf
 - Create an AWS CodeCommit Repository and SNS Topic
   - SNS topic to act as our manual approval step
 - Create an AWS CodePipeline Pipeline with manual approval step


Lab Scenario:
          QA approves ------ |
                             v
        CodeCommit  ----> Manual Approval ----> Code Deploy
           ^                                         |
           |                                         V
           |---- Developer commits code              Deploys CloudFormation Stack
-------

Create an AWS IAM Role

    From the AWS console, navigate to Identity and Access Management (IAM).
    Click Roles in the left-hand menu.
    Click Create role.
    Select CloudFormation as the service that will use this role.
    Click Next: Permissions.
    Select the checkbox for the AdministratorAccess permissions policy.
    Click Next: Tags.
    Click Next: Review.
    Enter pipelineperms4CF as the role name.
    Click Create role.

Create an AWS CodeCommit Repository and SNS Topic

    From the AWS console, navigate to CodeCommit and click Create repository.
    Enter pipeline4cf as the repository name.
    Click Create.
    In a new browser tab or window, navigate to the lab GitHub page.
    Click Raw.
    Select all content from the raw file, and then copy and paste into a new local file on your computer. Save the file as S3Retain.yaml.
    Click Add file.
    Click Upload file.
    Click Choose file.
    Select the S3Retain.yaml file that was saved to your local computer.
    Enter your name as the author name.
    Enter your email address as the email address.
    Click Commit changes.
    Navigate to Simple Notification Service (SNS).
    In the Create topic box on the main SNS page, enter manualapprove as the topic name.
    Click Next step.
    Accept the defaults on the next page by clicking Create topic.
    Click Create subscription.
    Choose Email as the protocol.
    Enter your email address as the endpoint.
    Click Create subscription.
    Navigate to your inbox, open the AWS Notification - Subscription Confirmation message, and click the Confirm subscription link.

Create an AWS CodePipeline Pipeline

    Navigate to the CodePipeline console.
    Click Create pipeline.
    Enter ManualApprovalPipeline as the pipeline name.
    Ensure New service role is selected.
    Ensure Allow AWS CodePipeline to create service role so it can be used with this new pipeline is checked.
    Expand the Advanced settings section and ensure the Default location and Default AWS Managed Key options are selected.
    Click Next.
    On the Add source stage page, set the following values:
        Source provider: AWS CodeCommit
        Repository name: pipeline4cf
        Branch name: main
        Change detection options: Amazon CloudWatch Events (recommended)
    Click Next.
    Click Skip build stage.
    Click Skip.
    On the Add deploy stage page, set the following values:
        Deploy provider: AWS CloudFormation
        Region: US East - (N. Virginia)
        Action mode: Create or update a stack
        Stack name: s3bucketretain
        Artifact name: SourceArtifact
        File name: S3Retain.yaml
        Role name: pipelineperms4CF
    Click Next > Create pipeline.
    Click the AWS CloudFormation link in the Deploy panel.
    Once CloudFormation shows complete, return to the CodePipeline service.
    Verify the manualapprove pipeline status shows Succeeded in the Deploy panel.
    Select our pipeline.
    Click Edit.
    Click + Add stage between the Source and Deploy panels.
    Enter manualapproval as the stage name and click Add stage.
    Click + Add action group.
    In the Edit action dialog box, set the following values:
        Action name: manualapproval
        Action provider: Manual approval
        SNS topic ARN: Select the listed SNS topic ARN we created earlier
    Click Done > Save > Save.
    Click Release change to restart the pipeline.
    Click Release.
    Navigate to your inbox, and open the APPROVAL NEEDED... message to see what your team would receive in a real-world environment.
    Navigate back to CodePipeline.
    Click Review in the Manual approve panel.
    Enter Looks good — approved. in the comments, and click Approve.
-------

9.13 Working with a DevOps CI/CD Pipeline in AWS Lab

About this lab

In this lab, we will create a Continuous Integration/Continuous Delivery pipeline using AWS, and then use that
pipeline to deploy a new version of our website automatically. We'll implement this with AWS CodeStar, a frontend
tool that will automatically configure AWS CodeCommit, AWS CodeBuild, AWS CodeDeploy, and AWS CodePipeline. By
the end of the lab, you should understand what a CI/CD pipeline is, as well as the basics of how to set one up
and push an update to a source code repository to trigger a new deploy.

Learning objectives
 - Create a CI/CD Pipeline
 - Deploy a New Version of a Website using a CI/CD Pipeline

 1. Create AWS CodeStar Project
 2. Deploy static HTML Webpage
 3. Update webpage with AWS CodeDeploy
 4. Completed Lab


What is AWS CodeStar?
AWS CodeStar is a cloud-based development service that provides the tools you need to quickly develop,
build, and deploy applications on AWS. With AWS CodeStar, you can set up your entire continuous delivery
toolchain in minutes, allowing you to start releasing code faster. AWS CodeStar makes it easy for your
whole team to work together securely, with built-in role-based policies that allow you to easily manage
access and add owners, contributors, and viewers to your projects. Each AWS CodeStar project comes with
a unified project dashboard and integration with Atlassian JIRA software, a third-party issue tracking
and project management tool. With the AWS CodeStar project dashboard, you can easily track your entire
software development process, from a backlog work item to production code deployment.

---------
Create a CI/CD Pipeline

    Navigate to CodeStar.
    Click Create project.
    If the Create service role dialog appears, click Create service role.
    Click the HTML card.
    Click Services and open EC2 in a new tab.
    Click KeyPairs located in the navigation menu.
    Click Create key pair.
    For Name, enter pipeline.
    Click Create key pair.
    Back in the CodeStar browser tab, close the key pair dialog.
    Click Next.
    For Project name, enter Pipeline.
    For the repository, select AWS CodeCommit.
    For the Instance type, select t2.micro.
    Choose the Subnet for us-east-1a.
    Choose the pipeline and click ** Next**.
    Click Create Project. It could take 5-10 minutes for the project to finish being set up.

Deploy a New Version of a Website using a CI/CD Pipeline

    Click View application to navigate to our web application.

    Back in the CodeStar browser tab, click IDE.

    Click Access your project code.

    Click Edit in AWS CodeCommit. This opens our repositories.

    Click the webpage folder.

    Click the index.html file. This is the code used to generate the website we just visited.

    Click Edit.

    Scroll down to line 61.

    Change the h1 header in line 61 to:
    Hello Cloud Gurus!

    Change the h2 header in line 62 to:
    You just deployed using your own CI/CD pipeline!

    In the Commit changes to master section, set the following values:
        Author name: Your name
        Email address: test@test.com
        Commit message: Update the website.

    Click Commit changes.

    In the CodeStar browser tab, click Pipeline to watch it all be deployed.

    Refresh the web app we visited before, where we should see our new message we just edited in the code.

9.14 Introducing CodeArtifact

  What is CodeArtifact?
    - An artifact repository that makes it easy for developers to find the software packages they need
    Artifact Repository
      - securely, store, push, and share artifacts related to your application
    Software Packages
      - Used in your software development process
      - a package is a bundle of software
    Including Open-Source software
      - Open-source software from public repositories, as well as software developed in-house

  What is CodeArtifact?
    - a central repository that can be used by all your developers to obtain the correct versions of the
      software packages required for their projects
    - integrates with public repositories (e.g. npm Registry [for node.js or Java script packages],
      Python Package Index, Maven Central [commonly used librarie])
    - integrates with your CI/CD system, e.g. CodeBuild can use CodeArtifact to obtain the correct
      package versions to build your application

  Artifacts
    Includes: Documentation, Compiled Applications, Deployable packages, Libraries

  CodeArtifact Integrating with Public Repositories
    Third-Party Software
      - Makes third-party software available for use (e.g. open-source packages from public repositories
        like npm Registry)
    Approved Packages
      - IT leaders can make approved packages available
      - Often this means the version that our engineering team is willing to support
    Efficient
      - Developers know where to find approved packages and can also publish and share their own
        packages by adding them to the repository

  How to Integrate CodeArtifact with an external repository
     Steps:
        1 Create a domain (e.g. my-domain) - where you group your repositories
        2 Create a repository in your domain (e.g. my-repo) - where your packages will be stored
        3 Create an Upstream Repository (e.g. npm-store) - will connect to external repository
        4 Add an external connection between upstream repository and Public Repo - allows your to
          pull packages down from external repository
          - everything is encrypted in transit and at-rest
        5 Associate Upstream repository (e.g. npm-store) with your repository (e.g. my-repo)
        6 Developer can use external repository {e.g. npm) CLI to pull in package (e.g. express package
          from Npm Registry)

    my-domain
    |-------------------------------------------|
    |                           Upstream        |
    | Repository                Repository      | External    Public Repo
    | |-----------|           |-------------|   | Connection  |-------------|
    | |my-repo    | --------> | npm-store   |   | ----------> | npm Registry|
    | |           | <-------- |             |   | <---------- |             |
    | |           |           |             |   |             |-------------|
    | |-----------|           |-------------|   |
    |      ^                                    |
    |------|------------------------------------|
           | CodeArtifact Domain
           |
         Developer

  Exam Tips

    CodeArtifact
      - an artifact repository makes it easy for developers to find the software packages they need
      Package
        - a bundle of software used in your software development process
      Approved Package
        - Developers can find approved packages and can also publish their own
      External Connections to 3rd party repos
        - Create upstream repository with an external connection to pull packages from an external
          repository (e.g. the npm Registry for Node.js packages)

---------------------------------------------------
9.15 Demo - Creating a CodeArtifact Repository

  lesson Resources (AWS CLI Commands)
  https://github.com/ACloudGuru-Resources/course-aws-certified-developer-associate/tree/main/Code_Artifact_Demo

  Connect a CodeArtifact repository to a public repository
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/codeartifact/latest/ug/external-connection.html

  1 Create a Domain (my-domain)
    Create a repository (my-repo) and an upstream repositoyr (npm-store)

    -> Console -> CloudShell

    #  Create a domain called my-domain:
    $ aws codeartifact create-domain --domain my-domain

    # Create a repository (my-repo) in your domain (my-domain):
    $ aws codeartifact create-repository --domain my-domain --repository my-repo

    # Create an upstream repository (npm-store) for your my-repo repository:
    $ aws codeartifact create-repository --domain my-domain --repository npm-store

  2 Add an External Connection
    Conect npm-store to the publci npm registry

    # Add an external connection to the npm public repository to your npm-store repository:
    $ aws codeartifact associate-external-connection --domain my-domain --repository npm-store --external-connection "public:npmjs"

  3 Associate the Upstream Repo
    Associate npm-store with my my-repo

    # Associate the npm-store repository as an upstream repository to the my-repo repository:
    $ aws codeartifact update-repository --repository my-repo --domain my-domain --upstreams repositoryName=npm-store


  4 Install a Package
    Use the npm CLI to install the Express package into my-repo

    # Configure the npm package manager with your my-repo repository (fetches an authorization token from CodeArtifact
    #  using your AWS credentials):
    $ aws codeartifact login --tool npm --repository my-repo --domain my-domain

    # Use the npm CLI to install an npm package. For example, to install the popular npm package "express", use the
    #  following command, if we don't specify a version, this command will install the latest version available in the
    #  external repo:
    $ npm install express

    # (express is a Node.js web application framework used to develop web and mobile applications)


    # View the package you just installed in your my-repo repository:
    $ aws codeartifact list-packages --domain my-domain --repository my-repo


    -> Console -> CodeArtifact -> you can see the my-repo and npm-store repos
      -> select "my-repo" -> shows a list of all the packages
      -> select "npm-store" -> shows "connected to public repository"


    5 Clean-up - delete "my-rep" and "npm-store repos and "my-domain" domain
    # Clean-up To avoid further AWS charges, delete the resources you created:
    $ aws codeartifact delete-repository --domain my-domain --repository my-repo
    $ aws codeartifact delete-repository --domain my-domain --repository npm-store
    $ aws codeartifact delete-domain --domain my-domain

   Exam Tips

    CodeArtifact Demo
      - if you want to pull packages from an external repository, create an upstream repository with
        an external connection to the public repository (e.g. the npm registy for Node.js packages)

    my-domain
    |-------------------------------------------|
    |                           Upstream        |
    | Repository                Repository      | External    Public Repo
    | |-----------|           |-------------|   | Connection  |-------------|
    | |my-repo    | --------> | npm-store   |   | ----------> | npm Registry|
    | |           | <-------- |             |   | <---------- |             |
    | |           |           |             |   |             |-------------|
    | |-----------|           |-------------|   |
    |      ^                                    |
    |------|------------------------------------|
           | CodeArtifact Domain
           |
         Developer

9.16 Elastic Container Service (ECS)

  Running Containerized Microservices on AWS
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/whitepapers/latest/running-containerized-microservices/introduction.html
   -> PDF

   The 12 Factor App
   https://12factor.net/

   Pushing a Docker image
   https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECR/latest/userguide/docker-push-ecr-image.html

   Using Amazon ECR with the AWS CLI
   https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECR/latest/userguide/getting-started-cli.html


  Containers
    What is a container?
      - similar to a virtual machine, but more like a virtual operating environment
    Standardized
      - a standard unit with everything the software needs to run, e.g. libraries, system tools,
        code, and runtimes
    Microservices
      - applications are created using independent stateless components or microservices running
        in containers
    Docker or Windows Containers
      - use Dockers Containers to create Linux containers and Windows Containers for Windows workloads

  Architecture of a Docker Container
    - Container includes: code, libraries, and virtual kernel
    - Docker containers run on Docker so Docker must be install on the Linux OS

  Advantages of Containers & Microservices Architecture
    Highly scalable
      - if the application becomes over loaded, scale only the services you need to instead of the entire application
    Fault Tolerant
      - a single error in one of your containers shouldn't bring down your entire app
    Easier to Maintain
      - Easier to maintain, update, and change than large monolithic applications because everything
        is broken down into smaller components

  What About ECS?
    - A container orchestration service which supports Docker and Windows Containers
    - quickly deploy and scale containerized workloads without have to install, configure, manage and
      scale your own orchestration platform
    - Similar to Kubernetes, but with deep integration with AWS Services, e.g. IAM, VPC, Route53

  Fargate or EC2
    Clusters of Virtual Machines
      - ECS will run your containers on clusters of virtual machines (EC2 instances)
    Fargate for Serverless
      - Use Fargate for Serverless containers and you don't need to worry about the underlying
        EC2 instances
    EC2 for More Control
      - if you want to control the installation, configuration, and management of your compute environment

  Introducing Elastic Container Registry (ECR)
    - registry of container images
    - ECS acccesses the container images from ECR
          ECS   --------> ECR [registry of container images]

  Cool services which use ECS
    Sagemaker
      - quickly deploy and scale machine learning models for training and inference jobs
    Amazon Lex
      - uses deep learning to build conversation interfaces (e.g. chatbots)
    Amazon.com recommendation engine
      - Amazon's own recommendation engine is running on ECS

  Exam Tips

    Elastic Container Service (ECS)
      Containers
        - virtual operating environment with everything the software needs to run
        - includes libraries, system tools, code, and runtime
        - allows applications to be built using independent stateless components or microservices
          running in multiple containers
      ECS
        - container orchestration
        - ECS will run your containers on clusters of virtual machines
      Fargate
        - Serverless
        - you don't need to worry about the underlying EC2 instances where your containers are running
      EC2
        - if you want to control the installation, configuration, and management of your compute environment
      Elastic Container Registry
        - container registery
        - this is where your can store your container images - Docker or Windows Containers

---------------------------------------------------
9.17 Introduction to CloudFormation

   AWS CloudFormation Resource attribute reference
   https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/aws-product-attribute-reference.html

   AWS CloudFormation Template Snippets
   https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/CHAP_TemplateQuickRef.html

  CloudFormation
    - manage, configure, and provision your AWS infrastructure as code
    - resources are defined using a CloudFormation template
    - cloudFormation interprets the template and makes the appropriate API calls to create the
      resources you have defined in the template
    - template supported formats: YAML or JSON

  CloudFormation Benefits
    Consistent
      - infrastructure is provisioned consistently with fewer mistakes
    Quick and Efficient
      - less time and effort thatn configuring things manually
    Version Control
      - you can version control and peer review your templates
    Free to Use
      - Free to use, but you are charged for the AWS resources you create using CloudFormation
    Manage Updates
      - can be used to manage updates to existing CloudFormation platforms and manage dependencies
        (create resoources in the needed order)
    Rolling Back
      - you can roll back to previous state and delete the entire stack as well

  CloudFormation Process
    YAML or JSON template
      - YAML or JSON template used to describe the end state or the infrastructure you are either
        provisioning or changing
    S3
      - after creating the template, you upload it to CloudFormation using S3
    API Calls
      - cloudFormation reads the template and makes the API calls on your behalf
    CloudFormation Stack
      - the resulting set of resources that CloudFormation builds from your template is called a stack

  CloudFormation Template Structure (YAML)

    AWSTemplateFormatVersion: 2010-09-09                  <---- Template format version - only supported version is ":2010-09-09"
    Description: "Template to create an EC2 Instance"
    Metadata:                                             <---- optional metadata field
      Instances:
        Description: "Web Server Instance"

    Parameters:                                            <----- input values
      EnvType:
        Description: "Environment Type"
        Type: String
        AllowedValues:
          - prod
          - test

    Conditions:
      CreateProdResources: !Equals [ !Ref EnvType, prod ]     <---- test a condition: if EnvType == "prod", create CreateProdResource
      Mappings:                                               <----  mappings: e.g. set AMI values based on a region
      RegionMap:
        eu-west-1:
          "ami": "ami-0bdb1d6c15a40392c"

    Transform:                                                <---- include snippets of code outside of main template
      Name: 'AWS::Include'                                    <----- example include Lambda code, your own code
      Parameters:
        Location: 's3://myAmazonS3BucketName/MyFileName.yaml'

    Resources:                                                 <----- the AWS resources your are deploying
      EC2Instance:                                             <------ Resource section is the only mandatory section
        Type: AWS::EC2::Instance
        Condition: CreateProdResources                         <------ create resource if Condition is 'true'
        Properties:
        Outputs:
      InstanceID:                                               <---- information that CF should output - here it outputs EC2 Instance ID
        Description: The Instance ID                            <---- CF outputs can be inputs to a different CF template
        Value: !Ref EC2Instance
          InstanceType: t2.micro
          ImageId: ami-0bdb1d6c15a40392c


   CloudFormation template
     The Resource section is Mandatory
       - The resource section is the only mandatory section of the CloudFormation template
     The Transform section is for Referencing Additional code
       - the transform section is used to reference additional code stored in S3, allowing for code re-use
         e.g. Lambda code ore template snippets / reusable pieces of CloudFormation code

  Exam Tips
     CloudFormation
       Infrastructure As Code
         - cloudformation allows you to manage, configure, and provision AWS infrastructure as
           YAML or JSON code
       Template main sections
         Parameters:
           - input custom values
         Conditions:
           - e.g. provision resources based on environment
         Resources
           - this section is mandatory and describes the AWS resources that CloudFormation will create
         Mappings
           - allows you to create custom mappings like mapping AMIs to Regions
         Transform
           - allows your to reference code located in S3, e.g. Lambda code, or reusable
             snippets of CloudFormation code

9.18 Demo: Provisioning AWS Resources Using CloudFormation

  Lesson Objectives
    1 Create and Download SSH Key Pair
      - use this later to log in to an EC2 instance

      -> Console -> EC2 -> Key Pairs -> Create Key Pair -> name: "cpkp", type: PEM -> Create

    2 Create a CloudFormation Stack
      - use the provided template to provision an EC2 instance with SSH access enabled

     -> Console -> CloudFormation -> Create Stack ->  Template Source: upload a template file,
         upload template file: ec2_cloudformation_template.yaml  -> Next
         Stack Name: my-cf-stack; Parameter: KeyName: mynvkp  -> Next -> -> Next -> Submit

    3 Review our CloudFormation Stack
      - Identify the new instance in the AWS console, and test that we can log in using SSH and the
        key pair we created at the beginning

     -> Console -> CloudFormation -> Stacks ->  select "my-cf-stack"
         examine tabs:
             events    -> shows create events
             resources -> shows created resources (ec2 & SG)
             outputs   -> shows requested outputs
             parameters
             template

    -> Console -> ec2 -> instances -> select "My CF Instance"
      -> get public IP
      $ ssh -i mynvkp.pem ec2-user@<publicIP>


     4 Clean-up
     -> Console -> CloudFormation -> Stacks ->  select "my-cf-stack"  -> Delete

  Exam Tips:

     CloudFormation Demo
       YAML or JSON Template
         - YAML or JSoN templates are used to describe the end state of the infrastructure you are
           either provisioning or changing
       S3
         - after creating the template, you upload it to CloudFormation where it is stored in S3
       API Calls
         - CloudFormation reads the template and makes API calls on your behalf
       CloudFormation Stack
         - the resulting set of resources that CloudFormation builds from yout template is called a "stackz"

9.19 Create a DynamoDB Table Using CloudFormation

About this lab

In this lab scenario, Alfredo of Alfredo's Pizza is building a web application to manage his supply and food inventory.
He's looking for help to provision his database infrastructure, which will hold his data. You'll use CloudFormation to
write the desired state of the infrastructure using code, which includes a DynamoDB table to hold inventory items.
You’ll first launch the resources in his development environment, but the template can later be used to launch a stack in
the test and production environments as well. By the end of this lab, you'll have a good understanding of infrastructure
as code using CloudFormation.

Learning objectives
  - Review and Download the CloudFormation Template
  - Launch the CloudFormation Stack
  - Verify the DynamoDB Table Was Created

  Resource:
     cloudformation template:
     https://github.com/linuxacademy/content-aws-certified-cloud-practioner/blob/main/labs/create-dynamodb-table-using-cloudformation/acg-dynamodb-template.yaml

-------
Review and Download the CloudFormation Template

    Navigate to the provided CloudFormation template for this lab: acg-dynamodb-template.yaml.

    Click Raw.

    Right-click and select Save As.

    Make sure the Format is YAML, and click Save.

        Note: You may need to add the .yaml extension to the filename.

    Click Save.

Launch the CloudFormation Stack

    From the top search bar in the AWS Management Console, search for and navigate to CloudFormation.
    Click Create stack > With new resources (standard).
    Select Template is ready.
    Select Upload a template file.
    Click Choose file.
    Select the YAML file you downloaded.
    Click Open.
    Click Next.
    For Stack name, enter MyStack.
    Click Next.
    On the Configure stack options page leave everything as-is, and click Next.
    On the Review MyStack page, click Create stack.
    Click the refresh icon in the upper-right section of the Events list to check the status of the stack being created.

        Note: It will take a few minutes for the stack to create. You can view the progress of the stack creation in the Status column of the Events list.

Verify the DynamoDB Table Was Created

    Once the stack is created, use the top search bar within the console to search for and navigate to DynamoDB.
    From the left menu, click Tables.
    Click the listed Inventory table. While there aren't currently any items within the table, it is ready for items to be added.

9.20 Demo: Exporting CloudFormation Stack Values

  Example Scenario
     CloudFormation stack: NetworkStack
     includes:

         VPC 10.0.0.0/16
         |-------------------------------------------------|
         | |------------|                                  |
         | |Public      | Security   Network   Route  IGW  | -> Outputs             ---> inputs: WebserverStack.json
         | |Subnet      |   Group      ACL     Table       |    export: SubnetID
         | |10.0.0.0/24 |                                  |    export: SecurityGroupID
         | |------------|                                  |
         |-------------------------------------------------|

         Next: Use CloudFormation to launch an EC2 instance in the NetworkStack's Public Subnet
               and using the NetworkStack's Secrurity Group

         We can import the SecurityGroupID and SubnetID that were output from the NetworkStack
         into our new stack by referencing them in the CloudFormation Template

  Example Template: Export Values

  Outputs: Use the Outputs section to define variables that you would like to export to other
           cloudFormation templates
           Note: Fn::Sub -> substitute function,
              JSON syntax: { "Fn::Sub" : [ String, { Var1Name: Var1Value, Var2Name: Var2Value }

              If you're substituting only template parameters, resource logical IDs, or resource attributes in the String
               parameter, don't specify a variable map.
               JSON syntax: { "Fn::Sub" : String }

       "Outputs" : {
        "PublicSubnet" : {
          "Description" : "The subnet ID to use for public web servers",
          "Value" :  { "Ref" : "PublicSubnet" },
          "Export" : { "Name" : {"Fn::Sub": "${AWS::StackName}-SubnetID" }}
        },
        "WebServerSecurityGroup" : {
          "Description" : "The security group ID to use for public web servers",
          "Value" :  { "Fn::GetAtt" : ["WebServerSecurityGroup", "GroupId"] },
          "Export" : { "Name" : {"Fn::Sub": "${AWS::StackName}-SecurityGroupID" }}
        }

  Example Template: Import Values

     - Use "ImportValue" function to import values
     - the name of the stack providing the import values is provided as a parameter:

      "Fn::ImportValue": {
                  "Fn::Sub": "${NetworkStackParameter}-SecurityGroupID"


       "Resources": {
        "WebServerInstance": {
          "Type": "AWS::EC2::Instance",
          "Properties": {
            "InstanceType": "t3.micro",
            "ImageId": "ami-0889a44b331db0194",
            "NetworkInterfaces": [
              {
                "GroupSet": [
                  {
                    "Fn::ImportValue": {
                      "Fn::Sub": "${NetworkStackParameter}-SecurityGroupID"
                    }
                  }
                ],
                "AssociatePublicIpAddress": "true",
                "DeviceIndex": "0",
                "DeleteOnTermination": "true",
                "SubnetId": {
                  "Fn::ImportValue": {
                    "Fn::Sub": "${NetworkStackParameter}-SubnetID"
                  }
                }
              }
            ]
          }
        }
      }

  Demo

    1. Create a New Stack named NetworkStack

     -> Console -> CloudFormation -> Create Stack ->  Template Source: upload a template file,
         upload template file: NetworkStack.json  -> Next
         Stack Name: NetworkStack;  -> Next -> -> Next -> Submit

    2. Outputs - "Export" SubnetID and SecurityGroupID

    3. Create another Stack with an EC2 instance in the NetworkStack public subnet
      - Use "Fn:ImportValue" to import the SubnetID and SecurityGroupID from NetworkStack

     -> Console -> CloudFormation -> Create Stack ->  Template Source: upload a template file,
         upload template file: WebServerStack.json  -> Next
         Stack Name: WebServerStack; Parameter: NetworkStackParameter: NetworkStack  -> Next -> -> Next -> Submit


     4. Clean-up
        -> delete both stacks

  Exam Tips

    Exporting CloudFormation Stack Values
      template outputs:
           - Use the Outputs section to define variables that you would like to export to other
               cloudFormation templates

           "Outputs" : {
            "PublicSubnet" : {
              "Description" : "The subnet ID to use for public web servers",
              "Value" :  { "Ref" : "PublicSubnet" },
              "Export" : { "Name" : {"Fn::Sub": "${AWS::StackName}-SubnetID" }}
            },
            "WebServerSecurityGroup" : {
              "Description" : "The security group ID to use for public web servers",
              "Value" :  { "Fn::GetAtt" : ["WebServerSecurityGroup", "GroupId"] },
              "Export" : { "Name" : {"Fn::Sub": "${AWS::StackName}-SecurityGroupID" }}
            }

    Importing CloudFormation Stack Values

       template ImportValue fucntion:
         - Use "ImportValue" function to import values
         - the name of the stack providing the import values is provided as a parameter:

          "Fn::ImportValue": {
                      "Fn::Sub": "${NetworkStackParameter}-SecurityGroupID"

9.21 The Serverless Application Model (SAM)

  Serverless Application Model (SAM)
    CloudFormation for Serverless
      - The serverless Application Model (SAM) is an extension to CloudFormation used to
        define Serverless applications
    Simplified Syntax
      - SAM uses a simplified syntax for defining serverless resources; APIs, Lambda functions,
        DynamoDB Tables, etc
    SAM CLI
      - use the SAM CLI to package your deployment code, upload it to S3, and deploy your
        Serverless application

  SAM CLI
    Sam Package: inputs a YAML template and outputs a SAM compatible template and uploads
      a deployment package to an S3 bucket you specify

      sam package \
        --template-file ./mytemplate.yml \
        --output-template-file sam-template.yml \
        --s3-bucket s3-bucket-name

    Sam Deploy:  inputs the sam template that you've just created with "sam package", it specifies
            the name of your CloudFormation stack, and this capabilities parameter enables CloudFormation
            to create an IAM role on your behalf to allow the function to execute

      sam deploy \
        --sam-template-file sam-template.yml
        --stack-name mystack
        --capabilities CAPABILITY_IAM

  Exam Tips

    Serverless Application Model (SAM)
      - define and provision serverless applications using CloudFormation
      - SAM has its own CLI to package and deploy its code
        sam package
           - packages your application and uploads a deployment file to S3
        sam deploy
          - deploys your serverless app using CloudFormation

    aws s3 mb s3://cfsambucket-pat

9.23 CloudFormation Nested Stacks

  AWS CloudFormation best practices
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/best-practices.html

  Walkthrough: Refer to resource outputs in another AWS CloudFormation stack
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/walkthrough-crossstackref.html

  Code RE-Use with Nested Stacks
    Nested Stacks
      - enable re-use of CloudFormation code for common use cases
      - e.g. standard configuration for a load balancer, web server, or application server
      - instead of copying out the code each time, create a standard template for each common use case
        and reference from within your CloudFormation template

  CloudFormation Nested Stacks Template structure

      Resources:
      Type: AWS::CloudFormation::Stack
      Properties:
        NotificationARNs:                      <--- supporting ARNs (e.g. existing SNS topic
          - String
        Parameters:                            <---- parameters you want to pass to CloudFormation when nested stack is created
          AWS CloudFormation Stack Parameters
        Tags:
          - Resource Tag                       <---- add tags to your stack
        TemplateURL: https://s3.amazonaws.com/..../template.yml     <---- URL for Nested stack template (must be in S3 bucket)
        TimeoutInMinutes: Integer              <---- mins to wait for nested stack to complete

  Exam Tips

    CloudFormation Nested Stacks
      Code Re-use
        - allows you to re-use your CloudFormation code so you don't need to copy/paste every time
      Common Use Cases
        - Useful for frequently used configurations, e.g. load balancers, web or application servers
      Stack Resource Type
        - Reference the nested stack in the Resource section of any CloudFormation template using "Stack"
          resource type

           Resources:
           Type: AWS::CloudFormation::Stack

---------------------------------------------------
9.24 Working with CloudFormation Nested Stacks - Lab

About this lab
In this hands-on lab, we will create a nested stack from the templates provided. We will use the two templates to create a
nested stack that will implement a child template, which will then create an S3 bucket with a policy of no retain. This
child template can be reused repeatedly whenever an S3 bucket of this type is needed.

Note: only difference between "noretain.json" and "s3static.json" is '"DeletionPolicy": "Retain"' in included "s3static.json
    $ diff noretain.json s3static.json
    17c17,18
    <             }
    ---
    >             },
    >             "DeletionPolicy": "Retain"


Learning objectives
 - Create an S3 Bucket and Upload CloudFormation Templates (noretain.json and s3static.json)
    myneststackdemo-pat
 - Create a Nested Stack with a Single Child Stack using "root.json" CF template which deploys s3stack child stack
 - Create a Nested Stack with a Multiple Child Stacks using "multinest.json" which will deploy 2 nested [child]
   stacks using "s3static.json" and "noretain.json" templates
 - Upload the index.html file and verify the page loads

--------
Create an S3 Bucket and Upload a CloudFormation Template to It

    Navigate to S3.
    Click + Create bucket.
    On the Name and region screen, give your bucket a unique name. (Note: It must be all lowercase letters and be unique across all AWS accounts.)
    Click Next.
    Accept the default settings on the next screens, and click Create bucket.
    Click to open the bucket, and click Upload.
    Click Add files, and select the s3static.json CloudFormation template you downloaded earlier.
    Accept the default settings on the next screens, and click Upload.
    Click Upload.
    Click Add files, and select the noretain.json CloudFormation template you downloaded earlier.
    Accept the default settings on the next screens, and click Upload.

Create a Nested Stack from the CloudFormation Root Template

    In a new browser tab, navigate to CloudFormation.

    Click Create stack.

    In the Prerequisite - Prepare template section, select Template is ready.

    Click Upload a template file.

    Upload the root.json file from your local machine.

    Click View in Designer.
    {
        "AWSTemplateFormatVersion" : "2010-09-09",
        "Resources" : {
            "myStack" : {
    	       "Type" : "AWS::CloudFormation::Stack",
    	       "Properties" : {
                  "TemplateURL" : "https://s3.amazonaws.com/<YOUR-BUCKET-NAME>/noretain.json",
                  "TimeoutInMinutes" : "60"
    	       }
            }
        }
    }

    In the "TemplateURL" line, replace <YOUR-BUCKET-NAME> with the name of the bucket you just created.

    Click the checkbox at the top to validate the template, and then click the cloud icon with the up arrow to create the stack.

    Click Next.

    On the stack details page, give it a Stack name of s3webnoretain.

    Click Next.

    Leave the settings on the stack options page, and click Next.

    Check the boxes to accept the acknowledgements, and click Create stack. It will take a few minutes for it to fully be created.

Create a Nested Stack with Multiple Child Stacks

    In the CloudFormation browser tab, click Create stack.

    In the Prerequisite - Prepare template section, select Template is ready.

    Click Upload a template file.

    Click Choose file.

    Select the multinest.json template, which is included in the lab GitHub repository.

    Click View in Designer.
    {
        "AWSTemplateFormatVersion" : "2010-09-09",
        "Resources" : {
            "myStack" : {
    	       "Type" : "AWS::CloudFormation::Stack",
    	       "Properties" : {
                  "TemplateURL" : "https://s3.amazonaws.com/<YOUR-BUCKET-NAME>/s3static.json",
                  "TimeoutInMinutes" : "60"
    	       }
            },
            "myStack2" : {
                "Type" : "AWS::CloudFormation::Stack",
                "Properties" : {
                   "TemplateURL" : "https://s3.amazonaws.com/<YOUR-BUCKET-NAME>/noretain.json",
                   "TimeoutInMinutes" : "60"
                }
             }
        }
    }

    In the "TemplateURL" lines, replace <YOUR-BUCKET-NAME> with the name of your bucket.

    Click the checkbox at the top to validate the template, and then click the cloud icon with the up arrow to create the stack.

    Click Next.

    On the stack details page, give it a Stack name of multinestdemo.

    Click Next.

    Leave the settings on the stack options page, and click Next.

    Check the boxes to accept the acknowledgements, and click Create stack. It will take a few minutes for it to fully be created.

Upload an index.html File and Browse to It

    In the S3 browser tab, click to open one of the nested stack buckets.
    Click Upload.
    Click Add files, and select the index.html file included in the lab GitHub repository.
    Accept the default settings on the next screens, and click Upload.
    Click Upload.
    In the CloudFormation browser tab, select the nested stack in which you uploaded the index.html file.
    Click the Outputs tab, and browse to the listed WebsiteURL. It should display an Elastic Beanstalk web page, which is fine — all that matters is if it shows up alright.

9.25 Introducing the Cloud Development Kit (CDK)

  Cloud Development Kit (CDK)
    Open-source software development framework
      - allows you to build applications, define, and deploy AWS resources using a programming
        language of your choice (supported: TypeScript, Python, Java, .NET, and Go)
  How does CDK work?
                                CDK calls CF to                         AWS Resources
                               provision AWS Resources                        |-----> SQS
    cdk-init-stack.ts ---> CDK -------------------------> CloudFormation  ----|
                            |                                                 |-----> SNS
                            | CDK transforms it to
                            |  a CloudFormation template
                            V
                      MyappStack.template.json


  CDK Terminology
    App:   Container for one or more stacks
    Stack: Unit of deployment (similar to a cloudformation stack)
           - all resources in a stack a provisioned as a single unit
    Contruct: Defines the AWS resources
              - a stack includes 1 or more constructs
    CDK Toolkit (also known as the CDK CLI): The toolkit is used to create and deploy using the CDK

         |------------------------|
         |App                     |
         | |-------------------|  |
         | |Stack              |  |
         | ||-----------------||  |
         | ||construct   SQS  ||  |
         | ||-----------------||  |
         | |                   |  |
         | ||-----------------||  |
         | ||construct   SNS  ||  |
         | ||-----------------||  |
         | |-------------------|  |
         |------------------------|

  Example Code: TypeScript

      import { Duration, Stack, StackProps } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
      import * as sns from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-sns';
      import * as subs from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-sns-subscriptions';
      import * as sqs from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-sqs';
      import { Construct } from 'constructs';
      // import * as sqs from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-sqs';

      export class MyappStacj extends Stack {                               <----|
        constructor(scope: Construct, id: string, props?: StackProps) {          | defines the stack
          super(scope, id, props);                                          <----|

          // The code that defines your stack goes here                     <-----|
          const queue = new sqs.Queue(this, 'MyappQueue', {                       | Defines two contructs:
             visibilityTimeout: Duration.seconds(300)                             |  SQS queue
          });                                                                     |  SNS queue
          const topic = new sns.Topic(this, 'MyappTopic');                        |
             topic.addSubscription(new subs.SqsSubscription(queue));         <----|
        }
      }


  The CDK Process

    Create a New CDK Project
      - run "cdk init" inside an empty directory
    Compile the App
      - use "npm run build" to compile the app
    Synthesize [cloudformation template]
      - use "cdk synth" to create a CloudFormation template
    Deploy the Stack
      - use "cdk deploy" to deploy the stack using CloudFormation

      cdk init ----> npm run build ------> cdk synth ------> cdk deploy

  Exam Tips

    Cloud Development Kit (CDK)
      Deploy
         - deploy applications and AWS resources
         - use a programming language of your choice (TypeScript, Python, Java, .NET, and Go)
      Define
         - Apps, Stacks, and Constructs
          App:   Container for one or more stacks
          Stack: Unit of deployment (similar to a cloudformation stack)
                 - all resources in a stack a provisioned as a single unit
          Contruct: Defines the AWS resources
                    - a stack includes 1 or more constructs
          CDK Toolkit (also known as the CDK CLI): The toolkit is used to create and deploy using the CDK
      Synthesize
        - CDK can synthesize the code to a CloudFormation template
        - Use CDK CLI (toolkit) to create CloudFormation template from your code and deploy it

       CDK Process
          Create a New CDK Project
            - run "cdk init" inside an empty directory
          Compile the App
            - use "npm run build" to compile the app
          Synthesize [cloudformation template]
            - use "cdk synth" to create a CloudFormation template
          Deploy the Stack
            - use "cdk deploy" to deploy the stack using CloudFormation

            cdk init ----> npm run build ------> cdk synth ------> cdk deploy

9.26 CDK [Cloud Development Kit] Demo


  Lesson Overview
    0. Install CDK to cloudshell

      -> Console -> CloudShell

      #  Install the latest version of the CDK, and update to the latest version of TypeScript. :
      #   note: npm is a JavaScript software registry; TypeScript (*.ts) is a language for application-scale JavaScript
      $ sudo npm install -g aws-cdk
      $ sudo npm update -g typescript

    1. Initialize a Sample App
       cdk init sample-app --language=typescript

       # Initialize a sample app using a language of our choice - e.g. typescript. :
       $ mkdir myapp
       $ cd myapp
       $ cdk init sample-app --language=typescript

       # verify cdk files/directories have been successfully created
       # Check your files have been successfully created, the following files should be present in your directory:
       #   bin  cdk.json  jest.config.js  lib  node_modules  package.json  package-lock.json  README.md  test  tsconfig.json
       # The file that contains the details of the stack and its constructs is lib/cdk-init-stack.ts.
       $ ls
          bin  cdk.json  jest.config.js  lib  node_modules  package.json  package-lock.json  README.md  test  tsconfig.jso

       # created sample SNS/SQS app in "lib/myapp-stack.ts":

          import { Duration, Stack, StackProps } from 'aws-cdk-lib';
          import * as sns from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-sns';
          import * as subs from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-sns-subscriptions';
          import * as sqs from 'aws-cdk-lib/aws-sqs';
          import { Construct } from 'constructs';

          export class MyappStack extends Stack {
            constructor(scope: Construct, id: string, props?: StackProps) {
              super(scope, id, props);

              const queue = new sqs.Queue(this, 'MyappQueue', {
                visibilityTimeout: Duration.seconds(300)
              });

              const topic = new sns.Topic(this, 'MyappTopic');

              topic.addSubscription(new subs.SqsSubscription(queue));
            }

       #  The cdk.json file tells CDK how to execute your app :
       # cdk.json

          {
            "app": "npx ts-node --prefer-ts-exts bin/myapp.ts",
            "watch": {
              "include": [
                "**"
              ],
              "exclude": [
                "README.md",
                "cdk*.json",
                "**/*.d.ts",
                "**/*.js",
                "tsconfig.json",
                "package*.json",
                "yarn.lock",
                "node_modules",
                "test"
              ]
            },
            "context": {
              "@aws-cdk/aws-lambda:recognizeLayerVersion": true,
              "@aws-cdk/core:checkSecretUsage": true,
              "@aws-cdk/core:target-partitions": [
                "aws",
                "aws-cn"
              ],
              "@aws-cdk-containers/ecs-service-extensions:enableDefaultLogDriver": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-ec2:uniqueImdsv2TemplateName": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-ecs:arnFormatIncludesClusterName": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-iam:minimizePolicies": true,
              "@aws-cdk/core:validateSnapshotRemovalPolicy": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-codepipeline:crossAccountKeyAliasStackSafeResourceName": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-s3:createDefaultLoggingPolicy": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-sns-subscriptions:restrictSqsDescryption": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-apigateway:disableCloudWatchRole": true,
              "@aws-cdk/core:enablePartitionLiterals": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-events:eventsTargetQueueSameAccount": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-iam:standardizedServicePrincipals": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-ecs:disableExplicitDeploymentControllerForCircuitBreaker": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-iam:importedRoleStackSafeDefaultPolicyName": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-s3:serverAccessLogsUseBucketPolicy": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-route53-patters:useCertificate": true,
              "@aws-cdk/customresources:installLatestAwsSdkDefault": false,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-rds:databaseProxyUniqueResourceName": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-codedeploy:removeAlarmsFromDeploymentGroup": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-apigateway:authorizerChangeDeploymentLogicalId": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-ec2:launchTemplateDefaultUserData": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-secretsmanager:useAttachedSecretResourcePolicyForSecretTargetAttachments": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-redshift:columnId": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-stepfunctions-tasks:enableEmrServicePolicyV2": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-ec2:restrictDefaultSecurityGroup": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-apigateway:requestValidatorUniqueId": true,
              "@aws-cdk/aws-kms:aliasNameRef": true,
              "@aws-cdk/core:includePrefixInUniqueNameGeneration": true
            }
          }

    2. Compile the App (cdk-init-stack.ts)
      npm run build

      # Manually compile the app:
      $ npm run build
      # verify - the name of our apps directory is the name of the stack)
      $ cdk ls
        MyappStack


    3. Synthesize a CloudFormation Template

      # Synthesise a CloudFormation template:
      $ cdk synth
        . . .
         AssertDescription: CDK bootstrap stack version 6 required. Please run 'cdk bootstrap' with a recent version of the CDK CLI.

      # It stores the template in cdk.out folder
      $ ls cdk.out/
       cdk.out  manifest.json  MyappStack.assets.json  MyappStack.template.json  tree.json


    4. Deploy the stack
       cdk bootstrap (if using CDK for the first time)
       cdk deploy

       # Before we deploy we need to run the bootstrap command. This is to provision required resources for CDK before we
       # can deploy CDK apps into an AWS account and Region - an S3 bucket to store files and IAM roles with permission to
       #  perform deployments
       $ cdk bootstrap

       #  Deploy the stack:
       $ cdk deploy
         . . .
         Do you wish to deploy these changes (y/n)? y
         . . .
         Stack ARN:
         arn:aws:cloudformation:us-east-1:012345678901:stack/MyappStack/63435160-2051-11ee-a7e8-129c98355865

         *  Total time: 106.51s


        -> Console -> Cloudformation -> should see "MyappStack" and "CDKTookit" stacks
                                       -> MyappStack resources include: "MyappQueue", "MyappTopic" and "CDKMetadata



                                CDK calls CF to                         AWS Resources
                               provision AWS Resources                        |-----> SQS
    cdk-init-stack.ts ---> CDK -------------------------> CloudFormation  ----|
                            |                                                 |-----> SNS
                            | CDK transforms it to
                            |  a CloudFormation template
                            V
                      MyappStack.template.json

  Exam Tips
    Cloud Development Kit (CDK) demo:
       CDK Process
          Create a New CDK Project
            - run "cdk init" inside an empty directory
          Compile the App
            - use "npm run build" to compile the app
          Synthesize [cloudformation template]
            - use "cdk synth" to create a CloudFormation template
          Deploy the Stack
            - if first time in a region, use "cdk bootstrap" create "CDKToolkit" stack with required provisioned resources
              (S3 bucket to store files and IAM roles with permission to  perform deployments)
            - use "cdk deploy" to deploy the stack using CloudFormation

            cdk init ----> npm run build ------> cdk synth ------> cdk deploy

9.27 Introducing Amplify

  Frontend, backend, and Full-Stack Developers
    Frontend Developer
      - Develops the code for Everything that an end user sees, experiences, and interacts with.
      - focused on developing the user interface (UI)
    Backend Developer
      - focused on what you cannot see: behind-the-scene, back-office functionality
      - Business logic, databases, and APIs
    Full-Stack Developer
      - a full-stack developer is somebody who is able to build both the frontend and backend
        of a website
  Amplify
    - a set of tools and services designed to make it easy to develop web and mobile applications on AWS
    Frontend Developers
      - Quickly and easily create full-stack web and mobile applications using AWS
    Build Applications
      - Configure a stable and reliable backend to support applications
    Suite of Tools
      - Client Libraries and CLI
      - Console that provides continuous deployment and hosting of applications

  Advantages of Using Amplify
    Frontend Developers
      - can focus on developing the web frontend of the application
    Amplify
      - takes care of creating a reliable backend (e.g. implementing authentication, storage, lambda
        functions, and more).
    Integrated
      - amplify libraries integrate your application with services like Cognito, S3, Lambda,
        and API Gateway

  Amplify Hosting and Amplify Studio
    Amplify Hosting
      - fully managed web hosting service
      - web applications and static website hosting
      - CI/CD functionality
      - integrates with your code repository (e.g. Git, Bitbucket, and CodeCommit)
    Amplify Studio
      - Simple visual interface, separate from AWS Management Console
      - Create your frontend UI
      - Configure and maintain your application backend
      - Add features like authentication, integration with data services, and serverless functions

  Exam Tips

     Introducing Amplify
       Frontend Developers
         - create full-stack web and mobile applications on AWS
       Amplify Libraries
         - integrate with Cognito, S3, Lambda, and API Gateways to create a reliable backend
       Amplify Services
         Amplify Hosting
           - Static web hosting that integrates with your code repository for CI/CD functionality
         Amplify Studio
           - Simple visual tool
           - Used to configure the frontend and backend of your application

9.28 Amplify Demo

  1 Create a CodeCommit Repository
    Name it "my-repo", and upload "index.html" file

      -> Console -> CodeCommit -> Create Repository -> Repository Name: my-repo -> Create

      ->  CodeCommit -> Repositories -> select "my-repo" -> Create file ->
         content:
          <html><body><h1>Hello Cloud Gurus - This web application was deployed using AWS Amplify!</h1></body></html>
         file name: index.html, Author: Pat, email: <email>, Commit Message: adding 'index.html'
          -> Commit Changes


  2 Create a New App in Amplify
   - select "Host web app", and provide the name of our repository

     -> Console -> Amplify -> Get Started -> select "Amplify Hosting "get started" -> From your exist code: "CodeCommit"
         -> Continue -> Recently Update repositories: "my-repo", branch: main -> Next ->
         App Name: my-repo; select "Allow AWS Amplify to automatically deploy all the files hosted in your project root directory";
         IAM service role: "Create and use a new service role" -> Next -> Save and Deploy

  3 Test Our App
    - After the application is deployed, we can test uring the provided URL

     -> Console -> Amplify -> my-repo -> General <left tab> -> Production branch URL -> to website

  4 Update our Code (index.html)
    - update the code in the repository, and check that Amplify successfully redeploys the application

      ->  CodeCommit -> Repositories -> select "my-repo" -> my-repo/index.html -> Edit -> change message ->
         Author: Pat, email: <email>, Commit Message: updatd 'index.html'
          -> Commit Changes

     -> Console -> Amplify -> App: "my-repo" -> should show it is updatinga (provision -> build -> deploy)

     -> refresh website

Note:
  Learn how to get the most out of Amplify Hosting:
     Add a custom domain with a free SSL certificate
       - You can connect a custom domain purchased through domain registrars (for example, Amazon Route 53,
         GoDaddy, and Google Domains) to your app.
      Set up a test version of your site by connecting a feature branch
        - Amplify Hosting leverages Git branches to create new deployments every time a developer connects a new
          branch in their repository.
      Password-protect your site
        - Working on unreleased features? Password protect feature branches that are not ready to be accessed publicly.
      Enable pull request previews
        - Previews offer a way to view changes from pull requests before they are merged to the target branch.
      Create a synthetic canary to proactively monitor your app
        - You can use CloudWatch Synthetics for 24/7 monitoring of broken links, heartbeat checks, and testing
          different user flows in your app.

  Exam Tips

    Amplify Demo
      Hosting
        - Amplify Hosting is used for hosting web applications
      Repository
        - you can upload your code to a repository like GitHub, Bitbucket, or CodeCommit
      Continuous Deployment
        - amplify will deploy your code directly from the repository every time a code change is detected
