 Chapter 11 Monitoring

---------------------------------------------------
11.1 Introduction to CloudWatch

  CloudWatch
    - a monitoring service to monitor the health and performance of your AWS resoruces, as
      well as the application that your run on AWS, and in your own datacenter

  What can CloudWatch monitor
    Compute
      - EC2 instances
      - Auto Scaling Groups
      - Elastic Load Balancers
      - Route53 health checks
      - lambda
    Storate & Content Delivery
      - EBS Volumes
      - Storage Gateway
      - CloudFront
    Databases & Analytics
      - DynamoDB tables
      - ElasticCache Nodes
      - RDS Instances
      - Redshift
      - Elastic Map Reduce (EMR) - AWS Big Data service
    Other
      - SNS topics
      - SQS queues
      - API Gateway
      - Estimated AWS [monthly] charges

  CloudWatch Agent - to define your own metrics

  CloudWatch Logs - allows your to monitor operating systems and application logs

  CloudWatch and EC2
    By default
     - all EC2 instances send key health and performance metrics to CloudWatch
     - Default EC2 host-level metrics consist of: CPU, network, disk, and status checks
     - metrics are stored indefinitely
     - you can retrieve data from any EC2 or Elastic Load Balancer instance even after it has
       been terminated
    Operating System-Level Metrics
      By Default
        - EC2 does not send operating system-level metrics to CloudWatch
      CloudWatch Agent
        - by installing the CloudWatch agent on your EC2 instances, you can collect operating
          system metrics and send them to CloudWatch
      Operating System Metrics
        - Memory usage, processes running on your instance, amount of free disk space, CPU idle
          time, etc.
        - these metrics can only be seen inside your instance, so you need a cloudWatch agent installed
          to for cloudWatch to access them

  How Frequently Does EC2 send Metrics to CloudWatch?
    Metric Frequency
      Default monitoring - 5 min:
      - by default, EC2 sends metric data to cloudWatch in 5-minute intervals
      Detailed monitoring - 1 min
      - for an additional charge, you can enable detailed monitoring that sends metrics
        at 1-minute intervals
      Custom monitoring - down to 1 sec
        - for custom metrics, the default is 1-minute intervals, and you can configure high resolution
          metrics that are sent at 1-second intervals [additional charges as the interval decreases]

  CloudWatch Logs
    - monitoring system and application logs
    Monitor Logs Files
      - monitor and troubleshoot your applications using existing system and application files
      - if error or warning messages are in your log files, these can be sent to cloudwatch
    Customize for Your Application
      - monitor your logs in near-real time for specific phrases, values, or patterns that you define
      - requires the CloudWatch Agent [to be installed]
    Use Case
      - Track the number of errors that occur in your application log and send yourself notification
        whenever the rate of errors exceeds a threshold you specify
    Metric Filters
      - You can search and filter the log data coming into CloudWatch Logs by creating one or more metric filters.
      - Metric filters define the terms and patterns to look for in log data as it is sent to CloudWatch Logs
      - CloudWatch Logs uses these metric filters to turn log data into numerical CloudWatch metrics
        that you can graph or set an alarm on

  CloudWatch Alarms
    - you can create an alarm to monitor any CloudWatch metric in your account
    Alarms
      - this can include EC2 CPU utilization, Elastic Load Balancer latency, or even the charges
        on your AWS bill
    Threshold
      - you can set appropriate thresholds to trigger the alarms and actions to be taken if an
        alarm state is reached
    Use Case
      - you can set an alarm that sends you a notification or executes an Auto Scaling policy if
        CPU utilization exceeds 90% on your EC2 instance for more than 5 minutes


  Exam Tips

    CloudWatch
      - cloudwatch is all about monitoring the performance and health of your systems
      CloudWatch EC2
        - default EC2 host-level metrics: CPU, network, disk, and status check
        - use [install] the CloudWatch agent for operating system-level metrics like memory usage,
          processes, and CPU idle time
      CloudWatch API Gateway
        https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/developerguide/api-gateway-metrics-and-dimensions.html
        Metrics include:
         IntegrationLatency: measures the responsiveness of the backend.
         Latency: measures the overall responsiveness of your API calls.
         IntegrationLatency: The time [millisecond] between when API Gateway relays a request to the backend and
                             when it receives a response from the backend.
         Latency: The time between when API Gateway receives a request from a client and when it returns a response to the client.
                  The latency includes the integration latency and other API Gateway overhead.
         Count: The total number API requests in a given period.
         4XXErrors: The number of client-side errors captured in a given period
         5XXErrors: The number of server-side errors captured in a given period
         CacheHitCount: The number of requests served from the API cache in a given period.
         CacheMissCount: The number of requests served from the backend in a given period, when API caching is enabled.
      CloudWatch Logs
        - monitor and store your logs to help you better understand your systems and applications
        Metric Filters
          - You can search and filter the log data coming into CloudWatch Logs by creating one or more metric filters.
          - Metric filters define the terms and patterns to look for in log data as it is sent to CloudWatch Logs
          - CloudWatch Logs uses these metric filters to turn log data into numerical CloudWatch metrics
            that you can graph or set an alarm on
      CloudWatch Alarms
        - you can create an alarm to monitor any CloudWatch metric in your account, generate an
          alert or take some action [e.g. send you a notification or executing an Auto Scale policy]

11.2 CloudWatch Demo

  1 Launch an EC2 Instance
    - attach an IAM Role with permissions to send CloudWatch Agent metrics to CloudWatch
    - use the cloudWatchAgentServerPolicy managed policy

       console -> IAM -> Roles -> Create Role -> Trusted Entity Type: AWS Service, Use Case: EC2
          -> Next -> Permission Policies: cloudWatchAgentServerPolicy
          -> Next -> Role Name: EC2CWRole -> Create role

      -> ec2 -> launch instance -> name: My EC2 instance, AL2023, t3.micro, no key pair,
         Network: vpc: default, subnet: us-east-1a, Auto-assign public IP: enable;
         Create SG: default, ssh anywhere ;
         Advanced details: IAM Instance Profile: EC2CWRole:
         Advanced details: User Data:
         #!/bin/bash
         dnf update -y
         -> Launch Instance

   Note: DNF or Dandified YUM is the next-generation version of the Yellowdog Updater, Modified (yum), a package
         manager for .rpm-based Linux distributions. DNF was introduced in Fedora 18 in 2013,[


  2 Install the CloudWatch Agent on EC2 instance
    - Configure the agent to send operating system metrics and logs to cloudwatch

  -> instance Connect window
    # Log in to the instance and install rsyslog, which will generate a readable text file of the operating system
    #  messages in /var/log/messages.

    sudo su
    dnf install rsyslog
    systemctl start rsyslog
    systemctl enable rsyslog

    # Install the CW Agent:
    dnf install amazon-cloudwatch-agent -y

    # Configure the CW agent:
    /opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/bin/amazon-cloudwatch-agent-config-wizard
      responses:
        default: linux
        default: EC2
        default; turn on StatsD daemon (collects metrics on your instance)
        default: port 8125 [ for StatsD deamon to listen to)
        default: 10s [interval for statsD daemon to collect metrics]
        2: 10s [ aggregattion interval for metrics collected by StatsD]
        2: no  [monitor metrics from CollectD - NO, CollectD is not installed]
        default: yes [monitor any hosts metrics?]
        default: yes [monitor CPU metrics]
        default: yes [add ec2 dimensions (ImageID, InstanceID,...) into  all metrics]
        default: yes [aggregate CPU dimensions (instanceID)]
        1: 1s [collect metrics resolution]
        2: standard [default metrics config do you want?]
        default: yes [satisfied with above config ...?]
        default: no [do you have an existing cloudwatch log agent?]
        default: yes [do you want to monitor log files?]
        Log file path: /var/log/messages
        default choice [messages]  [Log group name]
        default [{instance_id}] [Log stream name]
        2: 1 day [log group retention]
        2: no [additional log files?]
        1: no [store config in parameter store?]

    # View the CloudWatch agent config file:
    cd /opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/bin
    cat config.json

    # Start the CloudWatch Agent
    /opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/bin/amazon-cloudwatch-agent-ctl -a fetch-config -m ec2 -s -c file:/opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/bin/config.json


    # Generate some activity on our system by installing stress:
    # install "stress" utility:
    dnf install stress -y
    # run stress
    stress --cpu 1

  3 View Metrics in CloudWatch
    - we should be able to see the EC2 default metrics as well as the metrics and logs sent
      by the CloudWatch Agent

   -> Console -> CloudWatch -> Metric <left tab> -> All metrics -> CWAgent ->

   -> Console -> CloudWatch -> Metric <left tab> -> All metrics -> EC2 ->  Per Instance Metrics -> filter with instance_id

   # logs
   -> Console -> CloudWatch -> Logs <left tab> -> Log Groups -> messages -> Log Streams -> select instanceId

  CloudWatch Demo - Summary / Exam Tips
    1 Launch an EC2 Instance
      - attach an IAM Role with permissions to send CloudWatch Agent metrics to CloudWatch
      - use the cloudWatchAgentServerPolicy managed policy

    2 Install the CloudWatch Agent on EC2 instance
      - Configure the agent to send operating system metrics and logs to cloudwatch

    3 View Metrics in CloudWatch
      - we should be able to see the EC2 default metrics as well as the metrics and logs sent
        by the CloudWatch Agent

11.4 Understanding CloudWatch Concepts

  CloudWatch Metrics
    - a metric is a variable to monitor
    - metrics are uniquely defined by a name, a namespace, and zero or more dimensions
    Sequence
      - time-ordered sequence of values or data points, which are published to CloudWatch
    Timestamp
      - each data point in a metric has a timestamp, and optionally, a unit of measurement
    Example
      - the CPU Usage of an EC2 instance unit of measurement is a Percentage

  CloudWatch Namespaces
    - a namespace is a container for CloudWatch metric
    - e.g., EC2 uses AWS/EC2 namespace
    - Create your own namespace to publish custom metric data
    Publishing Custom Metrics
      Specify
        - you must specify a namespace for each data point or value that you publish to CloudWatch
      Create
        - Specify the namespace name when you create a metric
      Isolated
        - metrics in different namespaces are isolated from each other
      Not Aggregated
        - metrics from different applications are not aggregated into the same statistics
        - this means you can keep all the metrics for a specific appliction in its own namespace
    Viewing Custom Metrics
      - custom metrics appear under custom namespaces, while the default AWS metrics appear under AWS Namespaces
      - e.g. CloudWatch Agents metrics are kept under Custom Namespaces in the CWAgent namespace

  CloudWatch Dimensions
    - a dimension is like a filter
    Name/Value Pair
      - can be used to filter CloudWatch data
    InstanceId
      - for example, you can use the instanceId dimension to search for metrics related to a specific EC2 instance
    Aggregate
      - CloudWatch can aggregate data across dimensions for some services (e.g., EC2)
      - if you search for CPU metrics in the AWS/EC2 namespace without specifying a dimension, CloudWatch can
        provide aggregated data (e.g. across all instances) [ All Metrics -> EC2 -> ECS > Across all Instances ]
        - if we specify a dimension of our InstanceID, CloudWatch will show you the CPU Metrics related to that instanceId

  CloudWatch Dashboards
    - a custom view, like a home page that you create in CloudWatch
    - a single page for monitoring your resources
    - you can create a CloudWatch dashboard to display important metrics for all your production systems in one place
    - this allows you to check the health of your critical systems and applications from one dashboard
      - simply select the metrics that are important to you, and add them to your dashboard


  Exam Tips

    CloudWatch Concepts
      CloudWatch Metric
        - a metric is a variable to monitor (e.g. CPU usage of an EC2 instance)
      CloudWatch Namespace
        - a namespace is a container for cloudwatch metrics (e.g. EC2 uses AWS/EC2 namespace)
        - can create your own namespaces to contain your custom metrics
      CloudWatch Dimension
        - a dimension is like a filter (e.g. use the instanceId dimension to search for metrics related to a
          specific EC2 instance
      CloudWatch Dashboard
        - a dashboard is like a custom home page that you create in CloudWatch (e.g. display important metrics for all
          your production systems in one place)

---------------------------------------------------
11.5 CloudWatch vs CloudTrail

  CloudTrail
    - records all user activity in your AWS account including console and CLI activity
    - cloudtrail records events related to creation, modification, or deletion of resources (such as
      IAM Users, S3 Buckets, and EC2 instances)
    - by default, you can view the last 90 days of account activity

  When to use CloudWatch and CloudTrail
    CloudWatch
      - about performance and metrics in your AWS environment
      - includes CloudWatch Logs which allow you to monitor your logs for error messages, etc
      - includes CloudWatch Alarms which can send alerts and notifications based on thresholds that you define
    CloudTrail
      - records all the API calls for your AWS account
      - delivers log files containing API calls to an S3 bucket
      - can be integrated with CloudWatch Logs (e.g. you can use CloudWatch Logs to monitor for failed
        login in your CloudTrail logs, and then send notifications)
    Understand the difference between CloudWatch and CloudTrail
      - Do you need to monitor the performance of AWS resources? Use CloudWatch
      - Do you need an audit log of user activity in your AWS account? Use CloudTrail

  Exam Tips

    CloudWatch vs CloudTrail
      CloudWatch
        - monitors performance and metrics
        - CloudWatch Logs for monitoring your application and system logs for error messages, etc
        - CloudWatch Alarms for sending alerts and notifications based on thresholds that you define
        - Do you need to monitor the performance of AWS resources? Use CloudWatch
      CloudTrail
        - records all the API calls for your AWS account
        - has an API activity history related to creation, deletion, and modification of AWS Resources
        - delivers log files containing API calls to an S3 bucket
        - can be integrated with CloudWatch Logs (e.g. you can use CloudWatch Logs to monitor for failed
          login in your CloudTrail logs, and then send notifications)
        - Do you need an audit log of user activity in your AWS account? Use CloudTrail

11.6 Using CloudWatch for Resource Monitoring

About this lab

This lab provides practical experience creating and configuring multiple custom AWS CloudWatch dashboards
and widgets. The primary focus will be on the following features within CloudWatch: CloudWatch dashboards,
dashboard widgets, and CloudWatch metrics. CloudWatch dashboards built with custom widgets allow you to
visually monitor resources and proactively take action, if needed. Overall, the dashboards give you a
central, visual view of how your monitored resources are behaving at specific points in time.

Learning objectives
 - Create a CloudWatch Dashboard for the DMZ Layer
 - Create a CloudWatch Dashboard for the Application Layer
 - Test the Widgets

11.7 Using CloudWatch Actions

  CloudWatch API Reference Actions
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/APIReference/API_Operations.html

  CloudWatch API Reference Actions - PutMetricData
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/APIReference/API_PutMetricData.html


  CloudWatch Actions
    The CloudWatch API
      - supports a long list of different actions
      - these actions allow you to publish, monitor, and alert on a variety of metrics
      - these are particularly powerful when creating custom metrics for monitoring and
        alerting for your application
    CloudWatch API Reference Actions
      - review the API reference for the latest supported CloudWatch Actions
    Most Commoonly used:
      - 2 commonly used actions are "PutMetricData" and "PutMetricAlarm"


  PutMetricData
    - Publishes metric data points to CloudWatch, e.g.:
      aws cloudwatch put-metric-data --metric-name PageViewCount --namespace MyService --value 25 \
          --timestamp 2022-01-10T12:00:00.000Z
    - you can define the name of the metric, the namespace to publish to, the value to publish, and a
      timestamp for the data point
    - After running this command, this metric will become visible in CloudWatch

  PutMetricAlarm
    -  creates an alarm associated with a metric to alert you if a threshold has been reached, e.g.:
       aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm --alarm-name PageViewMon --alarm-description "PageView Monitor" \
           --metric-name PageViewCount --namespace MyService --statistic Average --period 300 --threshold 50 \
           --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold --evaluation-periods 1
     - For example, you want to alerted if the average PageViewCount exceeds a threshold of 50 within
       a time period of 5 minutes (300 seconds) in one evaluation period

  Example / Demo
    - An Application running on EC2 is regularly throwing errors
    - the application sends metrics to CloudWatch every time an error occurs
    - We can create an alarm that will be triggered if more than 1 CriticalError data point is received
      By CloudWatach within a set time period (e.g. 5 min)

             Critical Error
            -------------->
       EC2                      CloudWatch Alarm
             Critical Error
             -------------->


     Create "CriticalError" metric in "MyService" namespace and reporting an error at 15 timestamps over several minutes:
        aws cloudwatch put-metric-data --metric-name CriticalError --namespace MyService --value 1 --timestamp 2023-07-17T22:05:00.000Z

    -> Console -> CloudWatch -> Metrics -> All Metrics -> select "CriticalError"

    Create a "CriticalErrorMon" alarm reporting when Critical Error reports more than 1 error in a 5 min period:
       aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm --alarm-name CriticalErrorMon --alarm-description "CriticalError Monitor" --metric-name CriticalError \
         --namespace MyService --statistic SampleCount --period 300 --threshold 1 --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold
         --evaluation-periods 1

    -> Console -> CloudWatch -> Alarms -> All Alarms -> select "CriticalErrorMon"


  Exam Tips

    CloudWatch Actions
       CloudWatch Actions
         - Allow you to publish, monitor, and alert on a variety of metrics
       PutMetricData (aws cloudwatch put-metric-data)
         - publish metric data points to CloudWatch
         - e.g.
             EC2 app   ---> CriticalError  ----> CloudWatch
       PutMetricAlarm (aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm)
         - create an alarm associated with a metric to alert you if a threshold has been reached
         - e.g.
             EC2 app   ---> CriticalError  ----> CloudWatch Alarm

   - allows you to perform interactive query and analysis

   -> console -> Lambda -> Create Function -> "Auther from scatch", Function Name: cw-logs-insights-fcn, Runtime: Node.js -> create function
      -> cw-logs-insights-fcn -> Code -> change 'Hello from Lambda' to 'Hello Cloud Gurus!' -> Deploy

       -> cw-logs-insights-fcn -> code source -> click on "Test" (5 or more times)
          -> Response should be:

             "statusCode": 200,
             "body": "\"Hello Cloud Gurus!\""
           }

       - explore how we can use CloudWatch Logs Insights to analyze our log data

        -> Console -> CloudWatch -> logs <left tab> -> Logs Insight -> log groups -> /aws/lambda/cw-logs-insight-fcn
        -> Run Query (using provided sample query which retuns last 20 events)
           -> returns a list

        Sample Query:
         fields @timestamp, @message, @logStream, @log
         | sort @timestamp desc
         | limit 20

     4 Review Sample Queries
       - review some of the sample queries that are available

        -> Console -> CloudWatch -> logs <left tab> -> Logs Insight -> log groups -> /aws/lambda/cw-logs-insight-fcn
         -> Query <right tab> -> Lambda -> Find the most expensive requests -> Apply -> Run Query
           -> returns a list


        Most Expensive Requests Query:
        filter @type = "REPORT"
        | fields @requestId, @billedDuration
        | sort by @billedDuration desc


         -> Query <right tab> -> VPC Flow logs -> IP addresses using UDP transfer protocol -> Apply -> Run Query
         filter protocol=17
         | stats count(*) by srcAddr

         -> Query <right tab> -> Common Queries -> 25 most recently added log events -> Apply -> Run Query

        -> Console -> CloudWatch -> logs <left tab> -> Logs Insight -> log groups -> /aws/lambda/cw-logs-insight-fcn
          -> Visualization

  Exam Tips

    CloudWatch Log Insights
      Query and Analyze
        - we can use CloudWatch logs Insights to interactive query and analyze data store in CloudWatch Logs
      Visualization
        - Query the logs directly and also generate visualizations, e.g. bar graph, line graph, pie chart, etc.
      Examples
        Provides many example queries including:
          - display the 25 most recently added log events
          - search VPC Flow Logs to find out which IP addresses are using a specific protocol
          - Find the most expensive lambda requests

11.10 What is EventBridge

  EventBridge
    - EventBridge is all about event-driven architecture
    - an event is a change in state

                  Changes in State                                        Action on an
   AWS Services    Events                                                 AWS Service
   -----------   --------------                                          ---------------
   AWS Config  --- events ----->                 --- Rule --->   Target  --- action --->   shutting down an config non-complaint ec2 instance
   CloudTrail  --- events ----->    EventBridge  --- Rule --->   Target  --- action --->   trigger Lambda fcn
   CloudWatch  --- events ----->                 --- Rule --->   Target  --- action --->   send an SNS notification

  EventBridge can Scheduled Events
    - EventBridge rules can also run on a schedule
    - e.g. once an hour or day, or using a cron expression, we can set a rule to run at the same time
      on a specified day, week, or month
      - example: reboot an ec2 instance at a scheduled time, once a month

  Sounds Like CloudWatch Events
    - EventBridge is the preferred way to manage your events
    - CloudWatch Events and EventBridge are the same underlying service and API, but EventBridge
      provides more features
    - changes you make in either CloudWatch Events or EventBridge will appear in each console

  EventBridge Use Cases
    example 1: Your company requires that all EC2 instances have encrypted disks
       - AWS Config detects that somebody has created a new instance without encrypting the attached EBS volumes
       - an Event is generated and sent to EventBridge, which triggers a rule that invokes an action to send you
         an email using SNS


       AWS Config -> detects non-compliant --- event --> EventBridge  -- action --> SNS ---> email
                     EC2 instance

    example 2: CloudWatch detects that one of your EC2 instances is showing CPU utilization of 99%
       - An event is generated and sent to EventBridge, which triggers a rule that invokes an action to
         send you an email using SNS


       CloudWatch -> detects EC2  CPU      --- event --> EventBridge  -- action --> SNS ---> email
                     Utilization > 99.9%
       EC2
       CPU utilization > 99.9%

  Exam Tips

    EventBridge
      - Allows you to easily configure event-driven systems and define tasks that can be run on
        a pre-defined schedule
      - The same underlying technology as CloudWatch Events
      Events
        - Events are state changes generated by AWS services like AWS Config, CloudWatch, CloudTrail, etc.
      Rules
        - rules match events and route them to the correct target
      Targets
        - target can be services like Lambda, SNS, or EC2, and they respond to the event by taking
          some action

---------------------------------------------------
11.11 Using EventBridge - Demo

  Lesson Objectives

  1. Launch and EC2 instance


    -> ec2 -> launch instance -> name: EventBridge demo, AL2, t3.micro, no key pair,
       Network: vpc: default, subnet: us-east-1a, Auto-assign public IP: enable;
       Create SG: default, ssh anywhere ;
       -> Launch Instance

  2. Create and SNS Topic
    - Subscribe to the topic using our email address, and confirm the subscription

   -> SNS -> Topics <left tab> -> Create Topic: Topic Type: Standard, Topic Name: mytopic -> Create Topic
      -> Subscriptions <left tab> -> Create subscription -> ARN: <select mytopic>,
         Protocol: Email, Endpoint: <email address> -> Create Subscription

     -> email -> confirm subscription

  3. Create an EventBridge Rule to notify us of any EC2 state changes using SNS

    -> Console -> EventBridge -> Rules <left tab> -> Create rule -> Event bus: default -> Create Rule ->
      Name: ec2-state-change-rule, Rule Type: Rule with event pattern -> Next ->
      Event Source: AWS events or EventBridge partner events, Creation method: Use pattern form;
      Event Pattern: Event Source: AWS services, AWS Service: EC2, Event Type: EC2 Instance State-change Notification,
      State: Any State, Instance: Any Instance -> Next ->
      Target 1: Target Types: AWS Service, Select Target: SNS, Topic: mytopic -> Next -> Next -> Create Rule

 Note: EventBridge Rule Types:
    Rule with an event pattern:
      - A rule that runs when an event matches the defined event pattern. EventBridge sends the event to the specified target.
    Schedule
      - A rule that runs on a schedule
      - e.g run lambda rule every 24 hours (note:Lambda does not include a runtime scheduler)

  4. Stop the EC2 instance
    - Stop our EC2 instance and check if we have received an email notification

    -> Console -> EC2 -> "EventBridge Demo" instance -> Instance state -> Stop


    -> Console -> CloudWatch -> Events <left tab> -> Rules -> shows the "ec2-state-change-rule" created in eventbridge

  Exam Tips:

    Using Amazon EventBridge
      Events
        - Events are state changes generated by AWS services like AWS Config, CloudWatch, CloudTrail, etc.
        - EventBridge receives events related to state changes in AWS, e.g. EC2 instance changes state or
          CloudWatch alarm state changes
      Rules and Actions
        - rules match events and route them to the correct target
        - you can use EventBridge to create rules that take action based on the events it receives, e.g. send
          an SNS notification
      CloudWatch Events
        - EventBridge and CloudWatch Events use the same underlying technology

   Note: EventBridge Rule Types:
      Rule with an event pattern:
        - A rule that runs when an event matches the defined event pattern. EventBridge sends the event to the specified target.
      Schedule
        - A rule that runs on a schedule
        - e.g run lambda rule every 24 hours (note:Lambda does not include a runtime scheduler)

11.12 Recognizing Common HTTP Error Codes

  Client-Side Errors
    4XX - Client-Side Errors
       400 - Access Denied Exceptions:
           - You don't have the required access
       403 - Missing Authentication Token:
           - The request did not contain a valid X.509 certificate (when using SSL or TLS) or AWS access key ID
       404 - Malformed Query String, Object doesn't exist, or File not found
           - The query string contains a syntax error
           - To help you remember, 404 is always your fault! You tried to visit a page that doesn't exist

       Reference:
         API Reference - Common Errors:
         https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awssupport/latest/APIReference/CommonErrors.html

  Server-Side Errors
    5XX - Server-side Errors
        - the request was valid, but the server was not able to fulfill it
        - API Gateway 5xx HTTP response code: Server errors are indicated by a 5xx HTTP response code and need
          to be resolved by Amazon. You can resubmit or retry the request until it succeeds.
    500 - Internal Failure
        - the request failed due to an unknown error exception, or failure
    503 - Service Unavailable
        - the request failed due to temporary failure of the server
        - can be caused by high traffic to the website and the server is not responding, or internal failure
        - The Load Balancer generates the HTTP 503: Service unavailable error when the target groups for the
          load balancer have no registered targets.

  Encoded authorization failure message
     Encoded authorization failure message: 6h34GtpmGjJJUm946eDVBfzWQJk6z5GePbbGDs9Z2T8xZj9EZtEduSnTbmrR7pMqpJrVYJCew2m8YBZQf4HRWEtrpncANrZMsnzk.
     - Use decode-authorization-message to decode additional information about the authorization status of a request
       from an encoded message returned in response to an AWS request. e.g.:
          aws sts decode-authorization-message --encoded-message <value>

  A Memory Aid
    To help your remember:
      - "5" looks a bit like "S" is for "server-side-errors!

  Exam Tips

    Recognizing Common HTTP Error Codes
      4XX Errors - Client-Side Errors
        - client side - remember 404
        - Check that:
          - the requested object exists
          - you have access to it
          - your request is correctly formed
      5XX Errors - Server-Side Errors
        - Server side: remember "5" looks like "S" in Server-side
        - 5XX issues are generally caused by the server not responding
        - can be due to high traffic to your website and servers being overwhelmed or unavailable
        - capacity/performance issue
        - internal failure

11.13 Recognizing Common [DynamoDB] SDK Exceptions

  Error handling with DynamoDB
  https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Programming.Errors.html

  What is an SDK Exception
    - A response to an error that has occurred when processing an SDK or API request

  Common Exceptions
    BatchGetItem
      BatchGetItem Use:
        - returns details of one or more items from an DynamoDB table
      Single Operation
        - Limited to up to 16 MB of data and up to 100 items
      A partial Results
        - If DynamoDB cannot return all the items, it returns a partial result, along with an exception
      Causes of a Partial Result
        - you requested more than 100 items
        - you requested more than 16 MB of data
        - the request exceeded the provisioned throughput of the dynamoDB table
      Example BatchGetItem Errors:
        ValidationException
          - too many items requested for the BatchGetItem call - request fewer items
        UnprocessedKeys
          - if at least one item is successfully processed, you will receive a message that some items
            were not successfully processed. - Reduce the request size
          - returns a partial result if the response size limit is exceeded (not a read capacity issue)
             other options (e.g. use exponential backoff):
              - Update the application to use the AWS software development kit (AWS SDK) for JavaScript to make the API requests.
                 - AWS SDKs automatically implement retries using exponential backoff. If you delay the batch operation
                   using exponential backoff, the individual requests in the batch are much more likely to succeed.
              - Retry the operation using an exponential backoff algorithm.
                  - If you delay the batch operation using exponential backoff, the individual requests in the batch
                    are much more likely to succeed
        ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
          - no items were successfully processed, due to exceeding the provisioned throughput of the
            table.
                solutions:
                - Add capacity (e.g. add DAX)
                - If the error only appears occasionally, the first thing to do is to ensure that the application
                  is using exponential backoff to improve flow control.

    BatchWriteItem
      BatchWriteItem Use:
        - Puts or deletes one or more items in one or more DynamoDB tables
      Single Operation
        - Limited to up to 16 MB of data and up to 25 put or delete operations
      Failed Operations
        - If any puts or deletes fail, DynamoDB returns a list of UnprocessedItems
      Example BatchWriteItem Errors:
        UnprocessedItems
          - Some of the operations failed. You will need to retry the unprocessed items
        ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
          - None of the put or delete operations were processed, due to exceeding the provisioned
            throughput of the table
          hot partition
            - It's not always possible to distribute read and write activity evenly.
            - When data access is imbalanced, a "hot" partition can receive a higher volume of read and write traffic
              compared to other partitions.
            - To better accommodate uneven access patterns, DynamoDB adaptive capacity enables your application to
              continue reading and writing to hot partitions without being throttled, provided that traffic does
              not exceed your table’s total provisioned capacity or the partition maximum capacity.
            - if traffic exceed your table’s total provisioned capacity or the partition maximum capacity,
              You receive the error 'ProvisionedThroughputExceededException'.
        Need More Write Capacity?
          - increase the write capacity units on the table

       Reference:
         API Reference - BatchWriteItme
         https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/APIReference/API_BatchWriteItem.html

  Exam Tips

    Recognizinng Common [DynamoDB] SDK Exceptions
      Common Error Messages:
        UnprocessedKeys
          - some items were not successfully processed during a BatchGetItemYou receive the error 'ProvisionedThroughputExceededException'. operation
            Solution: Reduce the request size / Request fewer items
          - returns a partial result if the response size limit is exceeded (not a read capacity issue)
             other options (e.g. use exponential backoff):
              - Update the application to use the AWS software development kit (AWS SDK) for JavaScript to make the API requests.
                 - AWS SDKs automatically implement retries using exponential backoff. If you delay the batch operation
                   using exponential backoff, the individual requests in the batch are much more likely to succeed.
              - Retry the operation using an exponential backoff algorithm.
                  - If you delay the batch operation using exponential backoff, the individual requests in the batch
                    are much more likely to succeed
        ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
          - None of the put or delete operations were processed, due to exceeding the provisioned
            throughput of the table
          - solution for occasional occurence:
              - If the error only appears occasionally, the first thing to do is to ensure that the application
                is using exponential backoff to improve flow control.
          - solution for BatchGetItem: Use DAX to cache reads or increase the read capacity units on the table
          - solution for BatchWriteItem:  increase the write capacity units on the table
        UnprocessedItems
          - Some items were not successfully processed during a BatchWriteItem operation
          - solution: you will need to retry the unprocessed items
        Solutions:
          - Reduce the sized of the request
          - Request fewer items
          - Use DAX to cache reads
          - Increase provisioned [read or write] capacity

11.14 Monitoring Summary


  CloudWatch
    - cloudwatch is all about monitoring the performance and health of your systems
    CloudWatch EC2
      - default EC2 host-level metrics: CPU, network, disk, and status check
      - use [install] the CloudWatch agent for operating system-level metrics like memory usage,
        processes, and CPU idle time
    CloudWatch API Gateway
      https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/developerguide/api-gateway-metrics-and-dimensions.html
      Metrics include:
       IntegrationLatency: The time [millisecond] between when API Gateway relays a request to the backend and
                           when it receives a response from the backend.
       Latency: The time between when API Gateway receives a request from a client and when it returns a response to the client.
                The latency includes the integration latency and other API Gateway overhead.
       Count: The total number API requests in a given period.
       4XXErrors: The number of client-side errors captured in a given period
       5XXErrors: The number of server-side errors captured in a given period
       CacheHitCount: The number of requests served from the API cache in a given period.
       CacheMissCount: The number of requests served from the backend in a given period, when API caching is enabled.
    CloudWatch Logs
      - monitor and store your logs to help you better understand your systems and applications
      Metric Filters
        - You can search and filter the log data coming into CloudWatch Logs by creating one or more metric filters.
        - Metric filters define the terms and patterns to look for in log data as it is sent to CloudWatch Logs
        - CloudWatch Logs uses these metric filters to turn log data into numerical CloudWatch metrics
          that you can graph or set an alarm on
    CloudWatch Alarms
      - you can create an alarm to monitor any CloudWatch metric in your account, generate an
        alert or take some action [e.g. send you a notification or executing an Auto Scale policy]
      - You can create a CloudWatch alarm that will trigger if a CloudWatch metric (e.g. CPU Utilization) exceeds
        the specified threshold (e.g. for 5 minutes), and then Configure CloudWatch to send an SNS notification
        if the alarm is triggered.
      - Cloudwatch metric alarm states: OK, ALARM, or INSUFFICIENT_DATA

  CloudWatch Dashboards
    - customize your view
    A Custom View
      - create a dashboard which monitors the metrics that meaningfull to your, e.g. all your production systems
    Multi-Region
      - Display metrics from any Region
      - when adding widgets, change the region and add the widget you want
    Remember to Save
      - CloudWatch doesn't automatically save your dashboard, so remember to save it once you have configured it

  Receiving Notifications with CloudWatch Demo
    Alarm
      - you can use an alarm to notify your when a threshold is hit
    Use Case
      - you can create an alarm for any metric in CloudWatch (e.g. CPUUtilization)
    Email Notifications
      - cloudwatch can also send you email notification using an SNS Topic

  CloudWatch vs CloudTrail
    CloudWatch
      - monitors performance and metrics
      - CloudWatch Logs for monitoring your application and system logs for error messages, etc
      - CloudWatch Alarms for sending alerts and notifications based on thresholds that you define
      - Do you need to monitor the performance of AWS resources? Use CloudWatch
    CloudTrail
      - records all the API calls for your AWS account
      - has an API activity history related to creation, deletion, and modification of AWS Resources
      - delivers log files containing API calls to an S3 bucket
      - can be integrated with CloudWatch Logs (e.g. you can use CloudWatch Logs to monitor for failed
        login in your CloudTrail logs, and then send notifications)
      - Do you need an audit log of user activity in your AWS account? Use CloudTrail

  CloudWatch Concepts
    CloudWatch Metric
      - a metric is a variable to monitor (e.g. CPU usage of an EC2 instance)
      Standard resolution: metric data having a one-minute granularity
      High resolution: metric data at a granularity of one second
                       you can read and retrieve it with a period of 1, 5, 10, 30 sec, or any multiple of 60 seconds.
    CloudWatch Namespace
      - a namespace is a container for cloudwatch metrics (e.g. EC2 uses AWS/EC2 namespace)
      - can create your own namespaces to contain your custom metrics
    CloudWatch Dimension
      - a dimension is like a filter (e.g. use the instanceId dimension to search for metrics related to a
        specific EC2 instance
    CloudWatch Dashboard
      - a dashboard is like a custom home page that you create in CloudWatch (e.g. display important metrics for all
        your production systems in one place)

  CloudWatch Actions
     CloudWatch Actions
       - Allow you to publish, monitor, and alert on a variety of metrics
     PutMetricData API call (aws cloudwatch put-metric-data)
       - publish metric data points to CloudWatch
       - e.g.
           EC2 app   ---> CriticalError  ----> CloudWatch
     PutMetricAlarm API call (aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm)
       - create an alarm associated with a metric to alert you if a threshold has been reached
       - e.g.
           EC2 app   ---> CriticalError  ----> CloudWatch Alarm

  Recognizing Common HTTP Error Codes
    4XX Errors - Client-Side Errors
      - client side - remember 404
      - Check that:
        - the requested object exits
        - you have access to it
        - your request is correctly formed
      Common 4XX errors:
       400 - Access Denied Exceptions:
           - You don't have the required access
       403 - Missing Authentication Token:
           - The request did not contain a valid X.509 certificate (when using SSL or TLS) or AWS access key ID
       404 - Malformed Query String, Object doesn't exist, or File not found
           - The query string contains a syntax error
           - To help you remember, 404 is always your fault! You tried to visit a page that doesn't exist
       404 File not found:
          - means that the requested file is not on the server. It could mean that you typed the URL incorrectly.
    5XX Errors - Server-Side Errors
      - Server side: remember "5" looks like "S" in Server-side
      - 5XX issues are generally caused by the server not responding to your request
      - can be due to high traffic to your website and servers being overwhelmed or unavailable
      - capacity/performance issue
      - internal failure
      Common 5XX errors:
        500 - Internal Failure
          - the request failed due to an unknown error exception, or failure
          - e.g.: "AmazonS3Exception: Internal Error (Service: Amazon S3; Status Code: 500; Error Code: 500 Internal Error...)
        503 service unavailable:
          - means that something is going wrong with the software running on the server and
            often indicates a performance issue, server is not responding, or internal failure.
        504 Gateway Timeout

  Recognizinng Common [DynamoDB] SDK Exceptions
    Common Error Messages:
      UnprocessedKeys
        - some items were not successfully processed during a BatchGetItem operation
          Solution: Reduce the request size / Request fewer items
        - returns a partial result if the response size limit is exceeded (not a read capacity issue)
           other options (e.g. use exponential backoff):
            - Update the application to use the AWS software development kit (AWS SDK) for JavaScript to make the API requests.
               - AWS SDKs automatically implement retries using exponential backoff. If you delay the batch operation
                 using exponential backoff, the individual requests in the batch are much more likely to succeed.
            - Retry the operation using an exponential backoff algorithm.
                - If you delay the batch operation using exponential backoff, the individual requests in the batch
                  are much more likely to succeed
      ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
        - None of the put or delete operations were processed, due to exceeding the provisioned
          throughput of the table
        - solution for occasional occurence:
            - If the error only appears occasionally, the first thing to do is to ensure that the application
              is using exponential backoff to improve flow control.
        - solution for BatchGetItem: Use DAX to cache reads or increase the read capacity units on the table
        - solution for BatchWriteItem:  increase the write capacity units on the table
      UnprocessedItems
        - Some items were not successfully processed during a BatchWriteItem operation
        - solution: you will need to retry the unprocessed items
      Solutions:
        - Reduce the sized of the request
        - Request fewer items
        - Use DAX to cache reads
        - Increase provisioned [read or write] capacity

  CloudWatch Log Insights
    Query and Analyze
      - we can use CloudWatch logs Insights to interactive query and analyze data stored in CloudWatch Logs
    Visualization
      - Query the logs directly and also generate visualizations, e.g. bar graph, line graph, pie chart, etc.
    Examples
      Provides many example queries including:
        - display the 25 most recently added log events
        - search VPC Flow Logs to find out which IP addresses are using a specific protocol
        - Find the most expensive lambda requests

  EventBridge
    - EventBridge is a serverless event bus that can be used to trigger Lambda functions, SQS notifications, and
      SNS messages based on events or schedules
    - Allows you to easily configure event-driven systems and define tasks that can be run on
      a pre-defined schedule
    - The same underlying technology as CloudWatch Events
    Events
      - Events are state changes generated by AWS services like AWS Config, CloudWatch, CloudTrail, etc.
    Rules
      - rules match events and route them to the correct target
    Targets
      - target can be services like Lambda, SNS, or EC2, and they respond to the event by taking
        some action

  Using Amazon EventBridge
    Events
      - Events are state changes generated by AWS services like AWS Config, CloudWatch, CloudTrail, etc.
      - EventBridge receives events related to state changes in AWS, e.g. EC2 instance changes state or
        CloudWatch alarm state changes
    Rules and Actions
      - rules match events and route them to the correct target
      - you can use EventBridge to create rules that take action based on the events it receives, e.g. send
        an SNS notification
    CloudWatch Events
      - EventBridge and CloudWatch Events use the same underlying technology

   Note: EventBridge Rule Types:
      Rule with an event pattern:
        - A rule that runs when an event matches the defined event pattern. EventBridge sends the event to the specified target.
      Schedule
        - A rule that runs on a schedule
        - e.g run lambda rule every 24 hours (note:Lambda does not include a runtime scheduler)

  Using Amazon EventBridge between AWS accounts
    - You can configure EventBridge to send and receive events between event buses in AWS accounts.
    - When you configure EventBridge to send or receive events between accounts, you can specify which AWS
      accounts can send events to or receive events from the event bus in your account
    steps:
      receiver account: edit the permissions on an event bus to allow specified AWS accounts, an organization,
                        to send events to the receiver account.
      sender account: set up one or more rules that have the receiver account's event bus as the target
      receiver account: set up one or more rules that match events that come from the sender account.

Which of the following CloudWatch actions enables you to publish metric data points to CloudWatch?

 - PutMetricAlarm
 - PublishMetricData

 - PutDashboard

PutMetricData publishes metric data points to CloudWatch.

Question 10

Which of the following CloudWatch actions enables you to create an alarm associated with a CloudWatch metric so that you
can be alerted if a threshold has been reached?

Choices:
 - CreateMetricAlarm
 - PutMetricAlarm
 - SetAlarmState
 - PutMetricData        <- Correct Answer

Info:
PutMetricAlarm creates an alarm associated with a metric to alert you if a threshold has been reached.

13.5 AWS Certified Developer - Associate (DVA-C02) - Practice Exam 4

others info:

 What is AWS AppSync
    - AppSync simplifies application development by giving you the ability to create a flexible API to securely access, manipulate,
      and combine data from one or more data sources. AWS AppSync is a managed service that uses GraphQL to help applications get
      the exact data that they need. You can use AWS AppSync to build scalable applications that require real-time updates on a range
      of data sources, including Amazon DynamoDB.
    - Robust, scalable GraphQL interface for application developers
    - Combines data from multiple sources (e.g. DynamoDb and Lambda)
    - enable data interaction for developers via GraphQL
    - GraphQL: data language that enables apps to fetch data from servers
    - Seamless integration with React, React Native, iOS, and Android, & JavaScript

  What is GraphQL
    - GraphQL is a data language to enable client apps to fetch, change and subscribe to
      data from servers.
    - In a GraphQL query, the client specifies how the data is to be structured when it is
      returned by the server. This makes it possible for the client to query only for the data
      it needs, in the format that it needs it in.

  IAM Access Advisor:
    - Console -> IAM -> [User, Group, Role] -> Access Advisor <tab>
    - uses data analysis to help you set permission guardrails confidently by providing service last accessed information for
      your accounts, organizational units (OUs), and your organization managed by AWS Organizations
    - You can review service last accessed information for your AWS organization in the IAM console in your organization’s

  IAM Access Analyzer:
    - AWS IAM Access Analyzer helps you identify the resources in your organization and accounts, such as Amazon S3 buckets
      or IAM roles, that are shared with an external entity. This lets you identify unintended access to your resources and
      data, which is a security risk
    steps:
      Create an analyzer
        - You can set the scope for the analyzer to an organization or an AWS account. This is your zone of trust.
        - The analyzer scans all of the supported resources within your zone of trust
      Review Active Findings
        - When Access Analyzer finds a policy that allows access to a resource from outside of your zone of trust,
         it generates an active finding. Findings include details about the access so that you can take action
      Take Action
        -  If the access is not intended, you can resolve the finding by modifying the policy to remove access to the resource.

   Resource-based Policies
     - Resource-based policies are JSON policy documents that you attach to a resource such as an Amazon S3 bucket.
     - These policies grant the specified principal permission to perform specific actions on that resource and define
       under what conditions this applies.

   Trust Policy
     - Trust policies define which principal entities (accounts, users, roles, and federated users) can assume the role.
     - An IAM role is both an identity and a resource that supports resource-based policies. For this reason, you must
       attach both a trust policy and an identity-based policy to an IAM role.
     - The IAM service supports only one type of resource-based policy called a role trust policy, which is attached
       to an IAM role.

   Elastic Load Balancers (ELB)
     Cross-zone Load Balancing
       - The nodes for your load balancer distribute requests from clients to registered targets.
       - When cross-zone load balancing is enabled, each load balancer node distributes traffic across the registered targets
         in all enabled Availability Zones.
       - When cross-zone load balancing is disabled, each load balancer node distributes traffic only across the registered
         targets in its Availability Zone (e.g. if EC2 target are in 2 AZ, the 50% traffic goes to each AZ even if different
         number of EC2s in each AZ).
       - With Application Load Balancers, cross-zone load balancing is always enabled.
     ELB Access Logs
       - Elastic Load Balancing provides access logs that capture detailed information about requests sent to your load balancer.
       - Each log contains information such as the time the request was received, the client's IP address, latencies, request paths,
         and server responses. You can use these access logs to analyze traffic patterns and troubleshoot issues.
       - Access logging is an optional feature of Elastic Load Balancing that is disabled by default.
     ALB Request Tracing
       - You can use request tracing to track HTTP requests.
       - The load balancer adds a header with a trace identifier to each request it receives

   Auto Scaling
     [SQS] backlog per instance metric with target tracking scaling
       Backlog per instance: ApproximateNumberOfMessages queue attribute [number of messages availabe in SQS queue] divided instances
          in service
       Acceptable backlog per instance:  take the acceptable latency value and divide it by the average time that an EC2 instance
          takes to process a message
       Target Tracking Policy: configures your Auto Scaling group to scale based on the custom metric and a set target value.
          CloudWatch alarms invoke the scaling policy

    Auto Scaling Group
      - EC2 autoscaling attempts to distribute instances evenly between AZs that are enabled in your Auto Scaling group
      - An Auto Scaling group can contain EC2 instances in one or more Availability Zones within the same Region
      - Auto Scaling groups cannot span across multiple Regions
      - EC2 Auto Scaling works with Application Load Balancers and Network Load Balancers including their health check feature
      Auto Scaling Group Health check
        -  By default, the health check configuration of your Auto Scaling group is set as an EC2 type that performs
           a status check of EC2 instances.
        - To automate the replacement of unhealthy EC2 instances, you must change the health check type of your instance's
          Auto Scaling group from EC2 to ELB by using a configuration file.

    IAM Policy Variables
      - You can define dynamic values inside policies by using policy variables that set placeholders in a policy.
      - When the policy is evaluated, the policy variables are replaced with values that come from the conditional
        context keys passed in the request.

     Macie
       - uses AI to analyze data in S3 and help identify PII, PHI, and financial data
       - great for HIPAA and GDPR compliance as well as preventing identify theft
       - Macie alerts can be sent to EventBridge and integrated with your event management systems
       Macie can generate the following types of sensitive data findings for an S3 object
         SensitiveData:S3Object/Credentials
           - The object contains credentials data, such as AWS secret access keys or private keys.
         SensitiveData:S3Object/CustomIdentifier
           - The object contains text that matches the detection criteria of one or more custom data identifiers.
         SensitiveData:S3Object/Financial
           - The object contains financial information, such as bank account numbers or credit card numbers.
         SensitiveData:S3Object/Multiple
           - The object contains more than one category of sensitive data any combination of credentials data,
         SensitiveData:S3Object/Personal
           - The object contains personally identifiable information (such as mailing addresses or driver's license number)

     S3 Select
       - S3 Select enables applications to retrieve only a subset of data from an object by using simple SQL expressions
       - get data by rows or columns using simple SQL expressions
       - save money on data transfers and increase speeds
       S3 Select limitations
         - you can only perform a query on one object at a time
         - only supports the "SELECT" clause in SQL (no joins, no groupings, and no other sophisticated SQL operations

  What is EC2 Metadata
    - data about your ec2 instance
    - can include private IP address, public IP address, hostname, security groups, etc
  Retrieving Metadata
  Using User Data to Save Metadata
    - using the curl command, we can query metadata about our ec2 instance
    - e.g.:
       # get local IP address
       curl http://169.265.169.254/latest/meta-data/local-ipv4
       # list meta-data directory contents
       curl http://169.265.169.254/latest/meta-data/
    - using User Data to save Metadata
       - in below simple bootstrap (user data) script, the curl command is used to save
         IPv4 Address metadata
       #!/bin/bash
       curl http://169.265.169.254/latest/meta-data/local-ipv4 > myIP.txt

  EFS Storage Classes
     EFS Standard
     EFS Standard-Infrequent Access (Standard-IA),
     EFS One Zone, and
     EFS One Zone-Infrequent Access (EFS One Zone-IA).

  ECR Repository pulling Docker images steps (CLI login and pull images)
    - The get-login command retrieves a token that is valid for a specified registry for 12 hours, and then it prints
      a docker login command with that authorization token. You can execute the printed command to log in to your registry
      with Docker, or just run it automatically using the $() command wrapper.
    - After you have logged in to an Amazon ECR registry with this command, you can use the Docker CLI to push and pull images
     from that registry until the token expires. The docker pull command is used to pull an image from the ECR registry.

       $(aws ecr get-login --no-include-email)
       docker pull 1234567890.dkr.ecr.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com/demo:latest
